Light does the same, which lets us measure how stars move.|Christian Doppler||e|1842|Christian Doppler, an Austrian physicist, proposed the effect in 1842. In 1845 it was tested with musicians playing a note on a moving train. For light, objects moving away look redder and those approaching look bluer. Astronomers used this redshift to discover that galaxies are flying apart, the first evidence that the universe is expanding.
ideasci|Bioluminescence|Fireflies, glowing fungi and countless creatures of the deep sea make their own light, through a chemical reaction. In the dark ocean, light is a language: for finding mates, luring prey and hiding.|Marine biology||e|since ancient times|Most of the ocean's animals at depth can produce light. They use an enzyme, luciferase, acting on a molecule called luciferin. Some squid make a glow on their undersides that matches the light from above, so they cast no shadow. The jellyfish protein GFP, discovered in the 1960s, now lets biologists light up cells and genes, and won a Nobel Prize in 2008.
ideasoc|The Wisdom of Crowds|The average of many independent guesses is often closer to the truth than almost any single expert, as long as people think for themselves and their errors cancel out.|Francis Galton; James Surowiecki||e|1907; 2004|At a country fair in 1906, Galton found that the median of 787 guesses of an ox's weight was within one per cent of the true figure. James Surowiecki's book of 2004 explored when crowds are wise: diversity of views, independence, and a good way to combine them. When people copy each other instead, crowds become herds, and bubbles and panics follow.
ideasoc|Social Prescribing|Doctors can prescribe not only pills but gardening clubs, choirs, walking groups, volunteering and art classes, because loneliness and lack of purpose make people ill.|National Health Service, UK||e|2010s onwards|Britain's NHS employs "link workers" who connect patients with community activities. The idea rests on decades of research showing that social connection affects health as much as smoking or exercise. Results are encouraging but still being studied. The idea is old, too: healers have long sent people to the mountains, the sea or good company.
ideasoc|Groupthink|When a close group values agreement above all, it stops testing its own ideas. Doubts go unspoken, warnings are dismissed, and clever people make disastrous decisions together.|Irving Janis||e|1972|The psychologist Irving Janis studied foreign-policy fiascos, such as the Bay of Pigs invasion of 1961, in his book Victims of Groupthink (1972). He found the same signs: an illusion of invulnerability, pressure on dissenters, self-appointed "mind-guards". His remedies include naming a devil's advocate, inviting outside critics and having leaders speak last.
ideaeast|Mottainai|A Japanese word of regret at waste: it is a pity to throw away something that still has use or worth, whether food, things, time or talents.|Japanese tradition (Buddhist roots)||e|traditional|Mottainai comes from Buddhist ideas of the interconnected worth of all things. Children are told "mottainai!" if they leave rice in their bowl. The Kenyan Nobel laureate Wangari Maathai adopted it in 2005 as a global slogan for reducing, reusing, recycling and respecting resources. It is gratitude turned into care: valuing what we already have.
ideaeast|Gaman|A Japanese word for enduring the seemingly unbearable with patience and dignity, without complaint, often for the sake of others.|Japanese tradition (Zen roots)||e|traditional|Gaman has Zen Buddhist roots. It was widely noticed after the 2011 earthquake and tsunami, when survivors queued calmly and shared what little they had. It also names the beautiful objects made by Japanese Americans in the internment camps of the Second World War from scraps. Gaman can become harmful if it means hiding pain; at its best it is quiet, shared resilience.
ideaeast|Nunchi|A Korean word for the art of sensing what others feel and think, reading the mood of a room, and responding with tact.|Korean tradition||e|traditional|Nunchi literally means "eye-measure". Korean children are taught it early: noticing who is tired, who is uncomfortable, when to speak and when to stay quiet. It is close to emotional intelligence, but with a stronger sense of harmony in the group. Someone with quick nunchi makes everyone feel at ease; someone without it is said to have "no nunchi".
ideaeast|Jeong|A Korean word for the deep, sticky affection and loyalty that grows between people over time, through shared life, even between rivals or with places and things.|Korean tradition||e|traditional|Jeong is not chosen like love; it accumulates, through years of meals shared, hardships endured and small kindnesses. It can bind a neighbourhood, an old couple, or a person to their childhood home, and it can make leaving hard. Koreans say jeong is shown more in deeds, like the last rice cake saved for you, than in words.
ideaeast|The Mandate of Heaven|In ancient China, a ruler's right to govern came from Heaven, but only while he ruled justly. Famine, floods and revolt were signs the mandate had been lost, and rebellion against a bad ruler could be right.|Zhou dynasty||e|about 1046 BC|The Zhou kings used the idea, Tianming, to justify overthrowing the Shang dynasty around 1046 BC. Mencius later argued that Heaven sees through the eyes of the people. Unlike divine right in Europe, the mandate could be withdrawn. It shaped Chinese politics for three thousand years and explained the rise and fall of dynasties.
ideaeast|Many-Sidedness|The Jain principle of anekāntavāda holds that reality has many sides, and any single view sees only some. Truth is approached by holding several perspectives at once, with humility.|Jain philosophy||e|from about 500 BC|Anekāntavāda, "non-one-sidedness", is central to Jain thought, alongside non-violence. Jains developed syādvāda, a way of stating claims with "in some respect", to avoid dogmatism. The parable of the blind men and the elephant is often used to explain it. Gandhi said the doctrine taught him to judge others by their own standpoint.
ideaeast|Nirvana|Nirvana means blowing out, as a flame is blown out: the end of the fires of greed, hatred and delusion. It is described not as nothingness but as peace, freedom and the end of suffering.|The Buddha||e|about 5th century BC|In early Buddhism, nirvana (Pali nibbāna) is the goal of the path. The Buddha mostly described it by what it is not: unborn, unmade, free from craving. It can be glimpsed in this life, not only after death. Later schools debated whether it is the same as the world seen rightly. Many teachers describe it simply as the deepest kind of peace.
ideaart|Vanitas|Still-life paintings of skulls, guttering candles, wilting flowers and hourglasses among books and fine things, reminding the viewer that all of it passes.|Dutch and Flemish painters||e|17th century|The name comes from Ecclesiastes: vanitas vanitatum, "vanity of vanities". Painters like Pieter Claesz and Harmen Steenwyck arranged precious objects beside symbols of time and mortality. The paintings were often bought by the very merchants whose riches they showed. Their message is not gloom but proportion: hold lightly what you cannot keep.
ideaart|The Season Word|Almost every traditional haiku contains a kigo, a word that places it in a season: frogs, cherry blossom, autumn moon, snow. One word brings a whole world of feeling with it.|Japanese haiku tradition||e|17th century onwards|Poets keep almanacs (saijiki) listing thousands of season words with their meanings and example poems. "Autumn wind" carries loneliness; "spring rain" softness. Bashō's frog jumping into the old pond is a spring word. The kigo connects a tiny poem to the turning year and to every poem written with the same word before it.
ideaart|Ragas of the Hours|In Indian classical music, each raga, a set of notes and phrases with its own mood, belongs to a time of day or a season: there are morning ragas, evening ragas and ragas of the monsoon.|Indian classical tradition||e|traditional|A raga such as Bhairav belongs to dawn, Yaman to early evening, Malkauns to the deep night, and the Malhar ragas to the rains; legend says the singer Tansen could make rain fall by singing one. Musicians still largely follow the time theory. It reflects a belief that music should be in harmony with the rhythm of nature and the listener's state.
ideaart|Ubi Sunt|"Where are they now?" Poets of many centuries have asked where the people of the past have gone, to remember them and to feel how brief life is.|Medieval Latin poets; François Villon||e|Middle Ages|The phrase comes from the Latin "Ubi sunt qui ante nos fuerunt?": where are those who were before us? Villon asked "Where are the snows of yesteryear?" in his Ballad of the Ladies of Times Past (1461). The Old English Wanderer asks where the horse and the rider have gone. The question is sad, but it is also tender: it keeps the dead in mind.
ideaart|Duende|Lorca described duende as a dark, trembling power that rises from the soles of the feet in great art: in flamenco song, in bullfighting, in poetry. It cannot be learned, only struggled with, and it knows that life is fragile.|Federico García Lorca||e|1933|Lorca gave his lecture "Play and Theory of the Duende" in Buenos Aires in 1933. He contrasted the muse, who inspires from outside, and the angel, who gives light, with the duende, who must be wrestled from within. Nick Cave and many others have written about it since. Duende is what makes a performance, rather than merely correct, unforgettable.
ideaart|The Music of Silence|In music the rests are as important as the notes: silence gives shape, tension and breath. Many musicians say the art is in the space between sounds.|Musical tradition||e|since ancient times|Western notation has marked rests since the Middle Ages, and Japanese aesthetics speaks of ma, the meaningful interval. Composers such as Beethoven used sudden silences for drama, while Miles Davis was known for the notes he chose not to play. The idea reaches beyond music: in speech, in writing and in conversation, a pause lets meaning land.
ideamath|The Golden Angle|Sunflowers, pine cones and daisies place each new seed about 137.5° around from the last. This golden angle packs seeds most evenly and makes the spirals we see, in Fibonacci numbers.|Phyllotaxis; Douady and Couder||e|19th–20th centuries|Count the spirals in a sunflower head and you usually get neighbouring Fibonacci numbers, such as 34 and 55. The golden angle divides a circle in the golden ratio, so no two seeds ever line up exactly, leaving no gaps. In 1992 the physicists Stéphane Douady and Yves Couder reproduced the patterns with drops of magnetised fluid, showing they arise naturally from simple growth rules.
ideamath|Kepler's Snowflake|As a New Year's gift for a friend in 1611, Johannes Kepler wrote a little book asking why snowflakes always have six sides, and opened a question about how nature packs things together.|Johannes Kepler||e|1611|In On the Six-Cornered Snowflake, Kepler looked at honeycombs and pomegranate seeds and guessed that the closest way to pack equal spheres is the way grocers stack oranges. This "Kepler conjecture" was proved only in 1998, by Thomas Hales, with the help of computers. We now know the six sides of snow come from the way water molecules bond, as Kepler suspected without being able to see it.
ideamath|Turing Patterns|Alan Turing showed how two chemicals, one spreading fast and one slowly, reacting together, can produce spots and stripes from an even start: a possible secret behind leopard spots and zebra stripes.|Alan Turing||e|1952|In his paper The Chemical Basis of Morphogenesis, written two years before his death, Turing proposed that patterns in living things can form spontaneously from reaction and diffusion. For decades biologists were doubtful, but such patterns have since been found in the spacing of fingers, the stripes of fish, the ridges of the mouth's palate and hair follicles. Pure mathematics turned out to describe how a body takes shape.
ideamath|The Collatz Conjecture|Pick any whole number. If it is even, halve it; if odd, triple it and add one. Repeat. Every number ever tried ends up at 1, yet no one has proved that every number must.|Lothar Collatz||e|1937|Lothar Collatz proposed the problem in 1937. It has been checked by computer for numbers into the hundreds of quintillions. Some paths soar very high before falling: 27 takes 111 steps and climbs past 9,000. The great mathematician Paul Erdős said mathematics is not yet ready for such problems. It shows how the simplest rules can hide deep mysteries.
ideamath|Banach–Tarski|In pure mathematics, a solid ball can be cut into a handful of pieces and reassembled into two balls, each the same size as the original. The pieces are so strange that they have no well-defined volume.|Stefan Banach and Alfred Tarski||e|1924|The Banach–Tarski paradox (1924) depends on the axiom of choice, a principle that lets mathematicians pick elements from infinitely many sets at once. The pieces are infinitely scattered clouds of points that cannot be made in the physical world. The paradox shows that our intuition about volume rests on assumptions, and that infinity can behave in shocking ways.
mythworld|Rusalka|In Slavic folklore, rusalki are water spirits, young women with long loose hair who live in rivers and lakes and come out on summer nights to dance in the meadows and swing in the birches.|Slavic folklore||e|Eastern Europe|Rusalki were said to be the spirits of girls who had died young, often near water; during Rusalka Week in early summer, villagers left them gifts and avoided swimming. They could be dangerous, luring people into the water, or kind, bringing rain for the crops. Dvořák's opera Rusalka (1901), with its "Song to the Moon", turned the legend into a story of longing between two worlds.
mythworld|Saulė, the Sun Mother|In Baltic myth, the Sun is a goddess, Saulė, who drives a copper chariot across the sky by day and sails home in a golden boat across the sea at night.|Baltic myth||e|Lithuania and Latvia|Saulė was married to Mėnulis, the Moon, who fell in love with the Morning Star, Aušrinė; in anger Perkūnas, the thunder god, cut the Moon in two, which is why it waxes and wanes. Latvian and Lithuanian folk songs (dainas) still sing of Saulė. At midsummer, Rasos or Jāņi, people stay up all night with bonfires and flowers to greet her.
mythworld|Mami Wata|Mami Wata, "Mother Water", is a water spirit honoured across West and Central Africa and its diaspora: beautiful, wealthy, often shown with a snake, bringing healing, good fortune and danger.|West and Central African tradition||e|West and Central Africa and the Americas|Her followers wear white and red, and offer perfume, sweets and mirrors at the water's edge. Her popular image owes something to a 19th-century German print of a snake charmer that travelled widely in Africa. She embodies the sea's gifts and risks, and the riches and dangers of trade across it. She is still a living tradition in Nigeria, Benin, Togo and Ghana.
mythworld|The Seven Sisters|In many Aboriginal Australian songlines, the Pleiades are seven sisters fleeing across the land and up into the sky from a man who pursues them, and their journey maps waterholes and sacred places across the desert.|Aboriginal Australian songlines||e|Australia, many thousands of years|The Seven Sisters story is told across vast distances, by many peoples in different languages, each holding part of the route. In 2017 a major exhibition at the National Museum of Australia brought paintings and songs from these traditions together. Many cultures around the world, from Greece to North America, also see seven sisters in the Pleiades.
mythworld|Dangun|A bear who wished to become human lived in a cave for a hundred days on mugwort and garlic, and became a woman. She married the son of the heavenly king, and their son Dangun founded the first Korean kingdom.|Korean myth||e|traditionally 2333 BC|A tiger tried the same trial but gave up; the bear endured. Dangun Wanggeom founded Gojoseon, the first Korean state, according to the Samguk yusa, written down in the 13th century. Korea celebrates his founding of the nation on National Foundation Day, 3 October. The bear's patience is still held up as a Korean virtue.
mythworld|The Dragon Lord and the Fairy|Lạc Long Quân, a dragon lord of the sea, married Âu Cơ, a fairy of the mountains. She laid a hundred eggs, and from them hatched a hundred children, the ancestors of the Vietnamese people.|Vietnamese myth||e|Vietnam|Being of the sea and the mountains, the two could not live together. Fifty children went with their father to the coast and fifty with their mother to the highlands, and the eldest son became the first of the Hùng kings. Vietnamese people still call each other đồng bào, "from the same womb", recalling that everyone came from one sac of a hundred eggs.
mythworld|Malakas and Maganda|In a Philippine creation story, a bird pecked open a bamboo stalk, and out came the first man and woman: Malakas, "strong", and Maganda, "beautiful".|Tagalog myth||e|the Philippines|In one telling, the sea and the sky quarrelled, and a bird, with nowhere to land, pecked at a bamboo floating on the water. The two first people stepped out of the split stalk side by side, equal from the start. Their story appears in Philippine art and public statues, a reminder of the islands' links to bamboo, birds and the sea.
mythworld|The Simurgh|The Simurgh is a vast, wise bird of Persian myth, nesting in the Tree of Life. In Attar's poem, thirty birds seek her as their king, and find at the end that si murgh, "thirty birds", is themselves.|Persian myth; Attar, The Conference of the Birds||e|12th century|In Ferdowsi's Shahnameh the Simurgh raises the abandoned baby Zal and later helps his son Rustam with her healing feathers. Attar's Sufi poem turns her into a symbol of God: the hoopoe leads the birds through seven valleys, and most give up; the thirty who arrive see themselves reflected in the Simurgh. The search for the divine ends in finding it within.
mythworld|Viracocha|In Inca myth, Viracocha rose from Lake Titicaca, made the sun, moon and stars, and shaped people from stone. Then he walked across the land teaching, and vanished westward over the sea.|Inca myth||e|the Andes|Viracocha first made a race of giants, then destroyed them with a flood when they displeased him, and made humans anew. Dressed as a beggar, he wandered the world teaching people how to live, and was sometimes not recognised. At the coast he walked out across the waves and disappeared. The Spanish chroniclers later recorded the story from Inca accounts.
mythworld|Pachamama|Pachamama, Mother Earth, is honoured across the Andes: the land that feeds, shelters and must be given back to. Before eating or drinking, people still offer her the first drops.|Andean tradition||e|the Andes|Pacha means world, time and space, and mama means mother. In August, when the earth is thought to be hungry before planting, families bury offerings of food, coca leaves and chicha for her. Ecuador's constitution of 2008 gave rights to nature, naming Pachamama. The idea is reciprocity: we take from the earth and must return the gift.
mythancient|The Bennu Bird|The Bennu, a heron of Egyptian myth, flew over the first waters and gave the cry that began time. It was linked to the sun's rising and renewal, and the Greeks knew it as the phoenix.|Egyptian myth||e|ancient Egypt|The Bennu perched on the benben, the first mound of land to rise from the waters of chaos at Heliopolis. Each dawn the sun rose again, like the Bennu. The Greek historian Herodotus heard of a bird that came to Heliopolis every 500 years, and the legend grew into the phoenix that rises from its own ashes, a symbol of renewal for many cultures since.
mythancient|The Eloquent Peasant|An Egyptian peasant, robbed on the road, pleaded for justice before a high official so beautifully that the king had him kept waiting just to hear more speeches. In the end, justice was done.|Middle Kingdom tale||e|Egypt|The Tale of the Eloquent Peasant was written in Egypt around 1850 BC. The peasant, Khunanup, makes nine speeches about ma'at, justice and right order, and the duty of the powerful to protect the poor. It is one of the world's oldest pieces of writing about social justice.
mythancient|The Shipwrecked Sailor|An Egyptian sailor, the only survivor of a wreck, was washed up on an island ruled by a great golden serpent. The serpent comforted him, told him of its own loss, and sent him home laden with gifts.|Middle Kingdom tale||e|Egypt|The Tale of the Shipwrecked Sailor survives on a single papyrus from around 2000–1800 BC. The serpent tells the sailor that all its family were killed by a falling star, yet it endured. "If you are brave, control your heart", it says. It is one of the oldest known stories of consolation after disaster.
mythancient|Ptah Speaks the World|In the Egyptian city of Memphis, priests taught that the god Ptah made the world by thinking it in his heart and speaking it with his tongue: creation by thought and word.|Memphite Theology||e|Egypt|The text survives on the Shabaka Stone, carved around 710 BC from an older original. Ptah was the patron of craftsmen, and his creation is like a maker's plan carried out. The idea of creation through the word has been compared with the opening of Genesis and of John's Gospel.
mythnorse|The Norns|Three women, the Norns, sit at the foot of the world-tree Yggdrasil beside the Well of Urd. They water its roots each day and spin, or carve, the fates of all beings.|The Poetic and Prose Eddas||e|13th century|Their names are often given as Urd (what has been), Verdandi (what is coming into being) and Skuld (what shall be). Even the gods were subject to fate. The Old English word wyrd, fate, is related to Urd, and through it the "weird sisters" of Shakespeare's Macbeth. The Norns keep the tree alive even as fate unfolds.
mythnorse|The Mead of Poetry|The gods made a wise being, Kvasir, from their mingled spit. When he was killed, his blood was brewed into a mead that made anyone who drank it a poet. Odin won it back for the gods by cunning, and shares it with poets still.|Snorri Sturluson, Prose Edda||e|Scandinavia|Snorri tells the story in the Skáldskaparmál of the Prose Edda (about 1220). Odin, disguised, worked a summer for a giant, crept into a mountain as a snake and spent three nights with the giantess Gunnlöð, who guarded the mead. He flew home as an eagle. Some drops fell behind him, and those, Snorri says jokingly, are the share of bad poets.
mythnorse|Thor Goes Fishing|Thor went fishing with the giant Hymir, using an ox's head for bait, and hooked the World Serpent that lies coiled around the earth. As he raised his hammer, the frightened giant cut the line.|Poetic Edda and Prose Edda||e|Scandinavia|The tale appears in the poem Hymiskviða and in Snorri's Prose Edda, and is carved on several Viking-age stones, including the Altuna stone in Sweden. Thor and the serpent Jörmungandr are destined to meet again at Ragnarök. The story shows Thor's great strength, and the limits even of the gods' power.
mythnorse|Thor's Wedding Dress|When a giant stole Thor's hammer and demanded the goddess Freyja as his bride, Thor dressed up as Freyja himself, veil and all, and went to the wedding. When the hammer was laid in the bride's lap, he took it back.|Poetic Edda, Thrymskvida||e|Scandinavia|Thrymskviða is one of the most comic poems of the Poetic Edda. Loki goes along as a bridesmaid, explaining away the bride's appetite (an ox, eight salmon and three casks of mead) and fiery eyes. The poem shows how Norse myth could laugh at its fiercest god.
mythnorse|Sif's Golden Hair|Loki cut off the hair of Thor's wife Sif as a prank. To save himself, he had dwarves forge her new hair of real gold that grew like her own, and in the same contest they made Thor's hammer and Odin's spear.|Snorri Sturluson, Prose Edda||e|Scandinavia|Snorri tells how Loki, threatened by Thor, went to the sons of Ivaldi and the brothers Brokkr and Eitri. Their contest produced the gods' greatest treasures: Sif's hair, the ship Skíðblaðnir, the spear Gungnir, the ring Draupnir and the hammer Mjölnir. Sif's hair is sometimes read as golden fields of grain.
mythgreek|Halcyon Days|When Alcyone's husband Ceyx drowned at sea, she threw herself into the waves after him, and the gods turned them both into kingfishers. Each winter, her father the wind god calms the sea for seven days so she can nest.|Ovid, Metamorphoses; Greek myth||e|ancient Greece|Alcyone saw Ceyx's death in a dream sent by the goddess of sleep, and found his body on the shore. Out of pity the gods changed the faithful pair into birds, the halcyons. The calm days around the winter solstice, when the Greeks believed kingfishers nested on the sea, were called the halcyon days. We still use the phrase for a peaceful, happy time.
mythgreek|Chiron, the Wounded Healer|Chiron, wisest of the centaurs, taught heroes medicine, music and archery. Struck by a poisoned arrow, he could not heal his own wound, and became the image of the healer who knows pain from within.|Greek myth||e|ancient Greece|Chiron raised Asclepius, the god of medicine, and taught Achilles, Jason and Heracles. When Heracles accidentally wounded him with an arrow dipped in the Hydra's blood, the immortal Chiron could neither heal nor die, until he gave his immortality to free Prometheus. Zeus set him among the stars as the constellation Centaurus. Carl Jung used the "wounded healer" to describe therapists whose own wounds help them understand others.
mythgreek|The Choice of Heracles|As a young man, Heracles met two women at a crossroads. Vice offered him an easy life of pleasure; Virtue offered hard work and true glory. He chose the harder road.|Prodicus, as told by Xenophon||e|ancient Greece|The story was told by the sophist Prodicus and preserved in Xenophon's Memorabilia, where Socrates retells it. It became one of the most popular allegories in European art and music, painted by Annibale Carracci and set by Bach as a cantata. It is the ancestor of every story about the road not taken.
mythgreek|Medusa|Medusa's gaze turned people to stone. Perseus defeated her by looking only at her reflection in his polished shield, and from her body sprang the winged horse Pegasus.|Greek myth||e|ancient Greece|In Ovid's telling, Medusa was once a beautiful young woman punished by Athena after Poseidon assaulted her in Athena's temple, which has led many modern readers to see her as a victim rather than a monster. Her head was placed on Athena's shield as a protection. The reflection trick has been read as a lesson: some things can only be faced indirectly.
mythgreek|Tantalus|Tantalus stood in a pool of water beneath a fruit tree, forever thirsty and hungry: whenever he bent to drink the water drained away, and whenever he reached for fruit the branches rose out of reach.|Homer, Odyssey||e|ancient Greece|Tantalus, a king who had dined with the gods, betrayed their trust, and was punished in the underworld. Odysseus sees him there in the Odyssey. His name gives us the word tantalise. His fate has become an image of desire that can never be satisfied, the more we reach, the further things move away.
mythgreek|Ariadne on Naxos|Ariadne helped Theseus escape the labyrinth, but he abandoned her asleep on the island of Naxos. She woke alone, and there the god Dionysus found her, loved her and set her crown among the stars.|Greek myth||e|ancient Greece|The story is told by Catullus, Ovid and many others. Ariadne's crown became the constellation Corona Borealis. Artists from Titian to Richard Strauss have loved the moment of her desolation turned to joy. It is a myth about how the worst abandonment can come before an unexpected new life.
mythgreek|Erysichthon's Hunger|King Erysichthon cut down a sacred oak of Demeter. She cursed him with a hunger that grew the more he ate, until he had sold everything, even his daughter, and still was not full.|Ovid, Metamorphoses||e|ancient Greece|Ovid tells the story in Book 8 of the Metamorphoses. Erysichthon's daughter Mestra had the gift of changing shape and kept escaping the buyers. Modern readers often see in the myth a warning about destroying nature and about consumption that can never be satisfied.
mythgreek|Hermes and the Stolen Cattle|On the day he was born, baby Hermes crept out of his cradle, stole fifty of Apollo's cattle and walked them backwards to hide their tracks. Caught, he made peace by giving Apollo the first lyre, made from a tortoise shell.|Homeric Hymn to Hermes||e|ancient Greece|The Homeric Hymn to Hermes, from around the 6th century BC, is one of the funniest stories in Greek myth. Hermes lies so charmingly before Zeus that the king of the gods laughs. The brothers become friends, and Apollo becomes the god of music. Hermes remained the god of travellers, tricksters, merchants and messengers.
mythgreek|The Judgement of Paris|At a wedding, Strife threw down a golden apple marked "for the fairest". Hera, Athena and Aphrodite each claimed it, and the Trojan prince Paris was asked to choose. He chose Aphrodite, who promised him Helen, and so began the Trojan War.|Greek myth||e|ancient Greece|Hera offered Paris power and Athena offered wisdom and victory, but he chose love. The story explains the origin of the Trojan War, which Homer's Iliad tells in part. The "apple of discord" has become a phrase for anything that sparks a quarrel. Painters, especially Rubens, returned to the scene again and again.
mythworld|The Sun and the Moon Siblings|In an Inuit story, the Sun is a sister, Malina, and the Moon her brother, Anningan. After a quarrel she fled into the sky with a torch, and he chases her for ever, growing thin from hunger as he forgets to eat.|Inuit myth||e|Greenland and the Arctic|Anningan lit his own torch in the chase, but it sputtered out, leaving only embers, which is why the moon is pale. When he grows too thin he disappears for a few days to hunt, and the moon is dark, before returning to the chase. The story explains the moon's phases and the way sun and moon follow each other across the sky.
mythworld|The Sword in the Stone|A sword stood fixed in a stone, inscribed: whoever pulls this sword out is the rightful king. Knights and lords failed, until an unknown boy, Arthur, drew it out without effort.|Arthurian legend||e|Britain|The episode first appears in Robert de Boron's Merlin around 1200 and was retold by Thomas Malory in Le Morte d'Arthur (1485). T. H. White's The Sword in the Stone (1938) and Disney's film made it famous. It is a story about worth that is hidden until the right moment.
mythworld|The Fisher King|The wounded Fisher King guards the Grail, and his land lies waste while he suffers. He can be healed only when a knight asks the right question: "What ails you?"|Arthurian legend||e|Britain and France|The Fisher King appears first in Chrétien de Troyes' unfinished Perceval (about 1190). Perceval fails at first because he was taught not to ask questions. T. S. Eliot built The Waste Land (1922) on the myth. Its lesson is simple and deep: healing can begin with someone caring enough to ask.
mythworld|Gawain and the Green Knight|A giant green knight rode into Arthur's hall and offered a game: strike me now, and in a year I will strike you back. Gawain cut off his head; the knight picked it up and rode away. A year later Gawain kept his word.|Sir Gawain and the Green Knight||e|England|The poem was written in the late 14th century by an unknown poet. On the way, Gawain is tested by his host's wife, and hides a green sash meant to protect him. At the end he is only nicked, for that small dishonesty, and wears the sash ever after as a sign of his fault. It is a story about honesty, fear and forgiving our own failings.
mythworld|Finn and the Giant's Causeway|The Irish giant Finn McCool built a stone road across the sea to fight the Scottish giant Benandonner. Seeing how huge his rival was, Finn fled home, and his wife disguised him as a baby. The Scot, terrified at the size of the "baby", ran back, tearing up the road.|Irish folklore||e|Ireland|The Giant's Causeway in County Antrim is really made of about 40,000 basalt columns formed by cooling lava around 60 million years ago. Similar columns at Fingal's Cave on Staffa in Scotland are said to be the other end. Finn, or Fionn mac Cumhaill, is also the wise hero of many older Irish tales.
mythworld|Kaveh the Blacksmith|When the tyrant Zahhak demanded the brains of young men to feed the serpents growing from his shoulders, the blacksmith Kaveh, who had lost his sons, raised his leather apron on a spear and led the people in revolt.|Ferdowsi, Shahnameh||e|Persia|Ferdowsi's Shahnameh, the Persian Book of Kings, was completed around 1010. Kaveh's apron, jewelled by later kings, became the royal banner of Persia, the Derafsh Kaviani. Kaveh is remembered as the common man who stood up to tyranny, and his revolt is remembered at the autumn festival of Mehregan.
mythworld|Layla and Majnun|Qays loved Layla so completely that he was called Majnun, "possessed". Kept apart from her, he wandered the desert, speaking with animals and writing poems to her on the sand.|Nizami, Layla and Majnun||e|Arabia and Persia|The story began as Arabic tales of a 7th-century poet and was shaped into a great poem by the Persian Nizami in 1188. It has been retold across the Islamic world, from Turkish to Urdu. Sufi poets read it as the soul's love for God. Eric Clapton's "Layla" took its name from the story.
mythworld|Nala and Damayanti|Princess Damayanti chose King Nala for her husband even when four gods took his form to trick her. Later, ruined by a gambling curse, Nala abandoned her in the forest, but her courage and love brought them together again.|Mahabharata||e|India|The story is told in the Mahabharata to the exiled Pandava brothers, to give them hope. Swans carry messages between the lovers. Damayanti recognises the true Nala among the gods because the gods do not sweat, blink or cast shadows. It is one of the most loved love stories in Sanskrit literature.
mythworld|Shakuntala's Ring|King Dushyanta married Shakuntala in a forest hermitage and gave her his ring. Under a curse he forgot her completely, until a fisherman found the ring in a fish, and memory and love came flooding back.|Kalidasa, Shakuntala||e|India|The story appears in the Mahabharata and was made into a great play by the poet Kalidasa around the 5th century. When Sir William Jones translated it into English in 1789, it astonished Europe; Goethe wrote a poem in its praise. It is a story about how love can be lost and found through memory.
mythworld|Mazu, Goddess of the Sea|Lin Moniang, a fisherman's daughter, fell into a trance and in spirit saved her father and brothers from a storm at sea. After her death she was honoured as Mazu, the goddess who protects sailors.|Chinese folk religion||e|China and Taiwan|Lin Moniang is said to have lived on Meizhou Island in Fujian in the 10th century. Her worship spread with Chinese seafarers across Southeast Asia, and there are thousands of Mazu temples, especially in Taiwan. Her birthday festival draws millions of pilgrims. She is a figure of compassion and protection in danger.
mythworld|Kintu and Nambi|Kintu, the first man, wished to marry Nambi, daughter of the sky god Ggulu. He passed impossible tests, and the couple came down to earth with cattle and a banana plant, but Nambi's brother Walumbe followed them.|Buganda myth||e|Uganda|The Baganda people of Uganda tell this story of the first people. Ggulu warned the couple not to turn back for anything; Nambi went back for forgotten millet, and Walumbe, who is death, came with them. Kintu is remembered as the founder of the Buganda kingdom. The tale explains why life on earth is both rich and hard.
mythworld|Why Tortoise's Shell Is Cracked|Tortoise borrowed feathers from the birds to fly to a feast in the sky, called himself "All of You", and ate the feast meant for all of them. The angry birds took back their feathers, and he fell and cracked his shell.|Igbo folktale||e|Nigeria|Chinua Achebe retells this tale in Things Fall Apart (1958), told by Ekwefi to her daughter. Tortoise is a trickster, like Anansi, clever and greedy. His cracked shell, pieced back together, is a reminder that cunning without fairness ends badly.
mythworld|Glooscap|Glooscap, the great culture hero of the Mi'kmaq, shaped the land, named the animals and taught the people to hunt, fish and make tools. When he left, he promised to return if the people needed him.|Mi'kmaq tradition||e|Atlantic Canada|Glooscap, or Kluskap, is central to the stories of the Wabanaki peoples of Nova Scotia, New Brunswick and Maine. He shrank the animals that were too big and dangerous, and made features of the landscape, such as Cape Blomidon. His stories teach respect for the land and for all living things.
mythworld|The Rabbit in the Moon|When the god Quetzalcoatl wandered the earth starving, a rabbit offered itself as food. Moved, the god raised the rabbit up to the moon so that its image would be remembered forever.|Aztec legend||e|Mexico|Many cultures see a rabbit in the moon's markings, from Mexico to China, Japan and India, where a similar story is told of the Buddha in a former life. In the Aztec version, a rabbit was also thrown at the moon to dim it, so it would not shine as bright as the sun. The story honours generosity.
mythworld|Manco Cápac|The sun god Inti sent his children Manco Cápac and Mama Ocllo up from Lake Titicaca with a golden staff. Where it sank into the ground, they founded the city of Cusco and taught the people farming and weaving.|Inca legend||e|Peru|The Inca told this story to explain the origin of their empire and its rulers, who claimed descent from the sun. In another version, four brothers and four sisters emerge from a cave at Pacaritambo. Cusco became the capital of an empire stretching thousands of kilometres along the Andes.
mythworld|Koschei the Deathless|Koschei could not be killed because his death was hidden: in a needle, in an egg, in a duck, in a hare, in an iron chest, buried under an oak on an island. Only a hero who found it could break his power.|Russian folktale||e|Russia|Koschei is the great villain of Russian fairy tales, often a bony old sorcerer who kidnaps princesses. Prince Ivan succeeds with the help of animals he once spared. Stravinsky used him in The Firebird. The nested hiding place has become an image of the secrets that power hides at its heart.
mythworld|Nanabozho and the Flood|After a great flood, the trickster Nanabozho floated on a log with the animals. Each dived for mud to make new land; only the little muskrat, at the cost of great effort, brought some up, and from it Nanabozho made the earth on the back of a turtle.|Anishinaabe tradition||e|Great Lakes, North America|Nanabozho, also called Nanabush or Wenabozho, is a shape-shifting teacher and trickster of the Anishinaabe peoples. The earth-diver story is told in many Indigenous traditions, and explains why North America is called Turtle Island. It honours the smallest creature, whose courage succeeded where the strong failed.
ideahow|The Microwave Oven|Microwaves make the water molecules in food twist back and forth billions of times a second. All that jostling turns into heat, inside the food, not just on its surface.|Percy Spencer||e|1945|Percy Spencer, an engineer working on radar at Raytheon, noticed that a chocolate bar in his pocket had melted while he stood near a magnetron, the tube that makes microwaves. He tried popcorn next, and it popped. Microwave ovens use waves at about 2.45 gigahertz. Water molecules are lopsided, slightly negative at one end and positive at the other, so the rapidly flipping electric field keeps twisting them, and they knock into their neighbours, which is heat. The waves only reach a centimetre or two in, so the middle of a thick dish warms by ordinary conduction, which is why recipes say to stir or let food stand. The metal mesh in the door has holes smaller than the waves, so light gets out but the microwaves don't.
ideahow|The Refrigerator|A fridge doesn't make cold; it moves heat. A liquid boils inside the pipes in the fridge, soaking up heat, and is pumped outside where it gives that heat away through the coils at the back.|Jacob Perkins||e|1834|When a liquid evaporates it takes heat with it, which is why wet skin feels cold in a breeze. A fridge runs this trick in a loop. A refrigerant evaporates in the coils inside, at low pressure, drawing heat out of the food. A compressor squeezes the gas, which makes it hot, and pumps it through the coils at the back, where it cools and condenses back into a liquid, giving its heat to your kitchen. Then it passes through a narrow valve, the pressure drops, and the loop starts again. Jacob Perkins patented a vapour-compression machine in 1834; home fridges spread in the twentieth century. Heat pumps that warm houses use exactly the same cycle, run the other way round.
ideahow|Bread Rising|Yeast are tiny living fungi. In dough they eat sugars and breathe out carbon dioxide, and a stretchy web of gluten traps the bubbles, so the dough swells like a sponge.|Louis Pasteur||e|1857|People made leavened bread for thousands of years before anyone knew why it rose. In the 1850s Louis Pasteur showed that fermentation is done by living yeast cells. When flour meets water, two proteins join into gluten, a network of stretchy strands, and kneading lines them up. Yeast feed on sugars in the flour and give off carbon dioxide and a little alcohol. The gas inflates thousands of tiny pockets in the gluten. In the oven the bubbles expand once more, the yeast die, the alcohol evaporates, and the heat sets the starch and protein into a solid foam: the crumb. Sourdough uses wild yeasts together with bacteria that add the sour taste.
ideahow|The Induction Hob|An induction hob doesn't get hot itself. A coil under the glass makes a rapidly changing magnetic field, which sets electric currents swirling in the bottom of an iron pan, and the pan heats itself.|Michael Faraday||e|1831|The principle is Faraday's electromagnetic induction, discovered in 1831: a changing magnetic field makes electric current flow in a conductor. Under the glass of an induction hob is a copper coil carrying alternating current tens of thousands of times a second. Its field reaches into the base of the pan and drives swirling eddy currents there, and the metal's resistance turns them into heat right where the food is. That is why it heats so fast, wastes less energy and leaves the glass only as warm as the pan makes it. It needs a pan that a magnet sticks to, because iron concentrates the field; plain aluminium or copper pans barely warm.
ideahow|The Pressure Cooker|Water boils at 100 °C at normal air pressure. Seal the pot so steam can't escape and the pressure rises, the boiling point climbs to about 120 °C, and food cooks much faster.|Denis Papin||e|1679|Boiling happens when water's vapour pressure matches the pressure pushing down on it. Raise the pressure and the water has to get hotter before it can boil. The French physicist Denis Papin showed his "steam digester" to the Royal Society in London in 1679, softening bones into jelly, and added a safety valve after early experiments with bursting vessels. A modern pressure cooker raises the pressure inside to roughly twice that of the air, so the water stays liquid at about 120 °C, and tough beans and stews cook in a third of the time. The same physics works in reverse on mountains: at high altitude water boils below 100 °C, so pasta takes longer.
ideahow|Why Onions Make You Cry|Cutting an onion breaks its cells and mixes chemicals that were kept apart. They react to make a sulphur gas that drifts up to your eyes, and your eyes make tears to wash it away.|Shinsuke Imai and colleagues||e|2002|An onion is a defended plant. Its cells store sulphur compounds in one compartment and enzymes in another. When a knife breaks the cells, they meet: one enzyme makes sulphenic acids, and a second, which Japanese researchers found and described in Nature in 2002, turns them into syn-propanethial S-oxide, a volatile irritant. It reaches the eye's surface, stimulates nerves, and tear glands respond. Chilling the onion slows the reactions, a sharp knife crushes fewer cells, and cutting near a fan or under the cooker hood carries the gas away. Cooking destroys the enzyme, which is why cooked onions are sweet and mild.
ideahow|How Soap Cleans|Soap molecules have a head that loves water and a tail that loves oil. The tails bury themselves in grease, the heads face the water, and the grease is wrapped up in tiny balls and rinsed away.|Babylonian soap makers||e|about 2800 BC|Water alone can't shift oil, because oil and water don't mix. Soap is made by boiling fats with an alkali, and each soap molecule ends up with two very different ends: a long oily tail and a charged head. In the wash, the tails dig into grease and dirt while the heads stay in the water. Many molecules together surround a droplet of grease, heads outward, forming a tiny ball called a micelle, which water can now carry away. The same trick breaks apart the fatty outer layer of many viruses, which is why thorough handwashing with soap works so well. Clay tablets from ancient Babylon record soap recipes of fats boiled with ashes.
ideahow|The LED Bulb|An LED is a tiny sandwich of two kinds of crystal. When electricity pushes electrons across the boundary, they drop into lower-energy places and give off their extra energy as light, with very little wasted as heat.|Isamu Akasaki, Hiroshi Amano and Shuji Nakamura||e|1993|The old filament bulb made light by heating a wire white-hot, so most of its energy came out as heat. An LED is a semiconductor diode: on one side the crystal has spare electrons, on the other, gaps where electrons could be. When current flows, electrons fall into the gaps, and each fall releases a photon whose colour is set by the materials. Red and green LEDs existed for decades, but a bright blue one eluded engineers until the early 1990s, when Akasaki, Amano and Nakamura succeeded; they shared the 2014 Nobel Prize in Physics. A blue LED coated with a yellow phosphor gives white light. An LED bulb uses roughly a fifth of the electricity of a filament bulb giving the same light.
ideahow|Electricity at the Socket|The power in your socket comes from generators spinning in power stations. It travels at very high voltage along cables, is stepped down by transformers near you, and arrives as alternating current, changing direction 50 or 60 times a second.|Nikola Tesla and George Westinghouse||e|1880s|Most power stations spin a generator, a magnet turning inside coils of wire, pushed by steam, water or wind; solar panels are the main exception. The spinning gives alternating current, which flows one way and then the other. In the 1880s and 1890s Thomas Edison backed direct current, while Nikola Tesla and George Westinghouse championed alternating current, and AC won mainly because of transformers: they can step voltage up for long-distance lines, where high voltage wastes far less energy, and back down again for homes. Sockets deliver about 230 volts at 50 hertz in Europe and much of the world, and about 120 volts at 60 hertz in North America.
ideahow|The Thermostat|A thermostat is a feedback loop: it measures the temperature, compares it with what you asked for, and switches the heating on when it's too cold and off when it's warm enough.|Cornelis Drebbel||e|about 1620|The Dutch inventor Cornelis Drebbel built one of the first temperature regulators in the early 1600s, for an egg incubator: as the furnace heated, a column of liquid expanded and pushed a damper that cut the air supply. Old room thermostats used a strip of two metals bonded together, which bends as it warms because the metals expand at different rates, tipping a switch. Modern ones use an electronic sensor and a small computer. The idea is far bigger than heating: measure, compare, correct is how cruise control, autopilots and much of your own body, from temperature to blood sugar, keep themselves steady.
ideahow|The Flushing Toilet|Many toilets work by siphon. A rush of water raises the level in the bowl until it spills over the top of the bent pipe behind it, the pipe fills, and the falling water sucks the bowl empty with that familiar gurgle.|Alexander Cumming||e|1775|Sir John Harington, godson of Elizabeth I, built a flushing water closet in the 1590s, but it was the Scottish watchmaker Alexander Cumming's 1775 patent for the S-shaped trap that made toilets bearable: water left standing in the bend blocks smells from the sewer. When you flush, water from the cistern pours into the bowl. In a siphon toilet the level rises over the top of the trap, the downward leg fills completely, and the weight of falling water pulls the rest after it until air gets in and breaks the siphon. Many European toilets are simpler wash-down designs, which push the waste over the bend with the force of the flush instead. Either way, the cistern then refills, shut off by a float.
ideahow|The Smoke Alarm|Most home smoke alarms watch a beam of light inside a dark chamber. Normally it misses the sensor; when smoke drifts in, its particles scatter light onto the sensor, and the alarm sounds.|Duane Pearsall and Stanley Peterson||e|1965|There are two main kinds. In a photoelectric alarm a small LED shines across a dark chamber, angled away from a light sensor; smoke particles scatter some light onto the sensor. Ionisation alarms contain a tiny amount of radioactive americium that makes the air in a chamber conduct a small current; smoke particles soak up the charged particles, the current drops, and the alarm sounds. Photoelectric alarms are quicker with smouldering fires, ionisation ones with fast flaming fires. Duane Pearsall and Stanley Peterson developed an affordable battery-powered home alarm in the mid-1960s, and the cheap home smoke alarm is one of the great life-savers of the twentieth century. Test yours monthly.
ideahow|The Touchscreen|Your finger conducts electricity. Under the glass of a phone is a fine grid of transparent electrodes; when your finger comes close, it changes the electric field at that spot, and the phone works out exactly where you touched.|E. A. Johnson||e|1965|Eric Arthur Johnson, an engineer at the Royal Radar Establishment in England, described a capacitive touchscreen in 1965 for air-traffic control. Today's phones use projected capacitance: two layers of nearly invisible electrodes, made of indium tin oxide, form a grid of tiny capacitors, each storing a little charge. Your body is a conductor, so a fingertip near the glass draws some of the field away and changes the capacitance at the nearest crossing points. A controller scans the grid many times a second and calculates the centre of each touch, which is why it can track several fingers at once. A gloved finger or a plastic stylus doesn't conduct, so the screen doesn't notice it.
ideahow|GPS|About thirty satellites each broadcast the exact time from atomic clocks. Your phone hears four or more, works out how far away each one is from how long its signal took, and finds the one point that fits all the distances.|US Department of Defense||e|1978|The first GPS satellite was launched in 1978, and the system became fully available to civilians in the 1990s. Each satellite continuously broadcasts its position and the time. Radio signals travel at the speed of light, so a delay of one millionth of a second means about 300 metres. Knowing your distance from one satellite puts you somewhere on a sphere around it; three spheres meet at a point, and a fourth satellite lets your phone correct its own cheap clock. Accuracy depends on timing so precise that Einstein matters: the satellites' clocks run faster in weaker gravity and slower because of their speed, a net gain of about 38 microseconds a day, which would add kilometres of error daily if it weren't corrected.
ideahow|Wi-Fi|Wi-Fi is radio. Your router turns data into radio waves by changing their timing and strength in precise patterns, and your phone's antenna catches them and turns the patterns back into ones and zeros.|IEEE 802.11 engineers||e|1997|Wi-Fi uses radio frequencies around 2.4 and 5 gigahertz, close to those of a microwave oven, but at a tiny fraction of the power. Data is encoded by shifting the phase and amplitude of the waves, and modern Wi-Fi spreads each message over many narrow channels at once, which makes it robust against echoes off walls and furniture. A team at Australia's CSIRO, led by John O'Sullivan, patented a key technique for handling those echoes, and the first 802.11 standard was published in 1997. Walls, water and metal absorb or block the signal, which is why it's weaker in the far room, and why a fish tank between you and the router can matter.
ideahow|The Digital Camera|A camera sensor is a grid of millions of tiny light wells. Light knocks electrons free in each one; the camera counts them, and the counts, through red, green and blue filters, become the pixels of your picture.|Steven Sasson||e|1975|Steven Sasson, an engineer at Kodak, built the first digital camera in 1975: it weighed nearly four kilograms and took 23 seconds to record a black-and-white image of 0.01 megapixels onto cassette tape. Today's sensors work on the photoelectric effect that Einstein explained: each photon striking the silicon can free an electron, so the charge collected in each photosite measures the light. The sensor itself is colour-blind, so a mosaic of tiny filters, usually twice as many green as red or blue (Bryce Bayer's 1976 pattern, matching our eyes' sensitivity), sits on top, and software fills in the missing colours for each pixel.
ideahow|Noise-Cancelling Headphones|Sound is a wave of pressure. Microphones on the headphones listen to the noise outside, and the speaker plays the exact opposite wave, so the peaks of one fill the troughs of the other and the noise largely disappears.|Paul Lueg||e|1934|The German physicist Paul Lueg patented the idea of cancelling sound with sound in 1934. It relies on interference: two identical waves, one exactly out of step with the other, add up to almost nothing. Modern headphones do the calculation thousands of times a second. They work best on steady, low sounds like engine drone, whose waves are long and predictable, and less well on sudden or high-pitched sounds, which the ear cushions handle by simply blocking them. Amar Bose began developing noise-cancelling headsets after a noisy flight in 1978; pilots used them long before travellers did.
ideahow|The Lithium-Ion Battery|A phone battery stores energy by moving lithium ions. Charging pushes them into layers of graphite; when you use the phone, they flow back to the other side through the battery, and the electrons that go with them take the long way round, through your phone, powering it.|John Goodenough, Stanley Whittingham and Akira Yoshino||e|1980s|A battery has two electrodes separated by an electrolyte that lets ions through but not electrons. In a lithium-ion cell, lithium ions shuttle between a graphite anode and a metal-oxide cathode. Discharging, the ions cross inside the battery while their electrons travel through the outside circuit, which is the current that runs your phone. Charging forces them back. Stanley Whittingham made the first lithium battery in the 1970s, John Goodenough found a far more powerful cathode in 1980, and Akira Yoshino built the first practical cell in 1985; Sony sold the first commercial ones in 1991, and the three shared the 2019 Nobel Prize in Chemistry. Heat and being kept at full charge age them fastest.
ideahow|How a Plane Flies|A wing is shaped and tilted to push air downwards. Pushing air down pushes the wing up, and the air flowing faster over the curved top has lower pressure than the air beneath, which is the same lift seen another way.|George Cayley and the Wright brothers||e|1799–1903|Sir George Cayley worked out in 1799 that a flying machine needed separate systems for lift, thrust and control, and the Wright brothers made the first powered, controlled flight in 1903. A common school explanation says air over the top must "catch up" with air underneath; it doesn't, and in fact the air over the top arrives sooner. What matters is that the wing turns the airflow downward. By Newton's third law the air pushes the wing up, and the bending of the flow creates lower pressure above the wing and higher pressure below. Tilt the wing more and lift increases, until the airflow can no longer follow the top surface and breaks away: a stall.
ideahow|Bicycle Balance|A moving bicycle stays up by steering. When it starts to lean, the front wheel turns towards the lean, bringing the wheels back under you, just as you would move your hand to balance a broom.|Karl von Drais||e|1817|Karl von Drais rode his two-wheeled "running machine" in Mannheim in 1817, and riders soon found they could balance it while moving. Balance comes from steering into the fall. Riders do much of this without noticing, but a well-designed bicycle also steers itself: when it leans, its geometry turns the front wheel into the lean. For a century people credited the spinning wheels' gyroscopic effect and the trail of the front fork. In 2011 a team led by Jim Papadopoulos and Arend Schwab built a bicycle with neither and showed it could still balance itself, because of how its mass was distributed. Speed helps, which is why a slow bike wobbles.
ideahow|Why Ships Float|Anything in water is pushed up by a force equal to the weight of the water it moves aside. A steel ship is mostly air inside, so its hull pushes aside a lot of water and floats when that water weighs as much as the ship.|Archimedes||e|about 250 BC|Archimedes of Syracuse stated the principle: a body in a fluid is buoyed up by a force equal to the weight of the fluid it displaces. A lump of steel sinks, because it displaces only its own small volume of water, which weighs far less than the steel. Spread the same steel into a hull and it encloses a great volume of air, so it can push aside a much larger amount of water before it is fully under. The ship settles until the water displaced weighs exactly what the ship weighs. The marks painted on a hull, the Plimsoll line, show how low it may safely sit, and it floats higher in salty sea water, which is denser, than in a river.
ideahow|The Car Engine|A petrol engine runs on four strokes: the piston draws in fuel and air, squeezes them, a spark makes them burn and push the piston down, and the piston pushes the exhaust out. Thousands of times a minute, that push turns the wheels.|Nikolaus Otto||e|1876|Nikolaus Otto built the first successful four-stroke engine in 1876, and almost every petrol engine still follows his cycle. Intake: the piston moves down and pulls in air mixed with fuel. Compression: it moves up and squeezes the mixture. Power: a spark plug ignites it, the hot gases expand and drive the piston down. Exhaust: the piston rises and pushes the burnt gases out. Rods turn the pistons' up-and-down motion into the spinning of a crankshaft, and through the gearbox, the wheels. Diesel engines compress air so hard that it gets hot enough to ignite the fuel without a spark. Only around a third of the fuel's energy ends up moving the car; most leaves as heat.
ideahow|The Electric Motor|A wire carrying current in a magnetic field is pushed sideways. Wind the wire into a coil, place it between magnets, and the pushes turn it; keep switching the current at the right moments and it keeps spinning.|Michael Faraday||e|1821|In 1821 Michael Faraday made a wire carrying current circle continuously around a magnet, the first electric motor. The principle: electric current makes its own magnetic field, which pushes against the magnet's. In a simple motor a coil sits between magnet poles; the forces on opposite sides of the coil point in opposite directions and turn it. After half a turn they would push it back, so a switch called a commutator, or in modern motors the electronics, reverses the current at just the right moment. The same machine run backwards is a generator, which is why electric cars can recover energy when they slow down. Motors turn everything from fans and fridges to phones' vibrating buzz.
ideahow|Seatbelts|In a crash the car stops almost instantly, but your body keeps moving. A seatbelt stretches slightly to slow you over a longer time, and spreads the force across your hips and chest, the strongest parts of your body.|Nils Bohlin||e|1959|The force needed to stop a moving body depends on how quickly it stops: halve the stopping time and you double the force. A seatbelt gives you a few extra hundredths of a second and stretches over the pelvis and ribcage, instead of letting you hit the steering wheel or windscreen. A locking mechanism lets the belt move freely in normal driving but catches under a sudden jerk. Nils Bohlin, an engineer at Volvo, designed the three-point belt in 1959, and Volvo made the patent free for any car maker to use. Airbags work on the same principle, cushioning and slowing the head over a longer distance.
ideahow|Vaccines|A vaccine shows your immune system a harmless version or piece of a germ. Your body learns to recognise it and keeps memory cells, so if the real germ arrives, the defence is fast and strong.|Edward Jenner||e|1796|In 1796 the English doctor Edward Jenner tested a country observation, that milkmaids who caught mild cowpox seemed protected from deadly smallpox, by inoculating a boy, James Phipps, with cowpox and later with smallpox, which didn't take. The word vaccine comes from the Latin for cow. Vaccines can contain weakened or killed germs, pieces of their surface, or instructions that let your cells briefly make one harmless protein, as mRNA vaccines do. The immune system responds with antibodies and, crucially, long-lived memory B and T cells. Smallpox was declared eradicated in 1980, the only human disease ended so far. When enough people are protected, a germ struggles to spread, which shields those who can't be vaccinated.
ideahow|Caffeine|While you are awake, a molecule called adenosine builds up in your brain and makes you feel sleepy. Caffeine is shaped enough like it to block its receptors, so the sleepy signal can't land, for a while.|Friedlieb Runge||e|1819|The German chemist Friedlieb Ferdinand Runge first isolated caffeine in 1819, reportedly at the suggestion of Goethe. Adenosine is a by-product of the brain's activity, and as it accumulates over the day it binds to receptors that slow nerve cells down, one reason we feel sleep pressure. Caffeine sits in those receptors without activating them. It doesn't remove the adenosine, which is why tiredness can return in a rush as the caffeine wears off. In adults its half-life is roughly five hours, so half of a mid-afternoon coffee may still be acting at bedtime. Regular drinkers grow extra receptors, which is why they need caffeine just to feel normal and get headaches without it.
ideahow|Sunscreen|Sunscreen stands between your skin and the sun's ultraviolet light. Some ingredients absorb UV and release it as a little heat; mineral ones like zinc oxide also scatter and reflect it. SPF tells you how much of the burning rays get through.|Franz Greiter||e|1946|Ultraviolet light carries enough energy to damage DNA in skin cells. UVB mostly causes sunburn; UVA reaches deeper and ages skin; both raise cancer risk. The Austrian chemist Franz Greiter, said to have been sunburnt climbing a mountain, launched a "glacier cream" in 1946 and later popularised the sun protection factor. SPF measures UVB: SPF 15 blocks about 93 per cent, SPF 30 about 97 per cent and SPF 50 about 98 per cent, so higher numbers bring smaller gains. In practice people apply far less than the tested amount, so using enough and reapplying matter more than the number. "Broad spectrum" means it also protects against UVA.
ideahow|How Aspirin Eases Pain|When tissue is injured, enzymes called COX make prostaglandins, chemical messengers that sensitise nerves and cause swelling. Aspirin and ibuprofen block those enzymes, so fewer pain signals are made.|John Vane||e|1971|Willow bark was used against pain and fever for thousands of years; its active ingredient led to aspirin, which Felix Hoffmann of Bayer made in a stable form in 1897. No one knew how it worked until 1971, when the British pharmacologist John Vane showed it blocks the production of prostaglandins, work for which he shared the 1982 Nobel Prize. Prostaglandins make nerve endings more sensitive, raise fever and drive inflammation, but they also protect the stomach lining, which is why these drugs can irritate the stomach. Paracetamol, also called acetaminophen, eases pain and fever by a mechanism that is still not fully understood and does little for swelling.
ideahow|How Muscles Pull|Muscles can only pull. Inside each fibre, millions of tiny protein arms called myosin grab neighbouring strands of actin, pull, let go and grab again, sliding the strands past each other so the muscle shortens.|Andrew Huxley and Hugh Huxley||e|1954|In 1954 two research teams, one including Andrew Huxley and the other Hugh Huxley (no relation), published side by side in Nature the sliding filament theory. A muscle fibre contains long chains of repeating units in which thick myosin filaments overlap thin actin filaments. When a nerve signal releases calcium, myosin heads bind to actin, swivel in a power stroke, release, and re-cock, each cycle fuelled by a molecule of ATP. Because muscles only pull, they work in pairs, such as the biceps bending the elbow and the triceps straightening it. After death ATP runs out, the heads stay locked on, and the body stiffens: rigor mortis.
ideahow|Goosebumps|When you are cold or moved by music, tiny muscles at the base of each hair tighten and make it stand up, raising a little bump. In our furry ancestors this fluffed up the coat, trapping warmth or making them look bigger.|Charles Darwin||e|1872|Each hair follicle has a small muscle, the arrector pili, controlled by the same nervous system that drives the fight-or-flight response. Cold, fear and strong emotion all trigger it. Darwin wrote about the bristling of hair in The Expression of the Emotions in Man and Animals in 1872, noting how cats and porcupines puff themselves up when threatened. With so little body hair, the effect does almost nothing for us now: a reflex left over from ancestors. Recent research in mice suggests these muscles also help hair follicle stem cells grow new hair, so the system may not be entirely useless. The chills from a beautiful piece of music are the same reflex, set off by emotion.
ideahow|Rainbows|Sunlight entering a raindrop bends, bounces off the back of the drop and bends again on the way out. Each colour bends by a slightly different amount, so white light fans out into its colours, and you see each colour from drops at a particular angle.|René Descartes and Isaac Newton||e|1637|René Descartes traced rays through a glass globe of water and explained the rainbow's shape in 1637: light leaving the drops is concentrated at about 42 degrees from the point directly opposite the sun, so the bow is part of a circle around the shadow of your head. Isaac Newton then showed with prisms that white light is a mixture of colours, each refracted by a different amount, which spreads them into bands, red on the outside, violet inside. Because the angle is fixed, everyone sees their own rainbow from different drops, and it moves as you move. A second, fainter bow with the colours reversed comes from light reflecting twice inside the drops.
ideahow|Why the Sky Is Blue|Sunlight contains every colour. Air molecules scatter short, blue waves far more than long, red ones, so blue light bounces around the whole sky and reaches your eyes from every direction.|Lord Rayleigh||e|1871|Lord Rayleigh showed in 1871 that very small particles, such as air molecules, scatter light in proportion to the inverse fourth power of its wavelength: halve the wavelength and the scattering rises sixteen times. Blue light, with its short waves, is scattered around five times more than red. Violet is scattered even more, but sunlight has less of it, some is absorbed high in the atmosphere, and our eyes are less sensitive to it, so we see blue. At sunrise and sunset, light crosses much more air to reach you; most of the blue is scattered out of the beam along the way, and the remaining light is orange and red. On the Moon, with no air, the daytime sky is black.
ideahow|Tides|The Moon's gravity pulls hardest on the side of the Earth facing it and weakest on the far side. That stretches the oceans into two bulges, one towards the Moon and one away, and as the Earth turns, most coasts pass through both: two high tides a day.|Isaac Newton||e|1687|Isaac Newton explained the tides with his law of gravity in 1687. The key is the difference in pull across the Earth: near-side water is pulled towards the Moon more than the Earth's centre, and the centre more than far-side water, so the oceans bulge on both sides. The Earth rotates beneath the bulges once a day, while the Moon moves on in its orbit, so high tides come about 12 hours 25 minutes apart. The Sun has a similar but smaller effect. When Sun and Moon line up, at new and full moon, their effects add into larger spring tides; at half moon they partly cancel into smaller neap tides. Coastlines and the shape of the seabed make real tides vary enormously.
ideahow|Thunder and Lightning|In a storm cloud, colliding ice and hail separate electric charge. When it builds up enough, it jumps as a giant spark: lightning. It heats the air to about 30,000 °C in an instant, the air expands explosively, and that blast is thunder.|Benjamin Franklin||e|1752|Benjamin Franklin's famous kite experiment, proposed in 1750 and reportedly done in 1752, showed that lightning is electricity; French experimenters drew sparks from a storm cloud the same year. Inside a thundercloud, updrafts carry small ice crystals up while heavier soft hail falls, and collisions between them separate charge, generally leaving the top positive and the lower part negative. When the difference grows large enough, the air breaks down and a lightning channel forms. Light reaches you almost instantly, but sound travels about a kilometre every three seconds, so counting the gap tells you roughly how far away the strike was. Thunder rumbles because sound arrives from different parts of a long channel at different times.
ideahow|The Seasons|Seasons don't come from the Earth getting closer to the Sun. The Earth's axis is tilted, so for half the year your hemisphere leans towards the Sun, with higher sun and longer days, and for the other half leans away.|Ancient astronomers||e|antiquity|The Earth's axis is tilted about 23.4 degrees, and it points the same way in space all year. In June the northern hemisphere leans towards the Sun: the Sun climbs higher, its light strikes the ground more directly, concentrating its energy, and days are long. In December it leans away, and the southern hemisphere has summer. Distance plays almost no part: the Earth is actually closest to the Sun in early January, during the northern winter. Near the equator the tilt matters little, so seasons there are often wet and dry rather than warm and cold. The solstices and equinoxes, marked at ancient monuments from Stonehenge to Chichén Itzá, are the turning points of the tilt's effect.
ideahow|How Clouds Form|The Sun warms the ground, warm moist air rises, and as it rises it cools. Cool air can't hold as much water vapour, so the vapour condenses onto specks of dust and salt into countless tiny droplets: a cloud.|Luke Howard||e|1802|Air cools as it rises because pressure falls with height and the air expands. When it cools to its dew point, water vapour begins to condense, and it needs something to condense on: tiny particles of dust, sea salt or smoke called condensation nuclei. A single cloud may contain hundreds of tonnes of water, in droplets so small they float. Rain falls when droplets collide and grow, or when ice crystals grow at the expense of droplets and fall, melting on the way down. In 1802 the English chemist Luke Howard, an amateur meteorologist, gave clouds the Latin names we still use: cumulus (heap), stratus (layer), cirrus (curl of hair) and nimbus (rain cloud). Goethe wrote poems in his honour.
folkfable|The Fox and the Grapes|A FAMISHED FOX saw some clusters of ripe black grapes hanging from a trellised vine. She resorted to all her tricks to get at them, but wearied herself in vain, for she could not reach them. At last she turned away, hiding her disappointment and saying: “The Grapes are sour, and not ripe as I thought.”|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|Unable to reach what we want, we tell ourselves we never wanted it. The fable gave English "sour grapes".|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. This one is among the most quoted. The fox protects her pride by belittling what she failed to get, a move psychologists now study as reducing cognitive dissonance: when wanting and failing clash, we quietly change the wanting. It is worth noticing in ourselves, because the grapes we decide are sour are sometimes the very ones worth trying again for.
folkfable|The Hare and the Tortoise|A HARE one day ridiculed the short feet and slow pace of the Tortoise, who replied, laughing: “Though you be swift as the wind, I will beat you in a race.” The Hare, believing her assertion to be simply impossible, assented to the proposal; and they agreed that the Fox should choose the course and fix the goal. On the day appointed for the race the two started together. The Tortoise never for a moment stopped, but went on with a slow but steady pace straight to the end of the course. The Hare, lying down by the wayside, fell fast asleep. At last waking up, and moving as fast as he could, he saw the Tortoise had reached the goal, and was comfortably dozing after her fatigue. Slow but steady wins the race.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|Steady effort beats bursts of talent wasted by overconfidence. Slow but steady wins the race.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. The fable is less about slowness than about the hare's contempt: he loses not because he is slow but because he is sure he cannot lose. It has become shorthand for persistence, and a quiet comfort for anyone who isn't the fastest in the room.
folkfable|The Ants and the Grasshopper|THE ANTS were spending a fine winter’s day drying grain collected in the summertime. A Grasshopper, perishing with famine, passed by and earnestly begged for a little food. The Ants inquired of him, “Why did you not treasure up food during the summer?” He replied, “I had not leisure enough. I passed the days in singing.” They then said in derision: “If you were foolish enough to sing all the summer, you must dance supperless to bed in the winter.”|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|Prepare in good times for hard ones. Though some later tellers asked whether the singer's music was worth something too.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. La Fontaine retold it as the Cicada and the Ant, and it became a lesson in thrift for schoolchildren across Europe. Modern readers often take the grasshopper's side, noticing how coldly the ants mock him; Somerset Maugham wrote a story in which the idle brother ends up richer. Both readings are worth holding: plan ahead, and be kinder than the ants.
folkfable|The Shepherd's Boy and the Wolf|A SHEPHERD-BOY, who watched a flock of sheep near a village, brought out the villagers three or four times by crying out, “Wolf! Wolf!” and when his neighbors came to help him, laughed at them for their pains. The Wolf, however, did truly come at last. The Shepherd-boy, now really alarmed, shouted in an agony of terror: “Pray, do come and help me; the Wolf is killing the sheep;” but no one paid any heed to his cries, nor rendered any assistance. The Wolf, having no cause of fear, at his leisure lacerated or destroyed the whole flock. There is no believing a liar, even when he speaks the truth.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|Lie often enough and you won't be believed when you tell the truth. "Crying wolf" comes from this fable.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. Trust is built slowly and spent quickly. The fable describes something real about attention too: false alarms teach people to ignore alarms, which is why safety engineers worry about "alarm fatigue" in hospitals and cockpits.
folkfable|The Lion and the Mouse|A LION was awakened from sleep by a Mouse running over his face. Rising up angrily, he caught him and was about to kill him, when the Mouse piteously entreated, saying: “If you would only spare my life, I would be sure to repay your kindness.” The Lion laughed and let him go. It happened shortly after this that the Lion was caught by some hunters, who bound him by strong ropes to the ground. The Mouse, recognizing his roar, came and gnawed the rope with his teeth, and set him free, exclaiming: “You ridiculed the idea of my ever being able to help you, not expecting to receive from me any repayment of your favour; now you know that it is possible for even a Mouse to confer benefits on a Lion.”|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|No kindness is wasted, and the small can help the great. Never despise anyone as too weak to matter.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. The lion's mercy costs him nothing, and he laughs at the mouse's promise; yet the mouse's small teeth are exactly what the great lion needs. The fable has been loved for over two thousand years for its double lesson: be merciful, and don't measure people's worth by their size.
folkfable|The Crow and the Pitcher|A CROW perishing with thirst saw a pitcher, and hoping to find water, flew to it with delight. When he reached it, he discovered to his grief that it contained so little water that he could not possibly get at it. He tried everything he could think of to reach the water, but all his efforts were in vain. At last he collected as many stones as he could carry and dropped them one by one with his beak into the pitcher, until he brought the water within his reach and thus saved his life. Necessity is the mother of invention.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|Patience and ingenuity solve what force cannot.
