Necessity is the mother of invention.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. Remarkably, scientists have tested it. In 2009 researchers found that rooks, and later New Caledonian crows and Eurasian jays, really will drop stones into a tube of water to raise a floating treat within reach. The ancient storyteller had watched birds closely.
folkfable|The North Wind and the Sun|THE NORTH WIND and the Sun disputed as to which was the most powerful, and agreed that he should be declared the victor who could first strip a wayfaring man of his clothes. The North Wind first tried his power and blew with all his might, but the keener his blasts, the closer the Traveler wrapped his cloak around him, until at last, resigning all hope of victory, the Wind called upon the Sun to see what he could do. The Sun suddenly shone out with all his warmth. The Traveler no sooner felt his genial rays than he took off one garment after another, and at last, fairly overcome with heat, undressed and bathed in a stream that lay in his path. Persuasion is better than Force.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|Warmth and persuasion achieve what force cannot. The harder you push, the tighter people hold on.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. The fable is a whole philosophy of influence in a paragraph. Pressure produces resistance; warmth produces willingness. Parents, teachers, managers and diplomats keep rediscovering it.
folkfable|The Dog and the Shadow|A DOG, crossing a bridge over a stream with a piece of flesh in his mouth, saw his own shadow in the water and took it for that of another Dog, with a piece of meat double his own in size. He immediately let go of his own, and fiercely attacked the other Dog to get his larger piece from him. He thus lost both: that which he grasped at in the water, because it was a shadow; and his own, because the stream swept it away.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|Grasping at more, we can lose what we already have. Greed mistakes reflections for things.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. The dog loses twice: the larger piece was only a reflection, and his own is swept away while he snaps at it. It is a picture of comparison and envy, which make what we have look small beside an image.
folkfable|The Hen and the Golden Eggs|A COTTAGER and his wife had a Hen that laid a golden egg every day. They supposed that the Hen must contain a great lump of gold in its inside, and in order to get the gold they killed it. Having done so, they found to their surprise that the Hen differed in no respect from their other hens. The foolish pair, thus hoping to become rich all at once, deprived themselves of the gain of which they were assured day by day.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|Impatient greed destroys the source of steady good. Better versions of the tale gave us "killing the goose that lays the golden eggs".|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. In most retellings it is a goose, and the phrase "killing the goose that lays the golden eggs" is used for any short-sighted grab that ruins a lasting source of value, from overfishing a sea to burning out a good worker.
folkfable|The Mice in Council|THE MICE summoned a council to decide how they might best devise means of warning themselves of the approach of their great enemy the Cat. Among the many plans suggested, the one that found most favour was the proposal to tie a bell to the neck of the Cat, so that the Mice, being warned by the sound of the tinkling, might run away and hide themselves in their holes at his approach. But when the Mice further debated who among them should thus “bell the Cat,” there was no one found to do it.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A plan is worth little if nobody can carry it out. "Who will bell the cat?"|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. Every committee has met this cat. The best idea in the room is useless until someone is willing, and able, to do the dangerous part. The phrase "to bell the cat" still means taking on the risky task everyone agrees needs doing.
folkfable|The Oak and the Reeds|A VERY LARGE OAK was uprooted by the wind and thrown across a stream. It fell among some Reeds, which it thus addressed: “I wonder how you, who are so light and weak, are not entirely crushed by these strong winds.” They replied, “You fight and contend with the wind, and consequently you are destroyed; while we on the contrary bend before the least breath of air, and therefore remain unbroken, and escape.” Stoop to conquer.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|What bends survives the storm; what resists may break. Flexibility is a kind of strength.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. La Fontaine told it as the Oak and the Reed, and it echoes the Daodejing's praise of the supple over the stiff. Resilience is less about rigidity than about knowing when to bend and rise again.
folkfable|The Boy and the Filberts|A BOY put his hand into a pitcher full of filberts. He grasped as many as he could possibly hold, but when he tried to pull out his hand, he was prevented from doing so by the neck of the pitcher. Unwilling to lose his filberts, and yet unable to withdraw his hand, he burst into tears and bitterly lamented his disappointment. A bystander said to him, “Be satisfied with half the quantity, and you will readily draw out your hand.” Do not attempt too much at once.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|Try to grab everything at once and you may keep nothing. Take half and your hand comes free.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. Filberts are hazelnuts. The trapped hand is an image used ever since for greed and overreaching, and the so-called monkey trap, in which an animal won't let go of food in a narrow jar, tells the same story.
folkfable|The Fox and the Crow|A CROW having stolen a bit of meat, perched in a tree and held it in her beak. A Fox, seeing this, longed to possess the meat himself, and by a wily stratagem succeeded. “How handsome is the Crow,” he exclaimed, “in the beauty of her shape and in the fairness of her complexion! Oh, if her voice were only equal to her beauty, she would deservedly be considered the Queen of Birds!” This he said deceitfully; but the Crow, anxious to refute the reflection cast upon her voice, set up a loud caw and dropped the flesh. The Fox quickly picked it up, and thus addressed the Crow: “My good Crow, your voice is right enough, but your wit is wanting.”|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|Beware of flattery: the flatterer usually wants something you're holding.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. Vanity makes the crow open her beak. La Fontaine's version ends, in translation, with the lesson that every flatterer lives at the expense of whoever listens to him.
folkfable|The Father and His Sons|A FATHER had a family of sons who were perpetually quarreling among themselves. When he failed to heal their disputes by his exhortations, he determined to give them a practical illustration of the evils of disunion; and for this purpose he one day told them to bring him a bundle of sticks. When they had done so, he placed the faggot into the hands of each of them in succession, and ordered them to break it in pieces. They tried with all their strength, and were not able to do it. He next opened the faggot, took the sticks separately, one by one, and again put them into his sons’ hands, upon which they broke them easily. He then addressed them in these words: “My sons, if you are of one mind, and unite to assist each other, you will be as this faggot, uninjured by all the attempts of your enemies; but if you are divided among yourselves, you will be broken as easily as these sticks.”|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|Together we are hard to break; divided, easily broken. Unity is strength.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. The bundle of sticks became a symbol of strength in union, carved and printed on coats of arms, coins and seals. It is a gentle story about families and teams as much as nations.
folkfable|The Milk-Woman and Her Pail|A FARMER’S daughter was carrying her Pail of milk from the field to the farmhouse, when she fell a-musing. “The money for which this milk will be sold, will buy at least three hundred eggs. The eggs, allowing for all mishaps, will produce two hundred and fifty chickens. The chickens will become ready for the market when poultry will fetch the highest price, so that by the end of the year I shall have money enough from my share to buy a new gown. In this dress I will go to the Christmas parties, where all the young fellows will propose to me, but I will toss my head and refuse them every one.” At this moment she tossed her head in unison with her thoughts, when down fell the milk pail to the ground, and all her imaginary schemes perished in a moment.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|Don't count your chickens before they're hatched. Daydreams about the future can spill what we're carrying now.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. The girl is so busy spending imaginary money that she forgets the real pail on her head. The saying "don't count your chickens before they're hatched" grew from tales like this one.
folkfable|The Bald Knight|A BALD KNIGHT, who wore a wig, went out to hunt. A sudden puff of wind blew off his hat and wig, at which a loud laugh rang forth from his companions. He pulled up his horse, and with great glee joined in the joke by saying, “What a marvel it is that hairs which are not mine should fly from me, when they have forsaken even the man on whose head they grew.”|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|The best answer to being laughed at is to laugh too. Good humour disarms mockery.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, famous for his wit; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. A small fable with a large lesson in grace: by joining the joke, the knight takes away its sting and wins the company over.
folkfable|The Monkey and the Crocodile|A crocodile's wife longs to eat the heart of the monkey who throws her husband fruit. The crocodile offers the monkey a ride across the river, then reveals the plan midstream. "What a pity," says the monkey, "I left my heart in the tree. Take me back and I'll fetch it." Safe on his branch, he never comes down again.|Panchatantra||e|about 300 BC|The Panchatantra, "five treatises", is an ancient Indian collection of animal fables, traditionally said to have been told by the scholar Vishnu Sharma to teach three dull princes wisdom in statecraft and life. Its stories travelled through Persian and Arabic (as Kalila and Dimna) into dozens of languages and influenced fables across the world. This tale opens its fourth book, about losing what you have gained. A version also appears among the Buddhist Jataka tales. Its lesson is presence of mind: in danger, a calm wit is worth more than strength.
folkfable|The Talkative Tortoise|Two geese offer to carry a tortoise to a new lake: she will bite a stick and they will hold its ends, but she must not speak. Flying over a village, she hears people laughing at the sight, opens her mouth to answer them, and falls.|Panchatantra and the Jataka tales||e|about 300 BC|The story appears in both the Panchatantra and the Buddhist Jataka tales, stories of the Buddha's former lives. In the Jataka version the Bodhisattva, a wise counsellor, tells it to a king who talks too much. It travelled west too, into La Fontaine's fables. A gentle warning: the urge to answer every remark can cost us more than silence.
folkfable|The Lion and the Hare|A lion is eating the forest's animals one by one. When it is the clever hare's turn, she arrives late and says another lion stopped her, claiming to be king. Furious, the lion demands to see this rival. She leads him to a deep well, and he roars at his own reflection, leaps in to fight it, and drowns.|Panchatantra||e|about 300 BC|From the Panchatantra's first book. The weakest animal wins with intelligence, by turning the tyrant's pride against himself. Like the Lion and the Mouse in Aesop, it was loved by audiences who knew what it was to be ruled by the strong.
folkfairy|The Emperor's New Clothes|“But the Emperor has nothing at all on!” said a little child. “Listen to the voice of innocence!” exclaimed his father; and what the child had said was whispered from one to another. “But he has nothing at all on!” at last cried out all the people.|Hans Christian Andersen, Fairy Tales|Swindlers sell an emperor clothes said to be invisible to fools. Everyone pretends to see them, until a child says what all can see.|q||Hans Christian Andersen published it in 1837, adapting a medieval Spanish tale. Andersen added the child; in his first draft the story ended with the courtiers simply admiring the clothes. It has become the shorthand for collective pretence: when everyone fears being thought stupid, no one says the obvious. It takes someone with nothing to lose, or the honesty of a child, to break the spell.
folkfairy|The Ugly Duckling|To be born in a duck's nest, in a farmyard, is of no consequence to a bird, if it is hatched from a swan's egg.|Hans Christian Andersen, Fairy Tales|Mocked and chased out for being different, the duckling suffers a hard winter, then sees his reflection: he was a swan all along.|q||Andersen published it in 1843, and it is often read as his own story: the gangly son of a poor shoemaker and a washerwoman, mocked in his youth, who became one of the most famous writers in Europe. The tale comforts anyone who doesn't fit where they started. Its ending is gentle: the swan is "so happy, and yet not at all proud".
folkfairy|The Nightingale|Suddenly there came through the open window the sound of sweet music. Outside, on the bough of a tree, sat the living nightingale. She had heard of the emperor's illness, and was therefore come to sing to him of hope and trust.|Hans Christian Andersen, Fairy Tales|An emperor prefers a jewelled clockwork bird to the plain living nightingale. When he lies dying and the toy is broken, the real bird returns and sings.|q||Andersen wrote it in 1843, perhaps inspired by his unrequited love for the singer Jenny Lind, "the Swedish Nightingale". It is a story about the real and the artificial: the mechanical bird always sings the same tune and is admired for its jewels, while the living one sings freely and moves people to tears. The nightingale forgives and returns, asking only to come and go as she likes.
folkfairy|The Snow Queen|pieces were hardly so large as a grain of sand, and they flew about in the wide world, and when they got into people's eyes, there they stayed; and then people saw everything perverted, or only had an eye for that which was evil. This happened because the very smallest bit had the same power which the whole mirror had possessed. Some persons even got a splinter in their heart, and then it made one shudder, for their heart became like a lump of ice.|Hans Christian Andersen, Fairy Tales|A devil's mirror that shows everything ugly shatters, and its splinters lodge in people's eyes and hearts. Little Gerda crosses the world to rescue her friend Kai, whose heart has turned to ice.|q||Published in 1844 in seven stories, it is Andersen's longest tale and one of his best loved. The mirror is a picture of cynicism: a splinter in the eye makes us see only what is wrong; in the heart, it makes us cold. Kai is not saved by force but by Gerda's love and tears, which melt the splinter. It loosely inspired Disney's Frozen.
folkfairy|The Old Man and His Grandson|They were once sitting thus when the little grandson of four years old began to gather together some bits of wood upon the ground. ‘What are you doing there?’ asked the father. ‘I am making a little trough,’ answered the child, ‘for father and mother to eat out of when I am big.’ The man and his wife looked at each other for a while, and presently began to cry. Then they took the old grandfather to the table, and henceforth always let him eat with them, and likewise said nothing if he did spill a little of anything.|The Brothers Grimm, Household Tales (tr. Edgar Taylor and Marian Edwardes)|A couple make their frail old father eat apart from a wooden bowl, until their small son starts carving a trough for them, for when he is grown.|q||One of the shortest tales the Brothers Grimm collected, published from 1812. In a few lines it says something every generation needs to hear: the way we treat the old teaches our children how to treat us. Its power is that no one lectures; the child simply copies, and the parents see themselves.
folkfairy|Hansel and Gretel|The moon shone brightly, and the white pebbles which lay in front of the house glittered like real silver pennies. Hansel stooped and stuffed the little pocket of his coat with as many as he could get in. Then he went back and said to Gretel: ‘Be comforted, dear little sister, and sleep in peace, God will not forsake us,’|The Brothers Grimm, Household Tales (tr. Edgar Taylor and Marian Edwardes)|Abandoned in the forest by their parents in a famine, two children find their way home by moonlit pebbles, and later outwit a witch in a house of gingerbread.|q||The Grimms first published it in 1812. Scholars link it to the great European famines, when abandoning children was a terrible reality. What children have loved in it for two centuries is the children's own courage and cleverness: Hansel's pebbles, and Gretel's quick thinking that saves them both.
folkfairy|The Fisherman and His Wife|‘What does she want now?’ said the fish. ‘Ah!’ said he, ‘she wants to be lord of the sun and moon.’ ‘Go home,’ said the fish, ‘to your pigsty again.’ And there they live to this very day.|The Brothers Grimm, Household Tales (tr. Edgar Taylor and Marian Edwardes)|A poor fisherman's wife sends him back again and again to a magic fish: a cottage, a castle, a crown, the papacy. When she asks to rule the sun and moon, they are back in their pigsty.|q||The tale was written down in Low German dialect by the painter Philipp Otto Runge and published by the Grimms in 1812. Each wish brings a darker sea. It is a perfect picture of insatiable wanting: nothing satisfies, because the wanting grows with every gain. Pushkin retold it in verse as The Tale of the Fisherman and the Fish.
folkfairy|Briar Rose|A large hedge of thorns soon grew round the palace, and every year it became higher and thicker; till at last the old palace was surrounded and hidden, so that not even the roof or the chimneys could be seen.|The Brothers Grimm, Household Tales (tr. Edgar Taylor and Marian Edwardes)|A princess pricks her finger and sleeps for a hundred years, with all her court, behind a hedge of thorns, until the time is right.|q||The Grimms' Briar Rose (1812) is their version of the tale Charles Perrault had published as Sleeping Beauty in 1697. In the Grimms' telling the thorns open by themselves for the prince who comes when the hundred years are over. Many read it as a story about timing and patience: some things can't be forced, and open when their season comes.
folkfairy|Rumpelstiltskin|“Merrily the feast I’ll make.\nToday I’ll brew, tomorrow bake;\nMerrily I’ll dance and sing,\nFor next day will a stranger bring.\nLittle does my lady dream\nRumpelstiltskin is my name!”|The Brothers Grimm, Household Tales (tr. Edgar Taylor and Marian Edwardes)|A little man spins straw into gold for a miller's daughter at the price of her firstborn child, unless she can guess his name.|q||From the Grimms' collection of 1812. Folklorists note how widespread the motif is: knowing a being's true name gives power over it, from Rumpelstiltskin to Tom Tit Tot in England. When the queen speaks his name, his power is gone. Naming a fear, many people find, is the first step to loosening its hold.
folkfairy|Cinderella|A kind girl, mistreated by her stepmother and stepsisters, is helped by magic to go to the prince's festival, flees at midnight and leaves behind a slipper; the prince searches the kingdom for the foot it fits.|Charles Perrault and the Brothers Grimm||e|1697|One of the most widespread stories on earth, with hundreds of versions. The earliest known written one is Chinese: Ye Xian, recorded by Duan Chengshi in the 9th century, with a golden slipper and a magic fish. Charles Perrault's French version of 1697 gave us the fairy godmother, the pumpkin coach and the glass slipper; the Grimms' Aschenputtel has a hazel tree on her mother's grave instead. At its heart: goodness unrecognised, and finally seen.
folkfairy|Beauty and the Beast|To save her father, Beauty goes to live with a fearsome Beast in his enchanted castle. Slowly she comes to love his kindness, and her love breaks the spell that made him a beast.|Jeanne-Marie Leprince de Beaumont||e|1756|Gabrielle-Suzanne de Villeneuve wrote a long version in 1740; Jeanne-Marie Leprince de Beaumont's shortened retelling of 1756, written for young readers, became the classic. It belongs to an ancient family of animal-bridegroom tales, including Cupid and Psyche. Its message is to look beneath appearances, and that love sees what others can't.
folkfairy|Jack and the Beanstalk|A poor boy trades the family cow for five magic beans. His mother throws them out of the window; overnight a beanstalk grows into the clouds, and Jack climbs it to a giant's castle, a hen that lays golden eggs and a singing harp.|English folktale||e|1734|One of the best-known English folktales, first printed in the 18th century and popularised by Joseph Jacobs's English Fairy Tales of 1890. It is a tale of risk and luck: Jack's foolish-seeming trade turns out to be the wisest thing he could have done. Children love its reversal: the small, poor boy outwits the enormous, rich giant.
folkfairy|Little Red Riding Hood|A girl taking food to her grandmother through the woods meets a wolf, who learns where she is going, races ahead and waits in Grandmother's bed. "Grandmother, what big teeth you have!"|Charles Perrault and the Brothers Grimm||e|1697|Charles Perrault published it in 1697 with a blunt ending and a moral warning young girls against charming strangers. The Grimms' 1812 version, Little Red-Cap, adds the huntsman who rescues both girl and grandmother. Folklorists have found related tales across Europe and as far as China and Korea. It is a story about the moment when a friendly face hides danger, and about learning to notice.
folkfairy|Puss in Boots|A miller's poorest son inherits only a cat. But the cat asks for a pair of boots and a bag, and by sheer nerve and cunning makes his master rich, marries him to a princess and wins him a castle from an ogre.|Charles Perrault||e|1697|Earlier versions appeared in Italy in the 16th and 17th centuries, but Perrault's 1697 tale made Puss famous. It is a cheeky story in which a trickster hero succeeds through confidence and invention rather than virtue, and Perrault's own moral admits that cleverness often counts for more than inheritance.
folktrick|Nasreddin and the Fur Coat|One day Cogia Efendi went to a bridal festival. The master of the feast observing his old and wretched garments, paid him no consideration whatever. The Cogia saw that he had no chance of notice; so going out he hurried to his house, and putting on a splendid pelisse, returned to the place of festival. No sooner did he enter the door than the master advanced to meet him, and saying, 'Welcome, Cogia Efendi,' with all imaginable honour and reverence placed him at the head of the table, and said, 'Please to eat, Lord Cogia.' Forthwith the Cogia taking hold of one of the furs of his pelisse, said, 'Welcome, my pelisse, please to eat, my lord.' The master looking at the Cogia with great surprise, said, 'What are you about?' Whereupon the Cogia replied, 'It is quite evident that all the honour paid is paid to my pelisse, so let it have some food too.'|Tales of Nasreddin Hodja, The Turkish Jester (tr. George Borrow)|Ignored at a feast in his old clothes, Nasreddin returns in a fine coat and is seated in honour, so he feeds the coat.|q||Nasreddin Hodja is the wise fool of Turkish, Persian, Arab and Central Asian folklore, said to have lived in Anatolia in the 13th century; his grave is shown at Akşehir in Turkey. Hundreds of short tales are told of him, and UNESCO celebrated him in 1996. George Borrow, the English traveller and linguist, translated this Turkish collection. The coat tale is perhaps the most famous: a perfect puncture of snobbery.
folktrick|The Cauldron That Died|One day the Cogia borrowed a cauldron of a brazier, and carrying it home, put a little saucepan into it, and then carrying it back, returned it to its owner. The owner seeing a little saucepan in the cauldron, said, 'What is this?' 'Why,' cried the Cogia, 'the cauldron has borne a child'; whereupon the owner took possession of the saucepan. One day the Cogia asked again for the cauldron, and having obtained it, carried it home. The owner of the cauldron waited one day and even five days for his utensil, but no cauldron coming, he went to the house of the Cogia and knocked at the door. The Cogia coming to the door, said, 'What do you want?' 'The cauldron,' said the man. 'Oh, set your heart at rest,' said the Cogia, 'the cauldron is dead.' 'O Cogia,' said the man, 'can a cauldron die?' 'Oh,' said the Cogia, 'as you believed it could bear a child, why should you not believe that it can die?'|Tales of Nasreddin Hodja, The Turkish Jester (tr. George Borrow)|A neighbour happily accepts that his cauldron "gave birth" to a saucepan, but not that it then died. Nasreddin holds him to his own logic.|q||A favourite Nasreddin tale for its neat trap: the neighbour believes the absurd when it profits him and rejects it when it doesn't. Nasreddin's jokes often work this way, holding a mirror up to everyday self-interest and inconsistency.
folktrick|Nasreddin Preaches|One day the Cogia ascending again into the pulpit, said, 'O Mussulmen, do ye not know what I am going to say to you?' 'We do know,' they replied. Then said the Cogia, 'Some of ye do know already, what should I have to say to you?' Then descending from the chair he went out. The assembly separated quite astonished, and, when they were out, continued to say, 'Which are those of us who know? Which are those who do not know?' The Cogia one day again mounting the chair in the same manner, said, 'O brothers, when I said to ye, "Do you know what I shall say?" there were some who said, "We know," others said, "We do not." It were now well that those among ye who knew what the Cogia said should teach those that did not.'|Tales of Nasreddin Hodja, The Turkish Jester (tr. George Borrow)|Asked to preach, Nasreddin finds a reason not to whether the people know what he'll say or not, and finally tells those who know to teach those who don't.|q||Nasreddin was said to be an imam or teacher, and many tales show him in the pulpit, dodging the sermon with comic logic. Behind the joke is a real thought about learning: knowledge spreads best when those who have it share it.
folktrick|Nine Aspres|One night the Cogia dreamt that he was given nine aspres, whereupon the Cogia said, 'O now pray make them up ten'; afterwards he said, 'Make them up eleven,' and then presently, a dispute having arisen, he awoke and saw that in his hand he had nothing, thereupon closing his eyes anew and stretching out his hands, he said, 'Well, well, I shall be content with nine aspres.'|Tales of Nasreddin Hodja, The Turkish Jester (tr. George Borrow)|In a dream Nasreddin haggles for more coins, wakes empty-handed, and shuts his eyes to accept the nine after all.|q||An aspre was a small silver Ottoman coin. The tale is a tiny parable of greed: haggling for more, he loses all, and his comic attempt to go back to the dream is how we all sometimes try to recover a chance we pushed too far.
folktrick|How Anansi Got the Stories|In the olden days all the stories which men told were stories of Nyankupon, the chief of the gods. Spider, who was very conceited, wanted the stories to be told about him.|West African Folk-Tales (ed. W. H. Barker)|Anansi the spider wins all the world's stories from the sky god by capturing bees, a python and a leopard, each with a clever trick. Ever since, they are Anansi stories.|q||Anansi comes from the Akan peoples of present-day Ghana. In the version collected by W. H. Barker in 1917, Nyankupon, the sky god, sets three impossible tasks, and Anansi completes each one by flattering or fooling the creature into helping. Through the Atlantic slave trade, Anansi travelled to the Caribbean and the Americas, where his stories of the small outwitting the powerful carried a particular meaning, and are still told today.
folktrick|Brer Rabbit and the Tar Baby|Brer Fox makes a figure of tar and sets it by the road. Brer Rabbit greets it, gets no answer, hits it and sticks fast. Caught, he begs the fox: do anything, but please don't throw me in the briar patch. The fox does, and the rabbit, born and bred there, escapes.|African American folklore||e|19th century|Brer Rabbit stories were told among enslaved African Americans in the southern United States, drawing on African trickster tales of the hare. They were widely published by Joel Chandler Harris from 1880, in a framing that is now criticised for its nostalgic picture of slavery; the stories themselves belong to the people who told them. The tar baby has become an image for a situation that gets stickier the more you struggle with it, and the briar patch for using an opponent's assumptions against him.
folktrick|Reynard the Fox|Summoned to the court of King Noble the lion to answer for his crimes, Reynard the fox talks his way out of every charge with lies, flattery and nerve, and leaves his accusers worse off than himself.|Medieval European folklore||e|12th century|Reynard tales spread through France, Flanders, Germany and England from the 12th century, in Latin, French and Dutch poems; William Caxton printed an English version in 1481, and Goethe retold it in 1794. Under the animal masks they satirised the greed and hypocrisy of nobles and clergy. Reynard is a scoundrel, but readers have always enjoyed watching him outwit the powerful.
folktrick|Till Eulenspiegel|A wandering prankster takes every order literally to expose fools. Told to bake owls and monkeys, he bakes owl- and monkey-shaped loaves; told to make a sign that says what a thing is, he does exactly that, and leaves his masters fuming.|German folklore||e|1510|Tales of Till Eulenspiegel were printed in Strasbourg around 1510 and translated across Europe; Richard Strauss wrote a tone poem about his merry pranks in 1895. His name is often read as "owl mirror": he holds up a mirror to people's foolishness, especially the pompous and the hypocritical, by doing exactly what they say rather than what they mean.
folktrick|Kitsune, the Fox Spirit|In Japanese folklore foxes grow wiser and more magical with age, gaining up to nine tails. They can take human form, often as a beautiful woman, and may be tricksters, faithful lovers or messengers of the rice god Inari.|Japanese folklore||e|ancient|Fox spirits appear in Chinese and Korean lore as well as Japanese. In Japan, white foxes are the messengers of Inari, god of rice and prosperity, and thousands of fox statues guard Inari shrines, most famously at Fushimi Inari in Kyoto. The kitsune is neither good nor evil but clever and changeable, a reminder that cleverness can serve either.
folkcarib|Jack Mantora|Jack Mantora me no choose any.|Jamaican Anansi story ending, Jamaican Song and Story (ed. Walter Jekyll)|The traditional words that end a Jamaican Anansi story: "Jack Mantora, I don't choose any of you" for the trouble the tale's characters got into.|q||Walter Jekyll, who collected Jamaican stories and songs in the 1900s, explained that all Anancy stories ended with these or similar words: the teller addresses Jack, a member of the company, and says, in effect, I don't pick you out to be punished as the characters were. Anansi came to the Caribbean with enslaved Akan people from West Africa, and his tales of a small, quick-witted creature outwitting the strong were told across the islands. In Trinidad and Tobago he is often Compè Anansi.
folkcarib|Papa Bois|Keeper of the forest in Trinidad and Tobago: an old man covered in leaves or hair, with cloven hooves and sometimes small horns, who protects the animals and trees. He can turn into a deer to lead greedy hunters deep into the bush, and blows a cow's horn to warn the animals.|Trinidad and Tobago folklore||e|traditional|His name comes from French Creole, "father of the woods". In the stories he is stern but fair: he punishes those who kill for sport or take more than they need, and spares those who are respectful. Tradition says that if you meet him, you should greet him politely, "Bonjou, vieux papa", and never stare at his feet. He has been taken up as a symbol of conservation, a forest guardian for a modern age of deforestation.
folkcarib|Mama Dlo|Mother of the waters in Trinidad and Tobago lore, half woman and half great snake, who guards the rivers and punishes those who pollute them or harm their creatures. To escape her, it is said, take off your left shoe, turn it upside down and walk home backwards.|Trinidad and Tobago folklore||e|traditional|Her name comes from the French Creole maman de l'eau, "mother of the water", and she is related to water spirits across the Caribbean and West Africa, such as Mami Wata. In some tellings she is the partner of Papa Bois, so that together they guard both forest and river. Like him, she has become an emblem of respect for the natural world.
folkcarib|La Diablesse|A beautiful woman in a long dress and wide hat walks the country roads at night. Hidden under her skirt, one foot is a cow's hoof. She charms men into following her, then leads them astray into the bush or a river.|Trinidad and Tobago folklore and the French Caribbean||e|traditional|La Diablesse, "the she-devil", appears in the folklore of Trinidad and Tobago, Dominica, St Lucia, Grenada and the French Caribbean. The stories warned against wandering at night and against being taken in by appearances. She often appears in costume at Carnival, where the old characters of folklore still walk.
folkcarib|The Soucouyant|By day an old woman living at the edge of the village; by night she sheds her skin, hides it in a mortar, and flies as a ball of fire to drink the blood of sleepers. Throw salt on her skin and she can't put it back on; scatter rice at the door and she must stop to count every grain.|Trinidad and Tobago folklore and the eastern Caribbean||e|traditional|The soucouyant belongs to a family of skin-shedding spirits found in Guadeloupe, Martinique, Dominica and Suriname as well as Trinidad, with roots in West African and French folklore. The rice at the door is a lovely detail: she is compelled to count, and dawn catches her. The Trinidad-born novelist David Chariandy took her name for his 2007 novel Soucouyant.
folkcarib|The Douens|Small figures with big straw hats that hide their faces, and feet turned backwards, so their tracks seem to lead the other way. They call to children by name from the forest and lure them away to play. That is why, old people say, you never call a child's name aloud near the bush at night.|Trinidad and Tobago folklore||e|traditional|The douens are said to be the spirits of children who died before they were baptised. Their backward feet are a clever detail: anyone who follows their tracks walks in the wrong direction. The legend kept children close to home, and still turns up in Carnival costumes and Trinidad storytelling.
folkcarib|The Lagahoo|A man by day who becomes a beast at night, dragging a heavy chain, sometimes carrying a coffin on his head. He roams the dark roads, and his rattle of chains warns travellers to hide.|Trinidad and Tobago folklore||e|traditional|The name comes from the French loup-garou, "werewolf", brought to Trinidad with French planters and their enslaved workers in the 18th century, and the figure changed in the island's stories. Like the other night spirits, it belonged to the evening storytelling sessions, often around a wake or a fire, where frightening tales were a way of passing on caution and community.
folkcarib|The Moko Jumbie|Towering on stilts above the Carnival crowds, the moko jumbie dances high over the road. Tradition holds that his great height lets him see evil coming before others can and protect the village.|Trinidad and Tobago folklore and the Caribbean||e|traditional|The stilt-walker's tradition came to the Caribbean with enslaved West Africans; Moko is often linked to a protective spirit, and jumbie is a Caribbean word for a ghost or spirit. The tradition nearly faded in Trinidad but was revived from the 1980s, and today children learn stilt-walking in community camps. It is a living folklore: a guardian who dances.
folkcarib|The Rolling Calf|In Jamaican folklore, a monstrous calf with fiery red eyes that roams the roads at night, dragging a chain. It is said to be the spirit of a wicked person, and it can be held off by the light of the moon or by counting.|Jamaican folklore||e|traditional|The rolling calf belongs to the world of duppies, the spirits of the dead in Jamaican belief. Stories said it was the ghost of a butcher or a cruel person, chained by its wrongs. Like the soucouyant's rice, it can be delayed by objects it is compelled to count. The tales were told to warn against walking alone at night and against living cruelly.
folkcarib|The Bacoo|In Guyanese folklore, a small mischievous spirit kept in a bottle. Fed on milk and bananas, it can bring its keeper wealth; neglected, it turns to mischief, throwing stones and moving things about the house.|Guyanese folklore||e|traditional|The bacoo is a kind of bargain made flesh: a source of luck that demands constant feeding and brings trouble when ignored. Stories of bacoos warned against shortcuts to riches, and remain a familiar part of Guyanese storytelling.
folkworld|Stone Soup|Hungry travellers arrive in a village where no one will share. They set a pot of water over a fire with a stone in it, saying it makes wonderful soup, though a little carrot would improve it. One by one the curious villagers add something, and soon there is a feast for all.|European folktale||e|traditional|Told across Europe with a stone, a nail or an axe in the pot, the tale shows how cooperation can be coaxed out of people who each feel they have too little to share. It is a favourite of teachers and community organisers: everyone contributes a little, and together they make something none could alone.
folkworld|The Pied Piper of Hamelin|Plagued by rats, the town of Hamelin hires a piper in a coat of many colours, who leads the rats into the river with his tune. When the townspeople refuse to pay, he plays again, and the town's children follow him away, never to return.|German legend||e|1284|The legend is tied to a real date: a record from Hamelin speaks of 130 children lost on 26 June 1284. Historians still debate what happened, from emigration to the east to a children's crusade or a disaster; the rats were added in the 16th century. The Grimms recorded it, and Robert Browning's poem of 1842 made it famous in English. Its lesson is plain: keep your promises.
folkworld|Vasilisa the Beautiful|Sent by her cruel stepmother to fetch light from the witch Baba Yaga, Vasilisa survives impossible tasks with the help of a little doll her dying mother gave her, which she feeds and consults in secret.|Russian folktale||e|1850s|Collected by Alexander Afanasyev, whose great collection of Russian folktales appeared from 1855 to 1863. The doll is the mother's blessing carried into the dark forest: a picture of inner guidance or intuition, as many readers have seen it. Vasilisa succeeds not by strength but by attention, courtesy and trust in that quiet voice.
folkworld|The Tongue-Cut Sparrow|A kind old man keeps a pet sparrow, which his greedy wife punishes by cutting its tongue. The man finds the sparrow's home and is offered a gift: a small basket or a large one. He chooses the small one and finds treasure. His wife takes the large one, and it is full of monsters.|Japanese folktale||e|traditional|One of the best-known Japanese folktales, included in Yei Theodora Ozaki's Japanese Fairy Tales of 1908. The choice of baskets is the heart of it: modesty is rewarded and greed punished, a pattern found in folktales everywhere but told here with gentle Japanese humour.
folkworld|The Mirror of Matsuyama|A dying mother gives her daughter a mirror and tells her to look into it each day, and she will see her mother. The girl does, and sees her mother's face, which is her own, grown to resemble her.|Japanese folktale||e|traditional|A tender tale retold by Yei Theodora Ozaki in 1908. The girl's comfort comes from a small misunderstanding that is also a deep truth: we carry our parents in our faces, our habits and our love. It is one of the gentlest stories about grief in any tradition.
folkworld|Scheherazade|A king, betrayed by his wife, marries a new bride each night and has her killed at dawn. Scheherazade volunteers, and each night tells him a story that breaks off at the most exciting moment. After a thousand and one nights, he has changed.|The Thousand and One Nights||e|9th century onwards|The frame story of the One Thousand and One Nights, a collection that grew over centuries from Persian, Indian and Arab sources. Antoine Galland's French translation from 1704 made it famous in Europe, and added some of its best-known tales. Scheherazade's weapon is storytelling: she saves her own life, and many others, by keeping the king curious, and by showing him a world larger than his rage.
folkworld|Ali Baba and the Forty Thieves|A poor woodcutter overhears robbers open their treasure cave with the words "Open, sesame!" and helps himself to some gold. His greedy brother forgets the words to get out. The clever slave girl Morgiana outwits the thieves and saves the family.|The Thousand and One Nights||e|1710|The tale was added to the Nights by Antoine Galland, who heard it from a Syrian storyteller, Hanna Diyab, in Paris in 1709; no earlier Arabic manuscript is known. The true hero is Morgiana, whose quick wits save Ali Baba again and again. "Open sesame" has become the phrase for any magic key.
folkworld|The Magic Paintbrush|A poor boy, Ma Liang, longs to paint but has no brush. A wise old man gives him a magic one: whatever he paints comes to life. He paints ploughs and buckets for the villagers, and when a greedy emperor demands gold, Ma Liang paints a sea that sweeps the emperor away.|Chinese folktale||e|traditional|A favourite Chinese tale, often retold for children. It asks what a gift is for: Ma Liang uses his to serve the poor and refuses to serve greed, whatever the threat. It is also a tale about art: what we make can change the world.
folkworld|The Golem of Prague|To protect Prague's Jewish quarter from persecution, Rabbi Judah Loew shapes a giant figure from river clay and brings it to life with a holy word. It guards the people faithfully, until it grows too powerful and must be returned to clay.|Jewish folklore||e|16th century|Rabbi Judah Loew ben Bezalel was a real and famous scholar in 16th-century Prague, though the golem legend attached to him was written down only in the 19th century. Golem stories are among the ancestors of Frankenstein and modern fears about robots and artificial intelligence: a protector we create can slip beyond our control.
folkworld|The Flying Dutchman|A ghost ship doomed to sail the oceans forever, never able to make port. Sailors said that sighting it, glowing in a storm off the Cape of Good Hope, was an omen of disaster.|Sailors' legend||e|18th century|The legend grew among sailors from the late 18th century, often about a captain who swore he would round the Cape of Good Hope if it took until Judgement Day. Richard Wagner's opera of 1843 gave him a chance of redemption through faithful love. Mirages called Fata Morgana, which can make ships appear to float above the horizon, may explain some sightings.
folkworld|Tam Lin|Young Janet goes to Carterhaugh wood and meets Tam Lin, a mortal captured by the Fairy Queen. To win him back on Halloween night she must pull him from his horse and hold him fast while the fairies change him into a lizard, a snake, a bear, a lion and a burning brand.|Scottish ballad||e|16th century|Tam Lin is an old Scottish Border ballad, mentioned in 1549 and collected by Robert Burns and Walter Scott. It is unusual among fairy tales for its brave, determined heroine, who rescues the man. Holding on to someone you love through every terrible transformation has been read as a picture of steadfast love through illness or hard times.
ideahow|The Electric Kettle|A coil of resistance wire under the water heats it. When it boils, steam is piped onto a small disc made of two metals bonded together, which snaps into a new shape as it warms and flicks the switch off.|Bill Russell and Peter Hobbs||e|1955|Electric kettles existed from the 1890s, but they had to be watched. In 1955 Bill Russell and Peter Hobbs sold the first kettle that switched itself off, using steam to trigger a switch. The key part is a bimetallic disc: two metals that expand by different amounts when heated, so the disc bends, and at a set temperature it snaps from one curve to the other, like the bottom of an oil can. A typical kettle draws two to three kilowatts, one of the most powerful appliances in a home, which is why boiling only the water you need saves noticeable energy.
ideahow|The Toaster|Thin wires of a nickel-chromium alloy glow red-hot when current flows, beaming heat at the bread. The heat drives water out of the surface and browns it, while a timer or a heat-sensing strip pops it up.|Charles Strite||e|1919|Electric toasters needed a wire that could glow red-hot for years without burning through; Albert Marsh's nichrome alloy of 1905 made them practical. Early toasters toasted one side at a time and had to be watched. Charles Strite, annoyed by burnt toast in his factory canteen, patented the pop-up toaster in 1919, with springs and a timer. The browning itself is chemistry: as the surface dries and passes about 140 °C, sugars and proteins react to make hundreds of new flavour and colour compounds, the Maillard reaction.
ideahow|Why Food Browns|Toast, roast meat, coffee and fresh bread all brown because of the Maillard reaction: when food is hot and dry enough, sugars react with proteins to make hundreds of new flavours and colours.|Louis-Camille Maillard||e|1912|The French chemist Louis-Camille Maillard described the reaction in 1912 while studying how proteins form in the body. It needs heat, roughly 140 °C or more, and a fairly dry surface, which is why boiled meat stays grey while seared meat browns, and why patting meat dry before frying helps. Hundreds of different flavour molecules form, depending on the sugars and amino acids present, giving each food its own roasted taste. It is different from caramelisation, which is sugar alone breaking down. Too far, and the browning becomes burning.
ideahow|The Espresso Machine|An espresso machine forces hot water through tightly packed, finely ground coffee at about nine times the pressure of the air. In under half a minute it pulls out oils and flavours a slow drip can't, topped by a foam called crema.|Angelo Moriondo||e|1884|Angelo Moriondo of Turin patented a steam-driven coffee machine in 1884; Luigi Bezzera and Desiderio Pavoni made single-cup espresso machines in the early 1900s. In 1948 Achille Gaggia's lever machine reached far higher pressures, creating the crema and the modern espresso. The fine grind gives a huge surface area, and the pressure pushes water through quickly, so flavours are extracted fast before bitter compounds take over. Crema is an emulsion of coffee oils and tiny bubbles of carbon dioxide from freshly roasted beans.
ideahow|The Pin Tumbler Lock|Inside the lock, small spring-loaded pins of different lengths block a cylinder from turning. The right key pushes each pin to exactly the right height, so the gaps all line up and the cylinder turns.|Linus Yale Jr.||e|1861|The idea is ancient: the Egyptians used wooden pin locks four thousand years ago. Linus Yale Sr. made a pin tumbler lock in 1848, and his son Linus Yale Jr. patented the compact cylinder lock in 1861, still the commonest lock in the world. Each pin is split in two. Without a key, the upper pins cross the line between the cylinder and the housing and stop it turning. The ridges on the key lift each pair so that its split sits exactly on that line. Lock-picking works by lifting the pins one at a time while gently turning the cylinder.
ideahow|Air Conditioning|An air conditioner is a fridge for a room. A refrigerant evaporates in coils indoors, soaking up the room's heat, and is pumped outside where it gives that heat away. It also dries the air, as water condenses on the cold coils.|Willis Carrier||e|1902|Willis Carrier designed the first modern air conditioner in 1902 for a printing plant in Brooklyn, where humidity was making paper swell and the colours misprint. Controlling humidity was the original point; cooling came with it. Like a fridge, an air conditioner uses the vapour-compression cycle: evaporation inside absorbs heat, a compressor and outdoor coils release it. That is why the outdoor unit blows hot air and a pipe drips water. Air conditioning changed where people could live and work, and today uses a large share of the world's electricity.
ideahow|The Vacuum Cleaner|A fan inside pushes air out of the back, lowering the pressure inside the machine. The air around, at higher pressure, rushes in through the nozzle, carrying dust with it into a bag or bin.|Hubert Cecil Booth||e|1901|Early "cleaners" blew dust around. In 1901 the English engineer Hubert Cecil Booth tried sucking instead, reportedly through a handkerchief over his mouth on a restaurant chair, and found it worked. His first machine was horse-drawn and parked outside, with long hoses fed in through the windows. The physics: a vacuum cleaner doesn't so much pull as let the atmosphere push. The fan creates a partial vacuum, and the weight of air outside drives a fast stream through the nozzle. Bagless designs spin the air so dust is flung outwards and drops out.
ideahow|The Mirror|A mirror is glass backed with a thin layer of metal, usually silver or aluminium, so smooth that light bounces off at the same angle it arrived. That keeps the image sharp instead of scattering it.|Justus von Liebig||e|1835|People first used polished stone and metal. Venetian glassmakers in the 1500s backed glass with a tin and mercury amalgam, which was toxic to make. In 1835 the German chemist Justus von Liebig found a way to coat glass with a thin layer of pure silver using a chemical reaction, the basis of modern mirrors. Rough surfaces scatter light in all directions, but a smooth one reflects every ray at the same angle, preserving the image. A mirror doesn't actually swap left and right: it reverses front and back. Your right hand stays on the right side; it is the direction you face that flips.
ideahow|The Internet|When you send a message, it is cut into small packets, each labelled with where it's going. Routers pass them along, often by different paths, and your friend's device puts them back in order.|Vint Cerf and Bob Kahn||e|1974|The ARPANET, funded by the US Department of Defense, sent its first message in 1969 between two universities; it crashed after two letters, "lo". Its key idea, packet switching, meant no single line had to be held open for a whole conversation, and the network could route around damage. In 1974 Vint Cerf and Bob Kahn published the protocols that became TCP/IP, a common language that let different networks join into one internet. The World Wide Web, Tim Berners-Lee's system of linked pages, came later, in 1989; it runs on the internet but isn't the same thing.
ideahow|Bluetooth|Bluetooth is short-range radio. To avoid interference, a pair of devices hop together between 79 channels in an agreed pattern, about 1,600 times a second.|Jaap Haartsen||e|1994|Jaap Haartsen, a Dutch engineer at Ericsson, developed the technology in 1994 to replace the cables between phones and headsets. Frequency hopping spreads the signal so that interference on one channel only spoils a tiny moment, and the two devices, sharing the same pseudo-random sequence, keep in step. The name comes from Harald Bluetooth, a 10th-century Danish king who united warring tribes, as the technology would unite devices; its logo combines the runes for his initials, H and B. Range is usually around ten metres.
ideahow|QR Codes|A QR code stores information in a grid of black and white squares. Three big squares in the corners tell a camera which way up it is, and built-in error correction lets it be read even when part of it is damaged or covered.|Masahiro Hara||e|1994|Masahiro Hara designed the QR ("quick response") code in 1994 at Denso Wave, a Japanese company, to track car parts faster than barcodes allowed. He reportedly took the idea for the corner squares from a game of Go. A QR code can hold thousands of characters. It uses Reed–Solomon error correction, the same family of mathematics used on CDs, so at the highest setting up to about 30 per cent of the code can be missing and it still reads, which is why some have logos in the middle. Denso Wave chose not to enforce its patent, so anyone could use the codes freely.
ideahow|Barcodes|A barcode is a number written in stripes. A laser or camera sweeps across it, measuring the widths of the dark bars and light gaps, and turns them back into digits that the shop's computer looks up.|Norman Woodland and Bernard Silver||e|1952|Norman Woodland, a graduate student, had the idea on a beach in Miami in 1948, drawing lines in the sand like stretched-out Morse code dots and dashes; he and Bernard Silver patented it in 1952. It waited for lasers and cheap computers. The first product scanned with the modern Universal Product Code was a 10-pack of Wrigley's chewing gum, at a supermarket in Troy, Ohio, on 26 June 1974. The last digit of a barcode is a check digit, calculated from the others, so a misread almost always shows up as an error rather than the wrong price.
ideahow|Contactless Payment|A contactless card has a tiny chip and an antenna but no battery. The reader's radio field powers it for a moment when you tap, and the chip sends a one-time code that proves the card is genuine without revealing its secret key.|Payment card networks||e|1990s|The technology is near-field communication, which works only within a few centimetres, making it hard to read a card from across a room. When the card comes close, the reader's changing magnetic field induces a current in the card's coiled antenna, just enough to run its chip. The chip uses its secret key to sign the transaction with a code that is different every time, so a copied code is useless for another purchase. Phones work the same way but often send a substitute card number, a token, so the real one never leaves the phone. Early contactless systems such as Mobil's Speedpass appeared in 1997.
ideahow|Atomic Clocks|An atomic clock counts the vibrations of atoms. The second itself is defined as 9,192,631,770 cycles of a particular light given off by caesium atoms, and the network time on your phone comes from such clocks.|Louis Essen and Jack Parry||e|1955|Louis Essen and Jack Parry built the first accurate caesium atomic clock at the National Physical Laboratory in England in 1955. Every caesium-133 atom is identical, so its natural frequency is the same everywhere, a perfect pendulum provided by nature. In 1967 the second was redefined by it, replacing the Earth's rotation, which wobbles. GPS satellites carry atomic clocks, and your phone sets its time from the network and satellites. The newest optical clocks are so precise they would drift by less than a second over the age of the universe, and can detect the slowing of time from raising a clock a few centimetres.
ideahow|The Lift|A lift car hangs from steel cables over a pulley, balanced by a heavy counterweight, so the motor only lifts the difference. If the cables ever failed, spring-loaded safety brakes would grip the rails.|Elisha Otis||e|1854|Hoists had existed for centuries, but no one wanted to ride one: if the rope broke, you fell. In 1854, at the Crystal Palace exhibition in New York, Elisha Otis stood on a raised platform and had the rope cut. His safety brake, a spring that snapped teeth into the guide rails when the tension went slack, held it fast. "All safe, gentlemen, all safe!" Safe lifts made skyscrapers possible. Modern lifts use several cables, any one of which can hold the car, plus governors that trigger the brakes if the car moves too fast.
ideahow|The Helicopter|A helicopter's rotor blades are spinning wings. Tilting the whole spinning disc steers the lift, moving the helicopter forward, back or sideways, while a small tail rotor stops the body spinning the opposite way.|Igor Sikorsky||e|1939|Leonardo da Vinci sketched an "aerial screw" around 1490, but practical helicopters came only in the 20th century. Igor Sikorsky, who had built aeroplanes in Russia before emigrating to America, flew his VS-300 in 1939, with the now-standard single main rotor and tail rotor. The pilot changes the angle of all the blades together to climb or descend, and changes it around each turn to tilt the disc and travel. By Newton's third law, a rotor turned by the engine pushes the body the other way, so the tail rotor's sideways thrust is essential.
ideahow|The Hot-Air Balloon|Hot air is less dense than cold air: the same space holds fewer, faster-moving molecules. Fill a big enough envelope with it and the surrounding cooler air pushes it up, like a cork in water.|The Montgolfier brothers||e|1783|Joseph-Michel and Jacques-Étienne Montgolfier, sons of a paper-maker, noticed that paper bags rose over a fire. In September 1783 their balloon carried a sheep, a duck and a rooster at Versailles before Louis XVI; in November it carried the first people, Jean-François Pilâtre de Rozier and the Marquis d'Arlandes, across Paris. A balloon floats for the same reason a ship does, Archimedes' principle: it is pushed up by the weight of the cooler air it displaces. Pilots climb by firing the burner and descend by letting the air cool or venting it, and they steer only by finding winds blowing different ways at different heights.
ideahow|The Rocket|A rocket burns fuel and throws the hot gas out of the back very fast. By Newton's third law, pushing the gas backwards pushes the rocket forwards, which is why rockets work in space, with nothing to push against.|Robert Goddard||e|1926|The Chinese used gunpowder rockets for fireworks and war by the 13th century. In 1926 the American physicist Robert Goddard launched the first liquid-fuelled rocket from a farm in Auburn, Massachusetts; it flew for 2.5 seconds. The New York Times mocked him, claiming a rocket couldn't work in a vacuum; in 1969, as Apollo 11 headed for the Moon, the paper printed a correction. A rocket carries its own oxygen, since there is none in space. Most of a rocket's mass is fuel, and dropping empty stages along the way is the trick that makes orbit reachable.
ideahow|How We See Colour|Your eyes have three kinds of colour-sensing cone cells, most sensitive to short, medium and long wavelengths of light. Every colour you see is your brain comparing how strongly those three are stimulated.|Thomas Young||e|1802|Thomas Young proposed in 1802 that three kinds of receptors could explain all colour vision, and Hermann von Helmholtz developed the idea; it was confirmed in the 1960s when individual cones were measured. Because there are only three, very different mixtures of light can look identical, which is how screens make every colour from just red, green and blue dots. Colour blindness, most often red-green, comes from one cone type missing or shifted, and is far more common in men because the genes lie on the X chromosome. Colour, in a sense, is made in the brain: light itself has wavelengths, not colours.
ideahow|Fever|A fever isn't the infection overheating you. Chemical signals from your immune system tell the brain's thermostat to raise its set point, so you shiver and feel cold until your body reaches the new, higher temperature.|Carl Wunderlich||e|1868|The German physician Carl Wunderlich took over a million temperature readings and in 1868 established about 37 °C as normal and fever as a sign of disease. The hypothalamus at the base of the brain works like a thermostat. During infection, signalling molecules called pyrogens raise its set point; the body then conserves and makes heat by shivering and narrowing skin blood vessels, which is why you feel chilled as a fever rises. A modestly higher temperature helps some immune cells work and slows some germs. When the fever breaks, the set point drops and you sweat to shed the extra heat.
ideahow|How Antibiotics Work|Antibiotics kill bacteria or stop them multiplying by attacking parts our own cells don't have, such as the bacterial cell wall. They do nothing against viruses, like colds and flu, which work in an entirely different way.|Alexander Fleming||e|1928|In 1928 Alexander Fleming returned from holiday to find a mould, Penicillium, had killed bacteria on one of his culture plates. Turning penicillin into a medicine took Howard Florey, Ernst Chain and their team in Oxford in the 1940s; the three shared a Nobel Prize in 1945. Penicillin blocks bacteria from building their cell walls, so they burst; human cells have no such wall. Bacteria multiply quickly, and any that happen to survive a drug pass on their resistance. Taking antibiotics when they aren't needed, or stopping early against advice, helps resistance spread.
ideahow|How Hearing Works|Sound waves make your eardrum vibrate. Three tiny bones pass the vibration into the fluid-filled cochlea, where waves ripple along a membrane, and thousands of hair cells turn each pitch into nerve signals.|Georg von Békésy||e|1961|The three middle-ear bones, the hammer, anvil and stirrup, are the smallest in the body; they magnify the eardrum's movement to push on the fluid of the inner ear. The cochlea is a coiled tube about the size of a pea. Georg von Békésy, who won the 1961 Nobel Prize, showed that sound sends a travelling wave along its basilar membrane, peaking at different places for different pitches: high notes near the base, low notes near the tip. About 15,000 hair cells in each ear turn that movement into signals. They do not grow back once destroyed, which is why loud noise causes permanent hearing loss.
ideahow|Sore Muscles After Exercise|The soreness a day or two after unusual exercise isn't lactic acid. It comes from tiny strains in the muscle fibres, especially from lowering movements, and the inflammation as the body repairs them, stronger than before.|Theodore Hough||e|1902|Theodore Hough described delayed-onset muscle soreness in 1902. Lactic acid, long blamed, actually clears from the muscles within an hour or so of exercise. The soreness, peaking at 24 to 72 hours, comes from microscopic damage, particularly in "eccentric" movements where a muscle lengthens under load, like walking downhill or lowering a weight. Repair brings swelling and sensitivity, and leaves the muscle better protected next time, which is why the same workout hurts much less the second week. Gentle movement usually helps more than complete rest.
ideahow|Snowflakes|A snowflake grows from water vapour freezing directly onto a tiny ice crystal. Water molecules lock together in a hexagonal pattern, so flakes have six sides, and each takes its own path through different temperatures and humidity.|Ukichiro Nakaya||e|1936|Wilson Bentley, a Vermont farmer, first photographed a snowflake in 1885 and went on to capture more than five thousand. The Japanese physicist Ukichiro Nakaya grew the first artificial snowflakes in 1936 and mapped how their shape depends on temperature and moisture: plates at some temperatures, needles or columns at others, and the familiar branching stars around −15 °C when the air is humid. Because each flake falls through slightly different conditions, and conditions are the same across the whole flake at any moment, its six arms grow alike while flakes differ from one another. Nakaya called snowflakes "letters sent from heaven".
ideahow|Wind|The sun warms the Earth unevenly. Warm air rises and leaves lower pressure behind, and air flows in from areas of higher pressure. That flow is wind, bent into curving paths by the Earth's spin.|George Hadley||e|1735|On the smallest scale, a sea breeze: land heats faster than water by day, air rises over the land, and cooler sea air flows in to replace it. On the largest, the tropics are heated far more than the poles, driving great circulations of air. In 1735 George Hadley explained the steady trade winds this way and saw that the Earth's rotation turns them. The rotation's effect was put in mathematical form by Gaspard-Gustave de Coriolis in 1835: moving air is deflected to the right in the northern hemisphere and to the left in the southern, which is why storms spin in opposite directions in each.
ideahow|The Phases of the Moon|Half of the Moon is always lit by the Sun. As it orbits the Earth over about 29.5 days, we see different amounts of that sunlit half: none at new moon, all of it at full moon. It isn't the Earth's shadow.|Anaxagoras||e|5th century BC|The Greek philosopher Anaxagoras taught in the 5th century BC that the Moon shines by reflected sunlight, and used it to explain eclipses correctly. When the Moon is between us and the Sun, its lit side faces away: new moon. A week later we see half the lit side: first quarter, "half moon". When it is opposite the Sun, we see the whole lit face: full moon. A common mistake is to think the phases are the Earth's shadow; that only happens during a lunar eclipse, at full moon. The Moon always shows us the same face, because it turns once on its axis for each orbit.
ideahow|Solar Panels|A solar cell is a sandwich of two kinds of silicon. When sunlight strikes it, its energy knocks electrons loose, and the boundary between the layers pushes them one way round a circuit: electricity, with no moving parts.|Daryl Chapin, Calvin Fuller and Gerald Pearson||e|1954|Edmond Becquerel, aged nineteen, discovered the photovoltaic effect in 1839. In 1954 Daryl Chapin, Calvin Fuller and Gerald Pearson at Bell Labs made the first practical silicon solar cell, turning about 6 per cent of sunlight into electricity; it was soon powering satellites. Today's common panels manage around 20 to 23 per cent, and their price has fallen so steeply, roughly 90 per cent in the 2010s alone, that solar is now among the cheapest sources of new electricity in much of the world. Panels produce direct current, which an inverter turns into the alternating current of the mains.
ideahow|Wind Turbines|A wind turbine's blades are shaped like aeroplane wings. The wind flowing past them makes lift that turns the rotor, which spins a generator. Double the wind speed and the power available rises eightfold.|James Blyth||e|1887|James Blyth built a cloth-sailed wind turbine to light his holiday cottage in Scotland in 1887, the first known to make electricity; in Denmark Poul la Cour developed the idea in the 1890s. Modern turbines turn their blades to face the wind and change their angle to control speed, and can be over 200 metres tall. The power in wind rises with the cube of its speed, which is why sites are chosen so carefully and why turbines are built high, where wind is stronger and steadier. They shut down in storms to protect themselves.
ideahow|Nuclear Power|In a reactor, uranium atoms split when struck by neutrons, releasing heat and more neutrons, which split more atoms. Controlled carefully, this chain reaction boils water, and the steam turns turbines like any other power station.|Enrico Fermi||e|1942|Fission was discovered in 1938 by Otto Hahn and Fritz Strassmann, and explained by Lise Meitner and Otto Frisch. On 2 December 1942, under the stands of a sports field at the University of Chicago, Enrico Fermi's team ran Chicago Pile-1, the first self-sustaining chain reaction. Control rods that absorb neutrons keep each fission causing, on average, exactly one more. A kilogram of uranium fuel can release millions of times the energy of a kilogram of coal, without carbon emissions, but leaves radioactive waste that must be stored for thousands of years, and accidents like Chernobyl in 1986 and Fukushima in 2011 shaped public fear.
ideahow|Recycling Aluminium|Making aluminium from ore takes enormous amounts of electricity. Melting down an old can takes only about 5 per cent as much, and aluminium can be recycled again and again without getting worse.|Charles Martin Hall and Paul Héroult||e|1886|Aluminium was once more precious than gold; Napoleon III reportedly kept aluminium cutlery for his most honoured guests. In 1886 Charles Martin Hall in the United States and Paul Héroult in France independently found how to extract it cheaply with electricity, a process still used, which is why smelters are built near cheap hydroelectric power. Because recycling skips the electrolysis, a recycled can saves about 95 per cent of the energy, and a can can be back on the shelf within about two months. Roughly three-quarters of all the aluminium ever made is still in use.
ideahow|Earthquakes|The Earth's crust is broken into plates that slowly move. Where they grind past each other they stick, strain builds up for years, and then the rock suddenly slips: the jolt spreads out as seismic waves.|Harry Fielding Reid||e|1910|After the San Francisco earthquake of 1906, Harry Fielding Reid studied how fences and roads had been offset along the San Andreas Fault, and in 1910 proposed elastic rebound: rock bends like a stick under strain until it snaps back. Plate tectonics explained why. Each whole step up the magnitude scale means about 32 times more energy, so a magnitude 7 releases about a thousand times the energy of a magnitude 5. Seismic waves travel at different speeds; the fast P-waves arrive first, which is how early warning systems can give seconds of notice before the shaking.
ideahow|Water Towers|A water tower is a raised tank that gives the pipes their pressure by gravity. Every ten metres of height adds about one bar of pressure. Pumps fill it when demand is low, and it keeps water flowing steadily when everyone turns on their taps at once.|Municipal waterworks||e|19th century|Water pushes down with its weight: each metre of water above a tap adds about a tenth of a bar of pressure, so a tank 30 metres up gives roughly 3 bar at ground level, enough to reach upper floors. A tower also works as a buffer. Pumps can run steadily, often at night when electricity is cheap and demand low, while the tower absorbs the morning rush. In a power cut it keeps supplying water for hours. Towers are built on hills where possible, and many towns use a hilltop reservoir instead, which does the same job with the ground as the stand.
ideahow|Home Water Tanks and Pumps|Where the mains supply is irregular, homes store water in a tank and use a pump to push it through the house. A pressure switch starts the pump when pressure falls as you open a tap, and stops it when the pipes are full again.|Household plumbing||e|20th century|Many homes, in the Caribbean and elsewhere, keep a storage tank so there is water even when the mains supply is off. A pump draws from the tank and pushes water into the pipes. To stop the pump switching on for every glass of water, systems often include a small pressure tank with a cushion of air inside; the air compresses as water is pumped in and pushes the water out again when a tap opens. A pressure switch typically turns the pump on at one setting and off at a higher one, for example 20 and 40 psi. A pump that clicks on and off rapidly often means that air cushion has been lost.
ideahow|The Tap|An old-style tap screws a rubber washer down onto a seat to stop the flow; when the washer wears, it drips. Most modern taps use two ceramic discs with holes: turn the handle and the holes line up.|Al Moen||e|1947|Compression taps have been used since the 19th century: turning the handle drives a spindle down, pressing a washer against a seat. Washers harden and crack with age, which is why old taps drip and why changing a washer is the classic plumbing job. Ceramic disc cartridges, common since the 1970s, use two very hard, very smooth discs pressed together, sealing without rubber; a quarter turn opens them fully. Al Moen invented the single-handle mixer tap, reportedly after scalding his hands on a two-handled tap, and it went on sale in 1947.
ideahow|Drain Traps and Vents|The bend under every sink holds a little water that blocks sewer gas from coming up the drain. Pipes running up through the roof let air into the drains, so flowing water can't suck those seals dry.|Thomas Crapper||e|1880|The U-shaped trap, a refinement of Alexander Cumming's S-bend, keeps a plug of water between your home and the sewer. Thomas Crapper, a London plumber who did much to popularise good sanitary plumbing, patented an improved U-bend in 1880 (despite the legend, he didn't invent the flush toilet). Without vent pipes, a rush of water down a drain would pull a vacuum behind it and siphon the traps empty, letting smells in, and drains would gurgle and run slowly. If a rarely used sink starts to smell, its trap has often just dried out: run the tap.
ideahow|The Water Heater|A storage water heater keeps a tank of water hot with an element or burner and a thermostat. Cold water enters at the bottom; hot water, being lighter, rises and is drawn from the top. Tankless heaters instead heat water only as it flows.|Edwin Ruud||e|1889|The Norwegian-American engineer Edwin Ruud built an automatic storage water heater in Pittsburgh in 1889. In a tank, a dip tube carries cold water to the bottom, so the hottest water stays at the top where it is drawn off. A thermostat switches the heating on and off. A magnesium or aluminium "sacrificial anode" rod corrodes instead of the steel tank, and replacing it can add years to the heater's life. Tankless or instant heaters, including the electric shower heads common in hot countries, heat water as it passes, so there is no stored hot water to lose heat, but they need a lot of power at once.
ideahow|The Septic Tank|Where there are no sewers, waste goes to a buried tank. Solids sink to the bottom, grease floats to the top, and bacteria slowly break things down. The clearer water in the middle flows out to a drain field, where the soil finishes cleaning it.|Jean-Louis Mouras||e|1860s|Jean-Louis Mouras, a Frenchman, is credited with building the first septic tank in the 1860s, and was surprised years later to find it held little solid waste. A tank has three layers: sludge at the bottom, a scum of fats on top, and liquid between, which flows out to perforated pipes in gravel and soil, where microbes do the final treatment. Sludge builds up, so tanks need pumping out every few years. Bleach, grease and wipes are the enemies: they kill the bacteria or clog the system.
ideahow|Water Hammer|Water flowing in a pipe is heavy and moving. Shut a tap suddenly and it has to stop all at once; the jolt sends a pressure spike back down the pipe, and the pipes bang. That's water hammer.|Nikolai Zhukovsky||e|1898|The Russian scientist Nikolai Zhukovsky worked out the physics in 1898, after studying failures in Moscow's water mains: the pressure surge depends on how fast the water was moving and how quickly it is stopped. Washing machines and dishwashers, with valves that snap shut, are common culprits. The fixes are a cushion for the shock, an air chamber or a small "water hammer arrestor" with a sealed air pocket, securing loose pipes, and sometimes lowering the household pressure. Left alone, repeated hammering can loosen joints and damage valves.
ideahow|Clean Tap Water|Before it reaches your tap, water is treated: chemicals make dirt clump so it settles, sand filters catch what is left, and chlorine or another disinfectant kills germs, with a little left in the pipes to keep it safe on the way.|John Snow||e|1854|In 1854 the London doctor John Snow traced a cholera outbreak in Soho to a single water pump on Broad Street, and the outbreak waned after its handle was removed: the beginning of understanding that disease travels in dirty water. Slow sand filtration was already in use, and in 1908 Jersey City became the first American city to chlorinate its public supply continuously. A typical treatment plant adds a coagulant so particles clump into flakes, lets them settle, filters the water through sand and gravel, disinfects it and adjusts its acidity so it doesn't corrode pipes. Clean water and sewers did more to lengthen lives than almost any medicine.
ideahow|The Gearbox|An engine works best within a narrow range of speeds. Gears let it stay there: low gears trade speed for pulling force to get moving, high gears trade force for speed when cruising. The clutch disconnects the engine while you change.|Panhard and Levassor||e|1894|René Panhard and Émile Levassor built one of the first cars with a sliding-gear transmission in 1894; Levassor reportedly called it "brutal but it works". A small gear driving a big one turns the output more slowly but with more force, like a bicycle's low gear on a hill. Without gears, an engine would either stall pulling away or race uselessly at speed.
