Automatic gearboxes use planetary gear sets and a fluid coupling instead of a clutch pedal, and continuously variable transmissions slide smoothly through a range of ratios. Electric cars usually need just one gear, because electric motors deliver strong force over a very wide range of speeds.
ideahow|Hydraulic Brakes|Pressing the pedal pushes a small piston into a cylinder of brake fluid. Because liquid can't be squashed, the pressure travels down pipes to bigger pistons at each wheel, which squeeze pads against discs, turning the car's motion into heat.|Malcolm Loughead||e|1917|Malcolm Loughead (whose brother Allan's company later became Lockheed) patented hydraulic brakes in 1917. They rely on Pascal's principle: pressure in a closed liquid is the same everywhere, so a small piston pushing on the fluid makes larger pistons at the wheels push harder, multiplying your foot's force. Pads rubbing on discs turn movement into heat; on long downhill runs brakes can overheat and fade, which is why drivers use a lower gear to let the engine help. Brake fluid absorbs water over time, which lowers its boiling point, so it is replaced every few years.
ideahow|Anti-lock Brakes|A skidding wheel can't steer. Anti-lock brakes watch each wheel's speed, and when one is about to lock, release and reapply its brake many times a second, so you can stop hard and still steer.|Bosch||e|1978|Rolling tyres grip better than sliding ones, and a locked front wheel gives no steering at all. Anti-lock systems were tried on aircraft and trains, and Bosch introduced the first modern electronic ABS on the Mercedes-Benz S-Class in 1978. Sensors at each wheel feed a computer; if a wheel slows far faster than the car, valves briefly drop its brake pressure, then restore it, many times a second. Drivers feel this as a pulsing pedal and a buzzing noise, which is normal: the advice is to press firmly, keep pressing, and steer. ABS doesn't always shorten stopping distances, especially on gravel or snow, but it keeps control.
ideahow|The Differential|In a turn, the outside wheels travel further than the inside ones. A differential is a set of gears that lets the two driven wheels turn at different speeds while the engine still drives both.|Onésiphore Pecqueur||e|1827|Without a differential, two wheels fixed to one axle would have to skid around every corner. The French clockmaker Onésiphore Pecqueur patented a differential for steam vehicles in 1827, though the principle appears in older machines. Small gears inside the differential housing can spin around one another, sharing the engine's turning force between the wheels while letting their speeds differ. Its weakness: if one wheel is on ice or mud, the force goes to the wheel that spins easiest, and the car stays stuck. Limited-slip and locking differentials fix this for off-road driving.
ideahow|The Turbocharger|A turbocharger uses the engine's own exhaust. The hot gas spins a turbine, which drives a fan that squeezes more air into the cylinders. More air means more fuel can burn, and a smaller engine can give more power.|Alfred Büchi||e|1905|The Swiss engineer Alfred Büchi patented the idea in 1905. Turbochargers were first widely used in aircraft engines, where they made up for thin air at altitude, and then in lorries, ships and cars. The turbine and compressor share a shaft spinning at up to around 200,000 revolutions a minute. Because the turbine needs exhaust flow to spin up, there is a brief delay before the boost arrives when you press the accelerator, called turbo lag. Turbocharging lets car makers use smaller engines that use less fuel when cruising but still have power when needed.
ideahow|The Catalytic Converter|Inside the exhaust is a honeycomb coated with precious metals. As the hot gases pass, the metals help poisonous carbon monoxide, unburnt fuel and nitrogen oxides turn into carbon dioxide, water and nitrogen.|Eugene Houdry||e|1950s|The French engineer Eugene Houdry, concerned about smog, developed catalytic converters in the 1950s; they became standard on new American cars from 1975, which also required unleaded petrol, because lead ruins the catalyst. A catalyst speeds up a chemical reaction without being used up itself. The honeycomb gives an enormous surface area, coated with platinum, palladium and rhodium. Converters only work well once hot, so most pollution from a modern car comes in the first few minutes after a cold start. Those precious metals are also why catalytic converters are so often stolen.
ideahow|Airbags|In a crash, a sensor detects the sudden slowing within milliseconds and fires a small charge that fills the bag with gas in about a thirtieth of a second. The bag cushions your head and then deflates through vents as you sink into it.|Allen Breed||e|1968|John Hetrick in the USA and Walter Linderer in Germany patented airbags in the early 1950s, but compressed air was too slow. Allen Breed developed a reliable crash sensor in 1968, and chemical inflators made the bags fast enough. The whole event, from impact to full inflation, takes roughly 30 to 50 milliseconds, faster than a blink. The bag must already be deflating as you meet it, or it would bounce you back. Airbags are designed to work with seatbelts, not instead of them, and children should not sit in front of an active passenger airbag.
ideahow|Tyres and Grip|Each tyre touches the road over a patch about the size of your hand, and all a car's grip comes through those four patches. Soft rubber grips by moulding into the road's texture, and tread grooves channel water away so the tyre doesn't float on it.|John Boyd Dunlop||e|1888|John Boyd Dunlop, a Belfast vet, reinvented the air-filled tyre in 1888 for his son's tricycle (Robert Thomson had patented one in 1845). Air cushions the ride and lets the tyre flatten into a contact patch. On wet roads the tread must push water out of the way; at speed, with worn tread, a wedge of water can lift the tyre off the road entirely, called aquaplaning, and steering vanishes. Under-inflated tyres flex more, overheat and wear on the edges, and waste fuel. That is why tread depth and pressure are among the most important safety checks on any car.
ideahow|The Electric Car|An electric car stores energy in a large battery. Electronics feed it to a motor that drives the wheels directly, with full force from a standstill. Slowing down, the motor works as a generator and puts energy back into the battery.|Thomas Parker||e|1884|Electric cars are as old as petrol ones: the English inventor Thomas Parker built one in 1884, and around 1900 electric cars were common in American cities, quieter and cleaner than early petrol cars. Cheap oil and the electric starter motor for petrol engines nearly ended them, until lithium-ion batteries brought them back in the 2000s. An electric drive has very few moving parts and turns roughly three-quarters or more of the battery's energy into motion, against perhaps a third for a petrol engine. Regenerative braking recovers energy that friction brakes would waste as heat.
ideahow|The Hybrid Car|A hybrid has a petrol engine and an electric motor with a small battery. The motor handles slow driving and helps when accelerating; braking recharges the battery; the engine switches off at stops and runs mostly where it is efficient.|Ferdinand Porsche||e|1900|The young Ferdinand Porsche built a petrol-electric hybrid car in 1900, the Lohner-Porsche. The idea returned with the Toyota Prius, launched in Japan in 1997, the first mass-produced hybrid. A petrol engine is least efficient in stop-start city traffic, exactly where an electric motor shines, so the car shares the work: energy that would be wasted as heat in braking is stored and used to pull away. Plug-in hybrids have larger batteries that can be charged from the mains, giving some all-electric driving before the engine is needed.
ideahow|Heat Pumps|A heat pump is an air conditioner that can run in reverse. Even cool outdoor air holds heat, and the refrigerant cycle can gather it and release it indoors, giving about three to four units of heat for every unit of electricity.|Lord Kelvin||e|1852|William Thomson, Lord Kelvin, described the principle in 1852. The cycle is the fridge's: a refrigerant boils at low pressure and temperature, soaking up heat even from cold air, then a compressor squeezes it, making it hotter than the room, and it gives that heat away indoors. Because it moves heat rather than making it, a heat pump can deliver several times more heat than the electricity it uses, which is why it beats any electric heater. Many split-system air conditioners are heat pumps: a valve reverses the flow so the same unit heats in winter and cools in summer.
ideahow|Inverter Air Conditioners|An old air conditioner's compressor runs flat out, then switches off, then on again. An inverter model varies its compressor's speed, running gently and continuously to hold the temperature, which is quieter, steadier and usually uses much less electricity.|Toshiba engineers||e|1981|Toshiba introduced the first inverter air conditioner in 1981. The inverter is electronics that change the frequency of the electricity driving the compressor's motor, and so its speed. Starting a compressor from rest takes a surge of power, and on-off cycling lets the room swing above and below the set temperature. Running slowly for longer is more efficient, especially once the room is near the right temperature. Inverter units are now the norm in much of the world, and labels showing their seasonal efficiency let you compare models.
ideahow|Humidity and Comfort|You cool down by sweating: as sweat evaporates, it carries heat away. In humid air, sweat evaporates slowly, so 32 °C in the tropics can feel far hotter than 32 °C in a desert. Air conditioners help partly by drying the air.|Robert Steadman||e|1979|Relative humidity is how much water vapour the air holds compared with the most it could at that temperature. When it is high, sweat lingers on the skin instead of evaporating, and the body can't shed heat. The American researcher Robert Steadman published the "apparent temperature" in 1979, the basis of the heat index used in weather forecasts: at 32 °C and 70 per cent humidity, it feels like over 40 °C. That is why a dehumidifier, or an air conditioner's drying effect, can make a room feel comfortable at a higher temperature setting, saving energy.
ideahow|Evaporative Coolers|An evaporative cooler blows air through wet pads. As the water evaporates, it takes heat from the air, which comes out cooler but moister. It works brilliantly in dry heat and poorly in humid climates.|Persian builders||e|ancient|The principle is ancient. Persian builders combined windcatchers, called badgir, with water channels and porous clay jars to cool buildings in the desert, and Egyptians hung wet mats in doorways. Evaporation absorbs a great deal of heat, so in dry air an evaporative cooler can drop the temperature by 10 °C or more using a fraction of an air conditioner's electricity. But it adds moisture, so in already humid places like the Caribbean it cools little and makes the air feel muggy: there, air conditioning or simple fans are better choices.
ideahow|The Dehumidifier|A dehumidifier is a small air conditioner that keeps its heat in the room. A fan pulls damp air over cold coils, where water condenses and drips into a tank, then over warm coils, so the air leaves drier and slightly warmer.|Refrigeration engineers||e|20th century|Like a fridge or air conditioner, a dehumidifier uses the refrigerant cycle, but both sets of coils are in the same box. The cold coils chill the air below its dew point, so moisture condenses, just like drops on a cold glass; the warm coils then reheat it. The room ends up a little warmer, because the machine's motor adds heat, but much drier. Damp homes in humid climates suffer from mould, musty smells and damage to books and clothes; keeping indoor humidity around 40 to 60 per cent is generally recommended.
ideahow|Ceiling Fans|A fan doesn't cool the air; its motor even warms it slightly. It cools you, by moving air across your skin so sweat evaporates faster and warm air near your body is carried away. So turn it off when you leave the room.|Philip Diehl||e|1882|Philip Diehl, an engineer who had worked for Isaac Singer's sewing machine company, adapted a sewing-machine motor to make the electric ceiling fan in 1882. A moving breeze strips away the thin layer of warm, humid air that clings to the skin and speeds evaporation, so you feel a few degrees cooler. A fan uses a tiny fraction of an air conditioner's electricity, and using the two together lets you set the air conditioner a couple of degrees higher for the same comfort. Many ceiling fans have a reverse switch to push warm air down in cool seasons.
ideahow|Insulation|Insulation works mostly by trapping still air, which conducts heat very poorly. Fibreglass, wool, foam and feathers are all ways of holding air still. In hot climates, reflective roof layers and pale roofs stop the sun's heat getting in.|Count Rumford||e|1790s|Benjamin Thompson, Count Rumford, measured the insulating power of materials such as fur, wool and cotton in the 1790s and concluded that they work by trapping air in their fibres. Heat travels by conduction, convection and radiation: still air blocks conduction, and stopping it moving blocks convection. Radiation is handled by shiny or pale surfaces, which is why reflective foil under roofs and white or "cool" roofs can keep a tropical house several degrees cooler. Insulation works both ways: it keeps heat in during winter and out during summer, making air conditioning cheaper to run.
ideahow|Refrigerants and the Ozone Layer|The gases inside fridges and air conditioners have changed. The CFCs used for decades turned out to destroy the ozone layer, so the world banned them; their replacements spare the ozone but trap heat, and are now being phased down too.|Mario Molina and Sherwood Rowland||e|1974|Thomas Midgley developed CFCs in 1928 as safe, non-toxic, non-flammable refrigerants, replacing dangerous gases like ammonia and sulphur dioxide. In 1974 Mario Molina and Sherwood Rowland showed that CFCs drift up to the stratosphere and release chlorine that destroys ozone, which shields us from ultraviolet light; they shared the 1995 Nobel Prize in Chemistry. The Antarctic ozone hole was reported in 1985, and the Montreal Protocol of 1987 phased out CFCs; the ozone layer is now slowly healing. Their replacements, HFCs, are powerful greenhouse gases, so the Kigali Amendment of 2016 is phasing them down. That is why old units must be emptied properly, never vented.
ideahow|Car Air Conditioning|A car's air conditioner uses the same cycle as a home unit. A compressor driven by the engine, or by the battery in an electric car, squeezes the refrigerant; the condenser in front of the radiator sheds the heat; the evaporator behind the dashboard cools the air you feel.|Packard||e|1939|Packard offered the first factory car air conditioning in 1939, a bulky unit filling much of the boot. Because the compressor takes power from the engine, running the AC uses extra fuel, especially in town; at higher speeds, though, open windows add drag, so AC can be the more efficient choice on the motorway. The system also dries the air, which is why it clears a misted windscreen quickly. Car AC that stops cooling often has a small refrigerant leak, and running it regularly, even in cool weather, keeps its seals lubricated.
folkfable|The Wolf and the Lamb|WOLF, meeting with a Lamb astray from the fold, resolved not to lay violent hands on him, but to find some plea to justify to the Lamb the Wolf’s right to eat him. He thus addressed him: “Sirrah, last year you grossly insulted me.” “Indeed,” bleated the Lamb in a mournful tone of voice, “I was not then born.” Then said the Wolf, “You feed in my pasture.” “No, good sir,” replied the Lamb, “I have not yet tasted grass.” Again said the Wolf, “You drink of my well.” “No,” exclaimed the Lamb, “I never yet drank water, for as yet my mother’s milk is both food and drink to me.” Upon which the Wolf seized him and ate him up, saying, “Well! I won’t remain supperless, even though you refute every one of my imputations.” The tyrant will always find a pretext for his tyranny.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A wolf invents one excuse after another to eat a lamb, and when each is answered, eats it anyway. The powerful find reasons for what they already mean to do.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. The fable is one of the oldest records of a truth about power: when force has decided, argument is only decoration. Phaedrus, the Roman fabler, told it too, and La Fontaine made it famous with the line that the reason of the strongest is always the best.
folkfable|The Wolf and the Crane|A WOLF who had a bone stuck in his throat hired a Crane, for a large sum, to put her head into his mouth and draw out the bone. When the Crane had extracted the bone and demanded the promised payment, the Wolf, grinning and grinding his teeth, exclaimed: “Why, you have surely already had a sufficient recompense, in having been permitted to draw out your head in safety from the mouth and jaws of a wolf.” In serving the wicked, expect no reward, and be thankful if you escape injury for your pains.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A crane pulls a bone from a wolf's throat and asks for the promised reward; the wolf says not being eaten is reward enough.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. A sharp little fable about doing favours for the dangerous. Its lesson is a warning rather than a cynicism: help freely if you choose, but don't expect gratitude from those who live by force.
folkfable|The Mountain in Labour|A MOUNTAIN was once greatly agitated. Loud groans and noises were heard, and crowds of people came from all parts to see what was the matter. While they were assembled in anxious expectation of some terrible calamity, out came a Mouse. Don’t make much ado about nothing.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A mountain groans and heaves, crowds gather in dread, and out comes a mouse. Great fuss, tiny result.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. The Roman poet Horace used it to mock writers who promise epics and deliver trifles: "the mountains will be in labour, and a ridiculous mouse will be born." It is the ancestor of every overhyped launch.
folkfable|The Dog in the Manger|A DOG lay in a manger, and by his growling and snapping prevented the oxen from eating the hay which had been placed for them. “What a selfish Dog!” said one of them to his companions; “he cannot eat the hay himself, and yet refuses to allow those to eat who can.”|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A dog lies in the oxen's hay, which he can't eat, and snaps at them so they can't eat it either.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. "Dog in the manger" has become the phrase for anyone who keeps from others what is of no use to them. It names a particular meanness: not wanting a thing, only not wanting anyone else to have it.
folkfable|The Bear and the Two Travellers|TWO MEN were traveling together, when a Bear suddenly met them on their path. One of them climbed up quickly into a tree and concealed himself in the branches. The other, seeing that he must be attacked, fell flat on the ground, and when the Bear came up and felt him with his snout, and smelt him all over, he held his breath, and feigned the appearance of death as much as he could. The Bear soon left him, for it is said he will not touch a dead body. When he was quite gone, the other Traveler descended from the tree, and jocularly inquired of his friend what it was the Bear had whispered in his ear. “He gave me this advice,” his companion replied. “Never travel with a friend who deserts you at the approach of danger.” Misfortune tests the sincerity of friends.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|When a bear appears, one traveller climbs a tree and leaves his friend to play dead. Later the friend says the bear whispered: never travel with someone who deserts you in danger.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. The fable's lesson, that misfortune tests friendship, is as old as friendship itself. The bear's "whisper" is a gentle way of saying what everyone saw.
folkfable|The Boasting Traveller|A MAN who had traveled in foreign lands boasted very much, on returning to his own country, of the many wonderful and heroic feats he had performed in the different places he had visited. Among other things, he said that when he was at Rhodes he had leaped to such a distance that no man of his day could leap anywhere near him as to that, there were in Rhodes many persons who saw him do it and whom he could call as witnesses. One of the bystanders interrupted him, saying: “Now, my good man, if this be all true there is no need of witnesses. Suppose this to be Rhodes, and leap for us.”|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A man boasts of a mighty leap he made in Rhodes, with witnesses to prove it. A listener says: suppose this is Rhodes, and leap for us.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. The bystander's reply became a Latin proverb, Hic Rhodus, hic salta: here is Rhodes, jump here. Hegel and Marx both quoted it. It means: don't tell us what you did elsewhere; show us here and now.
folkfable|The Boys and the Frogs|SOME BOYS, playing near a pond, saw a number of Frogs in the water and began to pelt them with stones. They killed several of them, when one of the Frogs, lifting his head out of the water, cried out: “Pray stop, my boys: what is sport to you, is death to us.”|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|Boys pelt frogs with stones for fun, until one frog cries out: what is sport to you is death to us.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. A tiny fable with a large reach. What feels like a harmless game to the powerful can be catastrophe to those on the receiving end, a thought that applies to teasing, to cruelty to animals and to many grown-up games of power.
folkfable|The Man and His Two Sweethearts|A MIDDLE-AGED MAN, whose hair had begun to turn gray, courted two women at the same time. One of them was young, and the other well advanced in years. The elder woman, ashamed to be courted by a man younger than herself, made a point, whenever her admirer visited her, to pull out some portion of his black hairs. The younger, on the contrary, not wishing to become the wife of an old man, was equally zealous in removing every gray hair she could find. Thus it came to pass that between them both he very soon found that he had not a hair left on his head. Those who seek to please everybody please nobody.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A greying man courted by a young woman and an older one: one plucks out his grey hairs, the other his dark ones, until he is bald.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. Its moral is the motto of every people-pleaser's exhaustion: those who seek to please everybody please nobody. Trying to be what everyone wants, he ends up with nothing of his own.
folkfable|The Astronomer|AN ASTRONOMER used to go out at night to observe the stars. One evening, as he wandered through the suburbs with his whole attention fixed on the sky, he fell accidentally into a deep well. While he lamented and bewailed his sores and bruises, and cried loudly for help, a neighbor ran to the well, and learning what had happened said: “Hark ye, old fellow, why, in striving to pry into what is in heaven, do you not manage to see what is on earth?”|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|An astronomer gazing at the stars as he walks falls into a well. A neighbour asks why, peering into heaven, he can't see what is on earth.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. The story is older than Aesop's collection in one form: Plato tells that Thales, the first Greek philosopher, fell into a well while watching the sky, and a Thracian servant girl laughed at him. It is a joke against absent-minded thinkers, but Plato took the philosopher's side.
folkfable|The Belly and the Members|THE MEMBERS of the Body rebelled against the Belly, and said, “Why should we be perpetually engaged in administering to your wants, while you do nothing but take your rest, and enjoy yourself in luxury and self-indulgence?” The Members carried out their resolve and refused their assistance to the Belly. The whole Body quickly became debilitated, and the hands, feet, mouth, and eyes, when too late, repented of their folly.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|The hands, mouth and feet refuse to work for the idle belly, and the whole body weakens. Every part depends on the others.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. Livy tells that the Roman statesman Menenius Agrippa used this fable around 494 BC to persuade the plebeians, who had walked out of Rome in protest, to return: the city, like a body, needed all its parts. Shakespeare put it in Coriolanus. It has been used ever since to argue for cooperation, and criticised for asking the hands to accept their lot.
folkfable|The Crab and Its Mother|A CRAB said to her son, “Why do you walk so one-sided, my child? It is far more becoming to go straight forward.” The young Crab replied: “Quite true, dear Mother; and if you will show me the straight way, I will promise to walk in it.” The Mother tried in vain, and submitted without remonstrance to the reproof of her child. Example is more powerful than precept.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A mother crab tells her child to stop walking sideways. The child says: show me how, and I'll follow.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. Example is more powerful than precept. Every parent, teacher and leader meets this crab: we teach far more by what we do than by what we say.
folkfable|The Ant and the Dove|AN ANT went to the bank of a river to quench its thirst, and being carried away by the rush of the stream, was on the point of drowning. A Dove sitting on a tree overhanging the water plucked a leaf and let it fall into the stream close to her. The Ant climbed onto it and floated in safety to the bank. Shortly afterwards a birdcatcher came and stood under the tree, and laid his lime-twigs for the Dove, which sat in the branches. The Ant, perceiving his design, stung him in the foot. In pain the birdcatcher threw down the twigs, and the noise made the Dove take wing.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A dove drops a leaf to save a drowning ant; later the ant stings a hunter's foot and saves the dove.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. A companion to the Lion and the Mouse: kindness given freely comes back from unexpected places, and even the smallest creature can repay it.
folkfable|The Peacock and the Crane|A PEACOCK spreading its gorgeous tail mocked a Crane that passed by, ridiculing the ashen hue of its plumage and saying, “I am robed, like a king, in gold and purple and all the colors of the rainbow; while you have not a bit of color on your wings.” “True,” replied the Crane; “but I soar to the heights of heaven and lift up my voice to the stars, while you walk below, like a cock, among the birds of the dunghill.” Fine feathers don’t make fine birds.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A peacock mocks a crane's dull feathers; the crane answers that she soars to the heavens while he struts below.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. Fine feathers don't make fine birds. The fable sets display against ability, a contrast that never goes out of date.
folkfable|The Trees and the Axe|A MAN came into a forest and asked the Trees to provide him a handle for his axe. The Trees consented to his request and gave him a young ash-tree. No sooner had the man fitted a new handle to his axe from it, than he began to use it and quickly felled with his strokes the noblest giants of the forest. An old oak, lamenting when too late the destruction of his companions, said to a neighboring cedar, “The first step has lost us all. If we had not given up the rights of the ash, we might yet have retained our own privileges and have stood for ages.”|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|The trees let a woodcutter have a little ash wood for a handle. With it he fells the whole forest. The first step lost them all.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. A fable about giving away a principle that seems small: the trees provide the very handle that destroys them. It is often told about freedoms surrendered one small step at a time.
folkfable|The Boy Bathing|A BOY bathing in a river was in danger of being drowned. He called out to a passing traveler for help, but instead of holding out a helping hand, the man stood by unconcernedly, and scolded the boy for his imprudence. “Oh, sir!” cried the youth, “pray help me now and scold me afterwards.” Counsel without help is useless.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A boy drowning in a river is scolded by a passer-by for his carelessness. "Help me now and scold me afterwards."|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. Counsel without help is useless. The boy's cry is one of the most practical lines in all the fables: in a crisis, lectures can wait.
folkfable|The Ass in the Lion's Skin|AN ASS, having put on the Lion’s skin, roamed about in the forest and amused himself by frightening all the foolish animals he met in his wanderings. At last coming upon a Fox, he tried to frighten him also, but the Fox no sooner heard the sound of his voice than he exclaimed, “I might possibly have been frightened myself, if I had not heard your bray.”|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|An ass in a lion's skin frightens the animals, until he brays and the fox laughs.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. Disguise can borrow a look but not a voice. The fable warns that pretending to be what we are not is fragile: sooner or later we open our mouths.
folkfable|The Ass and His Purchaser|A MAN wished to purchase an Ass, and agreed with its owner that he should try out the animal before he bought him. He took the Ass home and put him in the straw-yard with his other Asses, upon which the new animal left all the others and at once joined the one that was most idle and the greatest eater of them all. Seeing this, the man put a halter on him and led him back to his owner. On being asked how, in so short a time, he could have made a trial of him, he answered, “I do not need a trial; I know that he will be just the same as the one he chose for his companion.” A man is known by the company he keeps.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A buyer tries out an ass by putting it among his herd. It goes straight to the laziest one, and the buyer returns it at once.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. A man is known by the company he keeps. We often choose companions who resemble us, and others read us by them.
folkfable|The Gnat and the Bull|A GNAT settled on the horn of a Bull, and sat there a long time. Just as he was about to fly off, he made a buzzing noise, and inquired of the Bull if he would like him to go. The Bull replied, “I did not know you had come, and I shall not miss you when you go away.” Some men are of more consequence in their own eyes than in the eyes of their neighbors.|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A gnat settles on a bull's horn and asks whether he should leave. The bull didn't notice he had come and won't notice him go.|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. A gentle puncture of self-importance: we are often of far more consequence in our own eyes than in anyone else's. Strangely, it can be a comfort too.
folkfable|The Hare and the Hound|A HOUND started a Hare from his lair, but after a long run, gave up the chase. A goat-herd seeing him stop, mocked him, saying “The little one is the best runner of the two.” The Hound replied, “You do not see the difference between us: I was only running for a dinner, but he for his life.”|Aesop's Fables (tr. George Fyler Townsend)|A hound gives up chasing a hare. "The little one is the better runner," mocks a goatherd. "I ran for a dinner," says the hound, "he ran for his life."|q||Aesop is said to have been a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC; whether he really lived is uncertain, and the fables under his name gathered over centuries from many tellers. Motivation matters more than ability. Biologists call it the "life-dinner principle": in an arms race between predator and prey, the prey often runs harder, because it has more to lose.
folkfairy|The Princess and the Pea|Now it was plain that the lady must be a real Princess, since she had been able to feel the three little peas through the twenty mattresses and twenty feather beds. None but a real Princess could have had such a delicate sense of feeling.|Hans Christian Andersen, Fairy Tales|A queen tests a rain-soaked girl claiming to be a princess with a single pea under twenty mattresses. She sleeps badly: proof of royalty.|q||Andersen published it in 1835, from a tale he said he had heard as a child; his tongue-in-cheek last line, asking whether this wasn't a lady of real delicacy, shows he was poking fun. It has been read ever since as a satire on aristocratic sensitivity, and as a warm joke about people who notice everything.
folkfairy|The Swineherd|“I am come to despise thee,” said he. “Thou would'st not have an honorable Prince! Thou could'st not prize the rose and the nightingale, but thou wast ready to kiss the swineherd for the sake of a trumpery plaything. Thou art rightly served.”|Hans Christian Andersen, Fairy Tales|A prince offers a princess a real rose and a living nightingale; she scorns them, but kisses a swineherd for a musical toy. The swineherd is the prince.|q||Andersen published The Swineherd in 1841. It mocks a taste for the artificial over the real, the same theme as The Nightingale, and ends with no happy marriage at all: the prince simply goes home. Few fairy tales are so unsentimental.
folkfairy|Snowdrop|‘Tell me, glass, tell me true!\nOf all the ladies in the land,\nWho is fairest, tell me, who?’|The Brothers Grimm, Household Tales (tr. Edgar Taylor and Marian Edwardes)|A jealous queen's magic glass tells her that her stepdaughter Snowdrop is fairer, and she tries again and again to kill the girl, who finds shelter with seven dwarfs.|q||This is the Grimms' Snow White, called Snowdrop in this early English translation; Disney's 1937 film made it world famous, with the line "Mirror, mirror on the wall". The mirror is one of the great images of vanity and envy: a queen who must always be told she is the best.
folkfairy|The Elves and the Shoemaker|There was once a shoemaker, who worked very hard and was very honest: but still he could not earn enough to live upon; and at last all he had in the world was gone, save just leather enough to make one pair of shoes. Then he cut his leather out, all ready to make up the next day, meaning to rise early in the morning to his work. His conscience was clear and his heart light amidst all his troubles; so he went peaceably to bed, left all his cares to Heaven, and soon fell asleep.|The Brothers Grimm, Household Tales (tr. Edgar Taylor and Marian Edwardes)|A poor, honest shoemaker finds his leather made into perfect shoes each morning. He and his wife sit up and see two tiny elves at work, and repay them with little clothes.|q||A gentle Grimm tale about help that comes unbidden and gratitude that answers it. The shoemaker's clear conscience and the couple's gift of clothes, which sets the elves free to dance away, have made it a favourite Christmas story.
folkfairy|The Valiant Little Tailor|And the little tailor hastened to cut himself a girdle, stitched it, and embroidered on it in large letters: ‘Seven at one stroke!’ ‘What, the town!’ he continued, ‘the whole world shall hear of it!’ and his heart wagged with joy like a lamb’s tail.|The Brothers Grimm, Household Tales (tr. Edgar Taylor and Marian Edwardes)|A tailor swats seven flies, stitches "Seven at one stroke" on his belt, and everyone, giants included, assumes he means men. Wit and nerve carry him to a kingdom.|q||One of the Grimms' liveliest tales of a small man outwitting the mighty by bluff and quick thinking: he squeezes "water" from a cheese and "throws" a bird higher than any stone. It is a cousin of the trickster tales, with the hero's confidence as his real weapon.
folkfairy|Thumbelina|A tiny girl, born from a flower, is carried off by a toad, a beetle and a mole who want to marry her. She nurses a frozen swallow back to life, and in spring it carries her away to a land of flowers.|Hans Christian Andersen||e|1835|Andersen published Thumbelina in 1835. Its heroine is pushed from one unsuitable marriage to the next by creatures who value her only by their own measures, and finds freedom through an act of kindness to a dying bird. It is a story about being small in a world that keeps deciding for you.
folkfairy|The Steadfast Tin Soldier|A one-legged tin soldier falls in love with a paper ballerina. Swept away down a gutter, swallowed by a fish and returned home, he stands steadfast through it all.|Hans Christian Andersen||e|1838|Andersen's 1838 tale is one of his most bittersweet: the soldier never speaks his love, and the story ends in fire with the two together. Its heart is constancy: a little figure who keeps his shape and his loyalty through every misfortune.
folkfairy|The Wild Swans|A princess's eleven brothers are turned into swans by a wicked queen. To free them she must knit shirts of stinging nettles in total silence, even when she is accused of witchcraft and led to the fire.|Hans Christian Andersen||e|1838|Andersen based it on a Danish folktale also told by the Grimms as The Six Swans. Elisa's silent, painful work for her brothers is one of the great images of devotion. In the end the youngest brother keeps one swan's wing, because the last shirt wasn't finished: love's rescue is real, but not always perfect.
folkfairy|Goldilocks and the Three Bears|A girl wanders into the bears' house, tries their porridge, chairs and beds, and finds one of each "just right", until the bears come home.|Robert Southey||e|1837|The poet Robert Southey published the first printed version in 1837, in which the intruder was a nasty old woman; she became a little girl, and then Goldilocks, over the following decades. The "just right" pattern has given its name to the Goldilocks principle in science, from habitable planets to economics.
folkfairy|The Three Little Pigs|Three pigs build houses of straw, sticks and brick. The wolf huffs and puffs and blows down the first two; the brick house stands.|English folktale||e|1840s|The tale was first printed in the 1840s and became famous through Joseph Jacobs's English Fairy Tales of 1890 and Disney's 1933 cartoon. It is a fable of preparation: taking the time to build well pays when the wolf comes.
folkfairy|East of the Sun and West of the Moon|A girl marries a white bear who is a prince by night. When she breaks her promise not to look at him, he is carried off to a castle east of the sun and west of the moon, and she journeys across the world, on the winds' backs, to find him.|Norwegian folktale||e|1840s|Collected by Peter Christen Asbjørnsen and Jørgen Moe, the Norwegian Grimms, in the 1840s. Like Cupid and Psyche, it tells of a forbidden look and a long journey to undo it. The heroine's courage and persistence, asking the four winds for the way, make it one of Europe's great quest tales.
folktrick|Duck Broth|One day the Cogia saw a great many ducks playing on the top of a fountain. The Cogia, running towards them, said, 'I'll catch you'; whereupon they all rose up and took to flight. The Cogia, taking a little bread in his hand, sat down on the side of the fountain, and crumbling the bread in the fountain, fell to eating. A person coming up, said, 'What are you eating?' 'Duck broth,' replied the Cogia.|Tales of Nasreddin Hodja, The Turkish Jester (tr. George Borrow)|The ducks escape him, so Nasreddin crumbles bread into the fountain where they swam and eats it as "duck broth".|q||A favourite small Nasreddin joke: the wise fool makes the best of what he has, with a straight face. Some read it as a parable of contentment; others just enjoy the logic.
folktrick|Birbal and the Line|The emperor Akbar draws a line and asks his courtiers to make it shorter without erasing any of it. They are baffled. Birbal quietly draws a longer line beside it.|Indian folklore||e|16th century|Birbal was a real minister at the court of the Mughal emperor Akbar in the 16th century, and hundreds of tales of his wit grew up around him. The line puzzle is the best known: to change how something looks, change what it is compared with. It is a lesson in perspective, and in solving problems by stepping outside the obvious approach.
folktrick|Juan Bobo|Puerto Rico's lovable fool. Sent to market, told to dress the pig up nicely for church, or to carry water in a basket, he takes every instruction too literally, with comic results, and sometimes comes out ahead.|Puerto Rican folklore||e|traditional|Juan Bobo ("Foolish John") tales are told across Puerto Rico and the Spanish-speaking Caribbean, often to children. Like Nasreddin and Till Eulenspiegel, the fool shows up the foolishness around him: his literal-mindedness reveals how often adults say what they don't mean.
folktrick|Pedro Urdemales|A clever rogue of Spanish and Latin American folklore who outwits rich landowners, priests and even the devil. In one tale he sells a "money tree" by hanging coins on its branches.|Spanish and Latin American folklore||e|traditional|Pedro Urdemales (also Urdimalas or Malasartes in Brazil) appears in Spanish literature by the 16th century, and Cervantes wrote a play about him. Across Mexico, Chile, Guatemala and beyond, he is the poor man's champion, beating the powerful at their own game of greed.
folkcarib|Gang Gang Sara|Legend says Gang Gang Sara was an African woman who flew from Africa to Tobago on the wind, searching for her family. She married there, and when her husband died she climbed a silk cotton tree to fly home, but she had eaten salt, and could no longer fly.|Tobago folklore||e|18th century|Her story is told at Golden Lane in Tobago, where a grave said to be hers is visited to this day. In Caribbean lore, eating salt makes a person too heavy to fly back to Africa, an image tied to stories of enslaved Africans who longed to return home. She is remembered as a wise, kind woman and a symbol of the African heritage of Tobago.
folkcarib|Jumbies|Jumbie is the Caribbean word for a spirit or ghost: the restless dead, mischievous spirits of the night, or any eerie presence. Old people warned children to be home before dark, when the jumbies come out.|Caribbean folklore||e|traditional|The word is used across the English-speaking Caribbean, and many particular spirits, such as the douen or the moko jumbie, belong to the jumbie family. Jumbie beads, the red-and-black seeds of the jumbie bead vine, were worn as protection. The Trinidadian writer Tracey Baptiste drew on this lore for her children's novel The Jumbies in 2015.
folkcarib|The Silk Cotton Tree|The great silk cotton tree, the ceiba, with its towering buttressed trunk, was believed to be a home of spirits. People were wary of cutting one down or passing it at night.|Caribbean folklore||e|traditional|The ceiba is sacred in many traditions: to the Maya it was the world tree linking the underworld, earth and heavens, and in West African and Caribbean belief it is a meeting place of spirits. In Trinidad and Tobago lore it is where the soucouyant hides her skin and where jumbies gather. Many old silk cotton trees survive simply because people feared to fell them.
folkcarib|Duppies|In Jamaican belief a duppy is the spirit of a dead person that can wander at night. Old customs, such as nine nights of wake-keeping, help the spirit settle peacefully rather than linger.|Jamaican folklore||e|traditional|The word may come from a West African language. Nine Night, a gathering of song, food and stories held in the nights after a death, is still kept in many Jamaican families, both to comfort the mourners and to see the spirit off. Duppies and duppy stories are part of the island's rich storytelling tradition.
folkcarib|Ol' Higue|In Guyana, an old woman who by night slips off her skin and flies as a ball of fire to suck the blood of babies. Scatter rice at the door, they say, and she must stop and count every grain until dawn.|Guyanese folklore||e|traditional|The Ol' Higue is Guyana's cousin of Trinidad's soucouyant. Guyanese say "Ol' Higue don't like scrubbing" and other proverbs about her. The poet Grace Nichols wrote a celebrated poem, Ol' Higue, giving the creature her own voice. The rice is a recurring Caribbean detail: a compulsion to count that the dawn can catch.
folkcarib|The Moongazer|A tall giant who stands at crossroads and in the middle of the road on moonlit nights, legs apart, staring at the moon. Anyone who walks between his legs is in danger.|Guyanese and Caribbean folklore||e|traditional|The Moongazer appears in Guyanese and some Trinidadian storytelling. Like many night spirits of the region, he is tied to the road at night, when walking home alone was dangerous, and the advice is always the same: walk around, never between.
folkworld|Dick Whittington and His Cat|A poor boy comes to London believing its streets are paved with gold. Ready to give up, he hears the bells call him back: "Turn again, Whittington, thrice Lord Mayor of London." His cat, sent on a trading voyage, makes his fortune.|English folktale||e|1600s|Richard Whittington was real: a merchant who was Lord Mayor of London four times and died in 1423, leaving his fortune to charity. The legend of the poor boy and his cat grew up in the 17th century and became a classic Christmas pantomime. It is a tale of persevering when about to give up.
folkworld|Robin Hood|An outlaw of Sherwood Forest who robs the rich to give to the poor, with Little John, Friar Tuck and Maid Marian, defying the Sheriff of Nottingham.|English legend||e|14th century|Robin Hood ballads were known by the late 14th century; whether a real Robin lay behind them is unknown. Over centuries he became a nobleman, a champion of the poor and a symbol of resistance to unjust authority. Each age has retold him for its own idea of justice.
folkworld|William Tell|The Swiss hero who refused to bow to a tyrant's hat on a pole. Forced to shoot an apple from his son's head with his crossbow, he succeeded, and later struck down the tyrant.|Swiss legend||e|14th century|The legend is set in 1307, at the founding of the Swiss Confederation, and first appears in writing in the 15th century; historians doubt there was a real Tell, and similar archer stories exist in Norse and English lore. Friedrich Schiller's 1804 play and Rossini's 1829 opera made Tell a symbol of freedom against tyranny.
folkworld|The Snow Maiden|A childless old couple build a girl of snow, and she comes to life. She is gentle and loved, but when spring comes and she leaps over a fire with other girls, she melts into a cloud of mist.|Russian folktale||e|19th century|Snegurochka was collected by Alexander Afanasyev and became the subject of Rimsky-Korsakov's opera of 1882. In Russian New Year celebrations she accompanies Grandfather Frost. The tale is a tender picture of a love that can't last, and of beauty belonging to its season.
folkworld|The Stonecutter|A stonecutter envies a rich man and is magically made rich; then he envies a prince, then the sun, a cloud and a mountain, becoming each in turn. Finally he feels a stonecutter chipping at the mountain, and wishes to be himself again.|Japanese and Chinese folktale||e|traditional|The tale is told in Japan and China and was popularised in the West by Andrew Lang's fairy books. Each thing he becomes is overpowered by another, until the circle brings him back where he started, with new contentment. It is a gentle teaching about envy, and the strength in one's own place.
folkworld|The Boy Who Drew Cats|A boy who can't stop drawing cats is sent away from the temple. Sheltering in a haunted temple, he covers its screens with cats, and in the night they come to life and kill the giant rat that haunted it.|Japanese folktale||e|1898|Lafcadio Hearn retold it in English in 1898. The boy's "useless" passion, which got him sent away, turns out to be what saves him. It has become a favourite story for young artists: follow what you love to make, even when others see no use in it.
ideahow|The Rice Cooker|A rice cooker heats the pot until the water is gone. While there is water, the temperature can't rise much above boiling; once it has all been absorbed, the temperature jumps, and a sensor switches the cooker to keep warm.|Toshiba||e|1955|Toshiba sold the first successful automatic electric rice cooker in Japan in 1955, and it transformed home cooking across Asia. Its trick is physics: water can't get hotter than about 100 °C at normal pressure, so the pot stays near boiling as long as any free water remains. When the rice has soaked up the last of it, the bottom of the pot heats past that point, and a thermostat, originally a magnet that loses its magnetism at a set temperature, clicks the switch. Modern "fuzzy logic" cookers adjust the heating for different grains.
ideahow|Non-Stick Pans|Non-stick pans are coated with PTFE, a plastic so slippery that almost nothing can grip it. Its carbon chains are sheathed in fluorine atoms, which hardly attract anything at all.|Roy Plunkett and Marc Grégoire||e|1938|Roy Plunkett, a chemist at DuPont, discovered PTFE by accident in 1938 when a cylinder of refrigerant gas seemed empty but still weighed full; inside was a waxy white solid. DuPont named it Teflon. In 1954 the French engineer Marc Grégoire, encouraged by his wife Colette, coated a frying pan with it, and they founded Tefal. The coating is safe at normal cooking temperatures but breaks down if an empty pan is heated very hot, so pans shouldn't be preheated dry on high heat. Metal utensils scratch it; wooden or silicone ones keep it longer.
ideahow|The Washing Machine|A washing machine combines water, detergent and movement: tumbling or agitating loosens dirt, detergent lifts it away, and a fast spin flings water out through the drum's holes, so clothes come out nearly dry.|Hurley Machine Company||e|1908|Hand-cranked washers existed in the 19th century; the Hurley Machine Company of Chicago sold one of the first electric washing machines, the Thor, in 1908. Few inventions freed more time, mostly women's. Front-loaders lift clothes and drop them, using gravity and less water; top-loaders often use an agitator. The spin cycle, which can reach over 1,000 revolutions a minute, works by centrifugal effect: the drum pushes the clothes in a circle, but the water passes through the holes and escapes. Washing at lower temperatures with modern detergents saves a large share of the energy.
ideahow|Circuit Breakers|A circuit breaker is a switch that turns itself off when too much current flows. A heated metal strip trips it during a slow overload, and an electromagnet trips it instantly in a short circuit, before wires can overheat and start a fire.|Hugo Stotz||e|1924|Early homes used fuses: a thin wire that melts if the current is too high, which must then be replaced. The German engineer Hugo Stotz patented a compact miniature circuit breaker in 1924 that could simply be switched back on. Too much current heats wires, and in walls that can start fires. A breaker has two ways to trip: a bimetallic strip that bends when heated by a sustained overload, and a coil that pulls the switch open at once if a short circuit makes the current surge. A separate safety device, the residual current device or GFCI, protects people by cutting the power if any current leaks to earth, as through a person.
ideahow|Mobile Phone Calls|Your phone talks by radio to the nearest tower. The land is divided into cells, each with its own tower; as you move, your call is handed from one to the next without dropping. Your voice travels as digital data.|Martin Cooper||e|1973|On 3 April 1973 Martin Cooper of Motorola made the first handheld mobile phone call, on a New York street, to a rival at Bell Labs. The cellular idea, worked out at Bell Labs, lets the same radio frequencies be reused in cells that aren't next to each other, so millions of people can call at once. Each tower connects to the wider network by cable. Your phone measures signal strength constantly, and the network switches you to a better tower as you move. Phones adjust their transmitting power, using less when the tower is close, which also saves battery.
ideahow|Email|An email travels from your device to your mail server, which looks up the recipient's server from the part after the @ and passes the message on. It waits there until their device collects it.|Ray Tomlinson||e|1971|In 1971 Ray Tomlinson, an engineer working on the ARPANET, sent the first email between two computers sitting side by side, and chose the @ sign to separate the user's name from the machine's. He later said he couldn't remember what the first message said, probably something like QWERTYUIOP. Email still runs on standards from the early 1980s, which is one reason spam and fake senders are hard to stamp out: checks added later, like SPF and DKIM, help servers verify that a message really came from the domain it claims.
ideahow|Encryption|When you see the padlock in your browser, your device and the website have agreed a secret key without ever sending it, using mathematics that is easy one way and practically impossible to reverse. Everything after that is scrambled.|Whitfield Diffie and Martin Hellman||e|1976|For thousands of years secret messages needed a shared secret key delivered in advance. In 1976 Whitfield Diffie and Martin Hellman published public-key cryptography: two parties can agree a secret over a public channel, because some mathematical operations, like multiplying large primes or certain exponentiations, are quick to do and enormously hard to undo. RSA followed in 1977. (British codebreakers at GCHQ had found the same ideas secretly a few years earlier.) Today it protects banking, messaging and shopping. Quantum computers could one day break some of these methods, so new "post-quantum" schemes are being rolled out.
ideahow|Search Engines|A search engine sends out programs that crawl the web, copying pages and following links. It builds an index, like the one at the back of a book, and ranks results by relevance and by signals such as how many trusted pages link to them.|Larry Page and Sergey Brin||e|1998|Early search engines ranked pages mostly by matching words, which was easy to game. In 1998 Larry Page and Sergey Brin, PhD students at Stanford, launched Google with PageRank, which treated a link as a vote and weighted votes from important pages more heavily. Modern search uses hundreds of signals and machine learning, considering where you are, how fresh a page is and what the words likely mean. Because results are ranked, the first few get most attention, which is why whole industries work to appear there, and why it's worth scrolling a little further.
ideahow|Traffic Lights|Traffic lights share a junction in turns. Many run on fixed timers; others sense waiting cars with loops of wire in the road or cameras, and adjust. The amber between green and red gives drivers time to stop safely.|J. P. Knight||e|1868|The first traffic signal, designed by the railway engineer J. P. Knight, was installed outside the Houses of Parliament in London in 1868: semaphore arms by day, gas-lit red and green by night. It exploded a few weeks later, injuring its policeman operator. Electric signals appeared in Cleveland, Ohio, in 1914. Garrett Morgan, an African American inventor, patented a three-position signal in 1923 with a warning stage before stop. Today many junctions detect cars with inductive loops cut into the road surface: the metal of a car changes the loop's magnetic field.
ideahow|Bridges|Every bridge moves its load to the ground through compression (pushing) and tension (pulling). Arches turn weight into compression along their curve; suspension bridges hang the deck from cables in tension, anchored in the ground at each end.|Thomas Telford||e|1826|Stone is strong in compression but weak in tension, which is why the Romans built arches: every stone is squeezed against its neighbours. Steel and wire are strong in tension, making long suspension spans possible. Thomas Telford's Menai Suspension Bridge in Wales, opened in 1826, was the longest of its day. Engineers also learned the hard way about wind and resonance: the Tacoma Narrows Bridge in the USA twisted itself apart in a moderate wind in 1940, and bridges have been designed with aerodynamics in mind ever since.
ideahow|How Wounds Heal|A wound heals in overlapping stages: blood clots to seal it, white cells clear germs and debris, new tissue and blood vessels fill the gap, and over months the scar slowly remodels and strengthens.|Joseph Lister||e|1867|Before antiseptics, a large share of surgical patients died of infection. In 1867 Joseph Lister, inspired by Pasteur's germ theory, began cleaning wounds and instruments with carbolic acid, and deaths fell sharply. The body's own process starts within seconds: platelets and fibrin form a clot. Inflammation follows, the redness and warmth of immune cells arriving. Then cells called fibroblasts lay down collagen, new capillaries grow, and skin closes over. A scar is never quite as strong as the original skin, reaching about 80 per cent of its strength. Keeping a wound clean and covered usually helps it heal faster.
ideahow|Blood Types|Red blood cells carry markers called antigens. Type A has A markers, B has B, AB has both, O has neither. Your immune system attacks blood with markers it doesn't have, which is why transfusions must be matched.|Karl Landsteiner||e|1901|Early blood transfusions often killed patients, and no one knew why. In 1901 the Austrian scientist Karl Landsteiner discovered the ABO groups by mixing blood samples, and won the 1930 Nobel Prize. He and Alexander Wiener later found the Rhesus factor, the plus or minus. Type O negative carries none of the main markers, so it can be given to almost anyone in an emergency: the universal donor. AB positive people can receive any type. Blood types are inherited, and their frequencies vary around the world.
ideahow|The Heart|Your heart is two pumps side by side. The right side sends blood to the lungs to pick up oxygen; the left side pumps it round the body. Valves act like one-way doors, and their closing makes the "lub-dub" sound.|William Harvey||e|1628|For 1,400 years doctors followed Galen, who thought blood was made in the liver and consumed by the body. In 1628 the English physician William Harvey showed that the heart pumps the same blood round and round in a circuit: he calculated that it pumped far more blood in an hour than the body could possibly make. Each heartbeat is triggered by a natural pacemaker, a cluster of cells in the right atrium, that sends an electrical wave across the heart. A resting heart beats about 60 to 100 times a minute, roughly 100,000 times a day.
ideahow|Hurricanes|A hurricane is a heat engine fuelled by warm ocean water. Warm, moist air rises and condenses, releasing heat that drives stronger winds, while the Earth's spin twists the storm into a vast rotating spiral around a calm eye.|William Redfield||e|1831|In 1831 the American William Redfield, studying the pattern of fallen trees after a storm in Connecticut, showed that hurricanes are giant whirlwinds rotating around a centre. They need sea water of at least about 26.5 °C, which is why the Atlantic season runs from June to November, peaking in late summer. The Coriolis effect makes them spin anticlockwise in the northern hemisphere, and it is too weak near the equator, so hurricanes rarely form within about five degrees of it, which helps keep Trinidad south of the main hurricane belt, though not entirely safe. Over land, cut off from warm water, they weaken.
ideahow|Eclipses|In a solar eclipse the Moon passes between the Sun and the Earth, casting its shadow on us; in a lunar eclipse the Earth's shadow falls on the Moon. They don't happen every month because the Moon's orbit is tilted.|Thales of Miletus||e|585 BC|Herodotus wrote that the Greek philosopher Thales predicted the eclipse of 585 BC, which halted a battle between the Lydians and Medes, who made peace. By a wonderful coincidence the Sun is about 400 times wider than the Moon and about 400 times further away, so they appear almost exactly the same size, and the Moon can just cover the Sun, revealing its glowing corona. The Moon's orbit is tilted about five degrees, so usually its shadow misses the Earth. During a total lunar eclipse the Moon turns red, lit by sunlight bent through the Earth's atmosphere, the light of every sunrise and sunset at once.
ideahow|Hydroelectric Power|A dam stores water high up. Released through pipes, it falls and spins turbines connected to generators. Water's weight falling through height becomes electricity, with no fuel burned.|William Armstrong||e|1878|Water wheels have powered mills for two thousand years. In 1878 the industrialist William Armstrong lit his house, Cragside in Northumberland, with electricity from a water turbine on his estate, the first house in the world lit by hydroelectric power. The energy depends on how much water falls and how far, so dams are built in deep valleys. Hydropower supplies about a sixth of the world's electricity, more than any other low-carbon source, and some schemes pump water back uphill when power is cheap, storing energy like a giant battery. Dams also flood land and change rivers, so their costs are weighed carefully.
ideaphil|The Morning Rehearsal|Begin the morning by saying to thyself, I shall meet with the busybody, the ungrateful, arrogant, deceitful, envious, unsocial. All these things happen to them by reason of their ignorance of what is good and evil. But I who have seen the nature of the good that it is beautiful, and of the bad that it is ugly, and the nature of him who does wrong, that it is akin to me; not only of the same blood or seed, but that it participates in the same intelligence and the same portion of the divinity, I can neither be injured by any of them, for no one can fix on me what is ugly, nor can I be angry with my kinsman, nor hate him. For we are made for co-operation, like feet, like hands, like eyelids, like the rows of the upper and lower teeth. To act against one another, then, is contrary to nature; and it is acting against one another to be vexed and to turn away.|Marcus Aurelius, Meditations (tr. George Long)|Expect difficult people before the day begins, and remember they are your kin. Then their faults can neither harm you nor make you hate them.|q|2nd century|Book II of the Meditations opens with Marcus preparing himself, before breakfast, for the people he will meet: the meddling, the ungrateful, the arrogant. The Stoics called this kind of exercise premeditation: imagining trouble in advance so that it does not ambush you. But the point is not cynicism. Marcus reminds himself that wrongdoers act from ignorance, that they share his nature and his reason, and that we are made to work together like hands and feet. Anger, on this view, is a kind of self-harm, and patience a kind of clear sight.
ideaphil|The Inner Retreat|Men seek retreats for themselves, houses in the country, sea-shores, and mountains; and thou too art wont to desire such things very much. But this is altogether a mark of the most common sort of men, for it is in thy power whenever thou shalt choose to retire into thyself. For nowhere either with more quiet or more freedom from trouble does a man retire than into his own soul, particularly when he has within him such thoughts that by looking into them he is immediately in perfect tranquillity; and I affirm that tranquillity is nothing else than the good ordering of the mind.|Marcus Aurelius, Meditations (tr. George Long)|You need no country house to find peace. You can withdraw at any moment into your own mind, if it is well ordered.|q|2nd century|Wealthy Romans escaped to villas by the sea; Marcus, who could have had any retreat he liked, wrote that the best one is always available and free: the mind itself. The idea, sometimes called the inner citadel, is that tranquillity is not a place but an order of thoughts. If we keep a few short, steadying principles ready, a moment of reflection can restore us anywhere, in a camp, a court or a crowded day. It anticipates modern practices of mindfulness and the brief, deliberate pause.
ideaphil|Take Away the Opinion|Take away thy opinion, and then there is taken away the complaint, "I have been harmed." Take away the complaint, "I have been harmed," and the harm is taken away.|Marcus Aurelius, Meditations (tr. George Long)|What hurts is often not the event but our judgement that we have been harmed. Drop the judgement and much of the hurt goes with it.|q|2nd century|This is the core Stoic claim, which Marcus learned from Epictetus: we are disturbed not by things but by our opinions about them. An insult is sounds; a loss is a change; the sense of injury is something we add. Marcus does not deny pain, but he notices how much of our suffering is a story told about it. Twentieth-century psychologists such as Albert Ellis and Aaron Beck built cognitive therapy on much the same insight: change the interpretation and the feeling changes too.
ideaphil|The River of Time|Time is like a river made up of the events which happen, and a violent stream; for as soon as a thing has been seen, it is carried away, and another comes in its place, and this will be carried away too.|Marcus Aurelius, Meditations (tr. George Long)|Events pour past like a fast river; each thing is swept away as soon as it is seen. Hold things lightly.|q|2nd century|Marcus borrows an old image, reaching back to Heraclitus, who said you cannot step into the same river twice. Everything we notice is already on its way out of sight, replaced by the next thing, which will go too. For Marcus this is not a cause for despair but a reason not to cling, not to be crushed by setbacks, and not to be inflated by success. Fame, grievance and fortune are all in the current.
ideaphil|The Swarm and the Bee|That which is not good for the swarm, neither is it good for the bee.|Marcus Aurelius, Meditations (tr. George Long)|What harms the community cannot truly be good for the individual. We are parts of a larger whole.|q|2nd century|In a single line Marcus sums up the Stoic view of society. Human beings, they held, are social by nature, as bees are; our reason is shared, and our good is bound up with the good of others. An advantage won at the community's expense is, on this view, no real advantage. The idea runs through his notes on duty and justice, and it echoes in later thought about the common good, from Christian ethics to modern ideas of public health.
ideaphil|Not Ten Thousand Years|Do not act as if thou wert going to live ten thousand years. Death hangs over thee. While thou livest, while it is in thy power, be good.|Marcus Aurelius, Meditations (tr. George Long)|You will not live forever, so do not put off goodness. Be good now, while you can.|q|2nd century|Marcus returns again and again to death, not to frighten himself but to wake himself up. This line is the Stoic version of memento mori, remember you must die. Because time is short, procrastinating on the important things, kindness, honesty, the work you were made for, is the real waste. The thought sharpens the present: the only life we can lose is the one we are living now.
ideaphil|The Best Revenge|The best way of avenging thyself is not to become like the wrong-doer.|Marcus Aurelius, Meditations (tr. George Long)|When someone wrongs you, the best answer is not to become like them.|q|2nd century|Among the Meditations' shortest entries, this is one of the most quoted. Revenge, Marcus suggests, lets the wrongdoer win twice: once by the injury and again by turning us into someone who injures. Refusing to imitate them keeps our character, which for a Stoic is the only thing that truly matters. The idea has parallels in the Buddha, in Jesus' teaching on turning the other cheek, and in modern thinking about breaking cycles of harm.
ideaphil|No Other Life|Though thou shouldest be going to live three thousand years and as many times ten thousand years, still remember that no man loses any other life than this which he now lives, nor lives any other than this which he now loses.|Marcus Aurelius, Meditations (tr. George Long)|However long we live, the only life we ever have or lose is the present moment.|q|2nd century|Marcus offers a consolation and a challenge at once. The person who dies young and the person who dies old lose the same thing: the present, since the past is gone and the future not yet theirs. So length of life matters less than we think, and attention to this moment matters more. Later thinkers, from Montaigne to the teachers of meditation, return to the same point: life is only ever lived now.
ideaeast|The Use of Emptiness|The thirty spokes unite in the one nave; but it is on the empty space (for the axle), that the use of the wheel depends.|Lao Tzu, Tao Te Ching (tr. James Legge)|A wheel turns on the empty hub, a bowl holds because it is hollow. What is not there makes things useful.|q||Chapter 11 of the Tao Te Ching gives three images: the wheel's hub, the clay vessel and the windows and doors of a room. In each, it is the empty space that gives the thing its use. The lesson is typical of the Tao Te Ching, which prizes what is quiet, open and receptive over what is full and assertive. Readers have applied it to design, to music (the rests between notes), to conversation and to a crowded schedule: room to breathe is part of what makes a life work.
ideaeast|The Sufficiency of Contentment|There is no guilt greater than to sanction ambition; no calamity greater than to be discontented with one's lot; no fault greater than the wish to be getting. Therefore the sufficiency of contentment is an enduring and unchanging sufficiency.|Lao Tzu, Tao Te Ching (tr. James Legge)|Wanting more is the root of trouble; knowing when you have enough is a wealth that lasts.|q||Chapter 46 contrasts a world at peace, where horses pull dung carts on the farms, with a world at war, where war horses breed on the borders, and traces the difference to greed. The person who knows that enough is enough always has enough. The idea of sufficiency runs through Daoist and Buddhist thought, and it returns today in discussions of consumerism, burnout and the 'hedonic treadmill', the way each gain quickly becomes the new normal.
ideaeast|Knowing Yourself|He who knows other men is discerning; he who knows himself is intelligent. He who overcomes others is strong; he who overcomes himself is mighty. He who is satisfied with his lot is rich; he who goes on acting with energy has a (firm) will.|Lao Tzu, Tao Te Ching (tr. James Legge)|Understanding others is cleverness; understanding yourself is wisdom. Mastering others is strength; mastering yourself is true power.|q||Chapter 33 sets outward achievements beside inward ones and prefers the inward each time. Knowing people is useful, but self-knowledge is deeper; overcoming rivals is impressive, but overcoming one's own impulses is harder and rarer. The same thought appears in the Greek command 'Know thyself', in the Buddha's teaching and in the Stoics, and it makes this one of the most quoted chapters of the book.
ideaeast|The Lowly Sea|That whereby the rivers and seas are able to receive the homage and tribute of all the valley streams, is their skill in being lower than they;--it is thus that they are the kings of them all. So it is that the sage (ruler), wishing to be above men, puts himself by his words below them, and, wishing to be before them, places his person behind them.|Lao Tzu, Tao Te Ching (tr. James Legge)|The sea rules all rivers because it lies below them. The wise leader leads by placing themselves last.|q||Chapter 66 uses water again: every valley stream runs into the sea because the sea is lowest. The Tao Te Ching applies the image to leadership. A ruler who puts himself below the people, speaks humbly and stands behind them is not resented, and the world is glad to follow. Two and a half thousand years later, management writers rediscovered the idea as 'servant leadership'.
ideaeast|Plain Simplicity|But simple views, and courses plain and true Would selfish ends and many lusts eschew.|Lao Tzu, Tao Te Ching (tr. James Legge)|Simple views and plain, true ways of living free us from selfish ends and many desires.|q||Chapter 19 urges a return to the 'uncarved block', the natural simplicity of a person before ambition and cleverness have shaped them into a tool. The Tao Te Ching distrusts elaborate rules and showy wisdom, which it sees as signs that the natural way has been lost. Its remedy is to want less and to be what you are. The idea fed Daoist hermits and Zen gardeners, and it speaks to anyone looking for a simpler life.
ideaphil|Spaces in Your Togetherness|But let there be spaces in your togetherness, And let the winds of the heavens dance between you.|Kahlil Gibran, The Prophet|Love should not bind two people into one. Keep room between you, as the pillars of a temple stand apart.|q|1923|In 'On Marriage', Almustafa tells the lovers to love one another but not to make a bond of love: to fill each other's cup but not drink from one cup, to stand together yet not too near, 'For the pillars of the temple stand apart, And the oak tree and the cypress grow not in each other’s shadow'. It is a vision of closeness that protects each person's freedom. The passage is read at countless weddings, perhaps because it says something vows usually leave out.
ideaphil|Your Children Are Not Your Children|Your children are not your children. They are the sons and daughters of Life’s longing for itself. They come through you but not from you, And though they are with you yet they belong not to you.|Kahlil Gibran, The Prophet|Children come through their parents, not from them. We can give them love, but not our thoughts.|q|1923|In 'On Children', a woman holding a baby asks Almustafa to speak, and he answers that children are 'the sons and daughters of Life’s longing for itself'. Parents are the bows from which living arrows are sent forth; they can give their love, house the children's bodies and strive to be like them, but they cannot own their souls, which 'dwell in the house of tomorrow'. It is one of the most beloved passages of The Prophet, and a gentle rebuke to the wish to shape a child into a copy of oneself.
ideaphil|Work Is Love Made Visible|Work is love made visible. And if you cannot work with love but only with distaste, it is better that you should leave your work and sit at the gate of the temple and take alms of those who work with joy.|Kahlil Gibran, The Prophet|Work done with care is a form of love. Work done grudgingly poisons what it makes.|q|1923|A ploughman asks about work, and Almustafa answers that labour is not a curse but a way of keeping pace with the earth. To work with love is to weave cloth as if your beloved would wear it, to build a house as if your beloved would live in it. If you cannot work with love, he says, it is better to sit at the gate of the temple and take alms. The line has become a motto for craftspeople, carers and anyone who thinks the spirit of work matters as much as its product.
ideaphil|Joy and Sorrow Are Inseparable|Your joy is your sorrow unmasked. And the selfsame well from which your laughter rises was oftentimes filled with your tears. And how else can it be? The deeper that sorrow carves into your being, the more joy you can contain.|Kahlil Gibran, The Prophet|Joy and sorrow come from the same deep place. The more grief has hollowed us, the more joy we can hold.|q|1923|In 'On Joy and Sorrow', a woman asks about the two, and Almustafa says they are inseparable: 'Together they come, and when one sits alone with you at your board, remember that the other is asleep upon your bed.' The cup that holds wine was burned in the potter's oven; the lute that soothes was hollowed with knives. It is a consolation for grief and a caution for good times, and a view shared by many traditions that see suffering as what deepens a person.
