ideaphil|Pain Breaks the Shell|Your pain is the breaking of the shell that encloses your understanding.|Kahlil Gibran, The Prophet|Pain can be the cracking open of what has kept us small, so that understanding can come through.|q|1923|In 'On Pain', Almustafa compares suffering to the stone of a fruit that must break so its heart can stand in the sun. He does not praise pain or ask for it, but suggests accepting the seasons of the heart as we accept the seasons over the fields, and trusting the 'bitter potion' of the physician within. The image of growth through breaking appears in many places, from the seed that dies to become wheat to modern research on post-traumatic growth.
ideaphil|Giving of Yourself|You give but little when you give of your possessions. It is when you give of yourself that you truly give.|Kahlil Gibran, The Prophet|The gifts that count are not things you own but your time, your care and yourself.|q|1923|A rich man asks Almustafa about giving, and he answers that possessions are only things we keep and guard for fear of needing them tomorrow. True giving is of oneself, and the best givers give as the myrtle breathes its fragrance into space, without seeking joy or virtue in it. He adds a sharp warning against making people bare their need and pride before they are helped. The passage questions both stinginess and the showy kind of charity.
ideaeast|Where the Mind Is Without Fear|Where the mind is without fear and the head is held high; Where knowledge is free; Where the world has not been broken up into fragments by narrow domestic walls; Where words come out from the depth of truth; Where tireless striving stretches its arms towards perfection; Where the clear stream of reason has not lost its way into the dreary desert sand of dead habit; Where the mind is led forward by thee into ever-widening thought and action— Into that heaven of freedom, my Father, let my country awake.|Rabindranath Tagore, Gitanjali|A prayer for a country, and a person, free of fear, narrow walls and dead habit, moving toward truth and wider thought.|q|1912|Poem 35 of Gitanjali was written as a prayer for India under British rule, but it asks for more than political freedom: a place where knowledge is free, where the world is not broken up by 'narrow domestic walls', where reason has not lost its way in 'the dreary desert sand of dead habit'. It is recited in Indian schools and quoted by leaders around the world, and it reads as a description of any healthy community, or any open mind.
ideaeast|God in the Dust|He is there where the tiller is tilling the hard ground and where the pathmaker is breaking stones. He is with them in sun and in shower, and his garment is covered with dust.|Rabindranath Tagore, Gitanjali|The sacred is not hidden in the temple's dark corners but out with the people who toil in the fields and on the roads.|q|1912|In poem 11, Tagore tells a worshipper to leave off chanting and telling beads in a lonely corner of the temple: the God he seeks is not there. He is with the farmer and the road-mender, in sun and in rain, his garment dusty. The poem joins a long tradition, from the Bhakti poets of India to Christian thinkers and the Sufis, which finds the divine in ordinary work and humble people rather than in ritual set apart from life.
ideaeast|Freedom in a Thousand Bonds|Deliverance is not for me in renunciation. I feel the embrace of freedom in a thousand bonds of delight.|Rabindranath Tagore, Gitanjali|Freedom does not come from giving the world up, but from loving it fully.|q|1912|Indian religious tradition often honoured the renunciant, the holy person who leaves family and possessions behind to seek liberation. In poem 73 Tagore respectfully disagrees: he will not shut the doors of his senses. His freedom will come through the delights of sight, hearing and touch, through love and work, burning in all of them like a flame. It is a world-affirming spirituality, close to the joy in the senses of poets like Whitman, whom Tagore admired.
ideaeast|The Stream of Life|The same stream of life that runs through my veins night and day runs through the world and dances in rhythmic measures.|Rabindranath Tagore, Gitanjali|The life that pulses in our veins is the same life that moves through the whole world.|q|1912|Poem 69 imagines one stream of life running through the poet's body and through the world: shooting up through the dust as grass, breaking into leaves and flowers, rocked in the ocean of birth and death. The poem expresses an ancient Indian idea, that the self and the universe share one life, in terms anyone can feel. Modern readers sometimes find in it an early ecological vision of our kinship with all living things.
ideaeast|The Song Unsung|The song that I came to sing remains unsung to this day.|Rabindranath Tagore, Gitanjali|We can spend our lives preparing, tuning and waiting, and still feel the one thing we came for has not yet been done.|q|1912|Poem 13 begins with this confession: the singer has spent his days 'stringing and in unstringing' his instrument, and the time has not come right; the words have not been rightly set. Only the longing remains. Many readers recognise the feeling of a life spent getting ready. The poem does not scold; it sits with the ache, and listens for the footsteps of the one who has not yet come.
ideaeast|Death as a Guest|On the day when death will knock at thy door what wilt thou offer to him? Oh, I will set before my guest the full vessel of my life—I will never let him go with empty hands.|Rabindranath Tagore, Gitanjali|When death comes, meet it like an honoured guest, and offer it the full harvest of your life.|q|1912|In poem 90 Tagore imagines death knocking at the door and decides how to receive it: not with fear, but as a host welcoming a guest, setting before him all the sweet vintage of autumn days and summer nights. Death becomes a moment of giving rather than taking away. The later poems of Gitanjali return to this calm, even eager, view of death, which drew many readers in the years of the First World War.
ideaart|Love Many Things|But I always think, that the best way to know God is to love many things. Love a friend, a wife, something, whatever you like, you will be in the right way to know more about it, that is what I say to myself.|Vincent van Gogh, Letters to His Brother (tr. Jo van Gogh-Bonger)|The way to understand the world, and what is holy in it, is to love it: a friend, a person, a thing, whatever you like.|q|1880|Vincent wrote this to Theo during his most difficult years, after he had failed as a preacher among the miners of the Borinage. Love, he insists, is a way of knowing: give yourself seriously to something and it will teach you. The thought fits his whole art. He painted sunflowers, worn boots, peasants and night skies with the attention of someone who loved them, and in loving them he showed them to us.
ideaart|The Fire in the Soul|There may be a great fire in our soul, and no one ever comes to warm himself at it, and the passers by see only a little bit of smoke coming through the chimney, and pass on their way.|Vincent van Gogh, Letters to His Brother (tr. Jo van Gogh-Bonger)|Others may see only smoke from the chimney, yet a great fire burns inside. Tend it and wait.|q|1880|Written in the same year, when Vincent felt useless and misunderstood, this image became one of his most loved. The fire is his passion, longing and love; the passers-by see only 'a little bit of smoke'. He resolves to tend the inner fire patiently until someone comes to sit by it. Within a few years the fire found its way onto canvas, though few people warmed themselves at it in his lifetime.
ideaart|A Series of Small Things|For the great things are not done only by impulse, but are a series of small things brought together.|Vincent van Gogh, Letters to His Brother (tr. Jo van Gogh-Bonger)|Great works do not come from inspiration alone; they are built from many small things, patiently brought together.|q|1882|Van Gogh is often pictured as a wild genius painting in a frenzy. His letters tell another story: of a man who taught himself to draw, copying exercises over and over, working out colour theories, making study after study. In this line he describes the method behind the miracle. It is the same lesson writers, athletes and scientists keep rediscovering: persistence in small steps is what makes great things possible.
ideaart|Experience Never Errs|Experience never errs; it is only your judgments that err by promising themselves effects such as are not caused by your experiments.|Leonardo da Vinci, Notebooks (tr. J. P. Richter)|Nature does not make mistakes; we do, when we expect from an experiment what it cannot give.|q|c. 1500|Leonardo had little formal schooling and no Latin, and scholars sometimes mocked him for it. He answered that he learned from experience, which never errs. When results surprise us, the fault lies in our predictions, not in what we observe. A century before Galileo and Francis Bacon, his notebooks show the habit of mind that would become the scientific method: look carefully, test, and let observation correct theory.
ideaart|Experience, the True Mistress|Many will think they may reasonably blame me by alleging that my proofs are opposed to the authority of certain men held in the highest reverence by their inexperienced judgments; not considering that my works are the issue of pure and simple experience, who is the one true mistress.|Leonardo da Vinci, Notebooks (tr. J. P. Richter)|Against those who quoted ancient authorities, Leonardo appealed to direct experience as the only true teacher.|q|c. 1500|In Leonardo's time, learning meant reading Aristotle, Galen and the other ancients and commenting on them. Leonardo, self-taught, turned instead to dissecting bodies, watching water and studying how birds fly. Here he defends that choice: his critics lean on the authority of men they revere, while his own works come from 'pure and simple experience'. It is a declaration of independence for the experimental mind.
ideaart|A Ship Without a Rudder|Those who are in love with practice without knowledge are like the sailor who gets into a ship without rudder or compass and who never can be certain whether he is going.|Leonardo da Vinci, Notebooks (tr. J. P. Richter)|Skill without understanding drifts like a ship without a rudder. Practice must rest on sound theory.|q|c. 1500|Leonardo believed painting was a science. A painter who copies by habit, without understanding perspective, light and anatomy, is like a sailor without rudder or compass, never sure where he is going. He spent years studying optics and the body so that his art would rest on knowledge. The same idea, that practice and theory need each other, is taught today in medicine, engineering and every craft.
